I.

# Society.

### A thought as the morning dawned.

You! — Artist of your own torments,
poor mortal! — your blessedness
hangs upon the chains of love,
which form the society of men.
Ah! Wretched man! why do you seek
to tear these chains asunder,
and, out of the stubbornness
of your unjust soul, accuse them
of being a burden to humanity?
Ah! how pitiable is the error
that deceives you!
Know, wretched man! — you are rushing
toward your own ruin! —
Do you then misjudge the power of these bonds?
Through them, continents are bound,
and climatic zones <note>Original: "Himmelsstriche." In the 18th century, this term referred to specific regions of the earth categorized by their climate or latitude—literally "strokes of the heavens."</note> are united. —
In vain does the immense partition
of the sea separate mankind;
nothing more exists upon the globe of the earth
that the chain of society does not link.
The whole world is now but one single land,

<sig>A</sig>

<vocab>Society, humanity, chains of love, continents, climatic zones, partition</vocab>

<summary>This page opens the main text with a poetic reflection on the necessity of social bonds, arguing that human connection is a source of salvation rather than a burden. It portrays society as a global force that unites different continents and overcomes geographical barriers like the sea.</summary>
<keywords>society, humanity, social bonds, globalism, Enlightenment philosophy, Johann Gottlob Marezoll, morality</keywords>

<note>Codrus was a legendary King of Athens who, according to myth, sacrificed his life to ensure his city's victory, embodying the ultimate selfless patriot.</note>

<note>A reference to the Great Lisbon Earthquake of 1755, which killed tens of thousands. The author uses it here as a symbol of mass death and catastrophe.</note>

O Nature! How you are desecrated!!
How your power is misunderstood!
When will your bright call
lead man back to his duties,
when will one read, marked with fire,
human duty in the heart,
written by the hand of love? —

to action for the welfare of brothers!

and the pleasure of being good! —

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 6: After a philosophical reflection on the duty of rulers and the communal nature of human existence, the text transitions into a narrative illustration of these principles through the character of Adelaide, an exemplar of virtue.</meta>

# The Deity always rewards virtue.

Adelaide was born of good parents. These noble people spared no effort to watch over the well-being of Adelaide's days; to raise her well, to form her into a noble and sensitive <note>German: *empfindsam*; a key 18th-century term referring to "sensibility," the capacity for deep feeling and moral sympathy.</note> soul, was their first endeavor.

Scarcely fifteen springs had passed for Adelaide when her beauty unfolded like a rose: already, young men looked upon her with envy, and everyone wished for Adelaide.

The girl's stature was slender, there was majesty in her posture, her eye was gentleness itself, pleasure smiled from her gaze, her company was cheerful, and her modesty <note>German: *Eingezogenheit*; literally "drawn-in-ness," referring to a reserved or retired character that was a highly prized feminine virtue in the 18th century.</note> (which is so little characteristic of the girls of our time) enhanced the charms of her beauty.

There was no young man in the region where she lived who did not wish for Adelaide. As butterflies flutter around young roses on a summer morning, so was Adelaide surrounded by admirers.

<meta>catchword: One</meta>

<term>Adelaide</term> (the protagonist’s name)
<term>Tugend</term> (virtue)
<term>Gottheit</term> (the Deity or God)
<term>Erziehung</term> (upbringing/education)
<term>Schönheit</term> (beauty)
<term>Mägdchen</term> (girl or maiden; archaic spelling)
<term>Eingezogenheit</term> (modesty or reserved nature)
<term>Anbether</term> (admirers or worshippers; archaic spelling)

<summary>This page introduces Adelaide, a young woman of noble upbringing and exceptional character, whose physical beauty is matched by her modesty and sensitivity. She is presented as an ideal figure of virtue, sought after by all the young men in her region.</summary>
<keywords>Adelaide, Virtue, Sensibility, Upbringing, Beauty, Modesty, Eighteenth-Century Ideals</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Adelaide’s many suitors from page 7, moving from their general interest to their specific attempts to win her over and Adelaide’s discerning reaction to them.</meta>

One man felt certain of winning Adelaide’s heart through the advantages of his superior education <note>The original German term 'Bildung' refers to more than just schooling; it implies a holistic cultivation of mind, character, and social grace expected of the upper classes.</note>; another plotted to conquer her, cloaking his true soul behind a mask of sensitivity.

Yet external advantages and pretense—which is so easily uncovered—were all in vain.

The young woman’s eye was too keen, and Adelaide knew how to distinguish the true value of a soul from its deceptive outer shell.

Before her piercing gaze, all pretense was unmasked. She laughed at the foolish infatuation <note>The original 'Schwärmerey' suggests an excessive, sentimental, or unrealistic devotion typical of the Romantic era.</note> of her suitors, and the various roles they performed led Adelaide’s soul to the melancholy realization of how corrupted the morals of our age had become.

Adelaide’s character was bolstered by her worthy mother, who instilled in the girl a clear understanding of the true value of virtue.

When morning dawned upon the horizon, as the shadows fled and the crimson
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Adelaide uses her keen perception to see through the false pretenses and "masks" of her many suitors. Supported by her mother’s moral guidance, she laments the superficiality and corrupted morals of her society.</summary>

<keywords>Adelaide, suitors, discernment, pretense, morality, virtue, Bildung, social criticism</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Adelaide’s inner life and her preference for natural solitude over the artificiality of high society. The previous page ended with the break: "as the shadows fled and the crimson..."</meta>

of the dawn announced the arrival of the sun, then, when the lark sang its first song to the Creator, Adelaide was often already awake, and wandered lonely among leafy paths of wild jasmine, and her heart spoke with the Deity. <note>The term 'Gottheit' (Deity) reflects the Enlightenment and early Romantic tendency to view God through the lens of nature and reason rather than strictly through church dogma.</note>

To sensitive souls <note>The original 'Empfindsamen Seelen' refers to the 18th-century literary movement of 'Empfindsamkeit' or Sensibility, which valued deep emotional responses to nature and morality.</note>, the hours of solitude are sacred—then, when nature lies in slumber and the creature is alone with the Creator, that is the hour of self-examination. In the temple of peace is the residence of wisdom—not in the throng of the world, not at court, nor in the noisy madhouse of the philosophers.

Only a quiet heart is receptive to wisdom—the sun is not reflected in the brook that the rain muddies, or that the storm-wind piles into floods—a calm spring is its mirror. Convinced of this truth, Adelaide often indulged in thoughts of sweet melancholy <note>In this period, 'Schwerhmuth' (melancholy) was often seen as a refined, poetic state of mind rather than a purely negative depression.</note>—her heart beat, a tear flowed from her eye—as she cried out: "Ah! Will I then never see virtue without pretense? Ah! How many contradictions do I not find daily among the..."

<summary>Adelaide finds spiritual clarity in early morning solitude, contrasting the peaceful wisdom found in nature with the chaotic "madhouse" of society and philosophy. She laments the lack of genuine, unmasked virtue in the world around her.</summary>

<keywords>Adelaide, Sensibility, nature, solitude, wisdom, philosophy, virtue, melancholy, morning</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Adelaide's monologue from page 9, finishing the sentence "...daily under the sun!"</meta>

sun! <note>original: "Sonne!" - This completes the exclamation from the previous page: "...daily under the sun!"</note> How much vanity, how much hypocrisy <note>original: "Verstellung"</note> — how much false friendship — honesty has become a mere toy to the wicked.

How my heart revolts when I see the swarm of those unfaithful people — how they, prostrate at the feet of their beloveds, take the Deity as witness to their love, which their unfeeling souls do not even know.

How my poor heart pities those weak creatures who seek contentment in a lifestyle <note>original: "Lebenswandel," referring to one's conduct or way of life</note> upon which Nature has placed only contempt and shame.

"No," Adelaide continued, "no, let Heaven be my support, so that my heart never sleeps the slumber of false love. — I am called beautiful; I am surrounded by young men, but what is their chatter but foolishness? Their love is like the northern lights, which glow only in the midnight hours and vanish like a meteor in the morning — and their passion <note>original: "Hitze," literally "heat"</note> is like a rocket <note>original: "Raket" - likely referring to a firework rocket, a common 18th-century metaphor for fleeting brilliance</note>, which rises magnificently and then on

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Adelaide’s monologue from page 10, completing the metaphor of the rocket that vanishes eternally.</meta>

eternally vanishes. — I know it well, that I will love one day — I feel it, that my heart is created for this gentle impulse <note>original: "sanften Triebe"</note>, but where is the good mortal who can captivate my heart? — alas, I fear that it is only a phantom <note>original: "Schattenbild," literally a shadow-image or silhouette</note> for which I long.

So thought Adelaide; her days flowed by quietly and calmly, and her soul had never yet felt the tumult of passions; still the good girl tasted the quiet joys of innocence, tenderly loved by her parents <note>original: "Aeltern," an archaic spelling of "Eltern"</note>, adored by all those who knew her. — Favored by fortune, nothing remained for her to wish for, except that Nature might have given her a better father.

Adelaide’s father was extremely dissolute <note>original: "ausschweifend"</note>. Drunkenness and lust <note>original: "Geilheit" — in this historical context, referring to lechery or lack of moral restraint</note> exhausted his fortune and weakened his health. — Vain were the pleas <note>original: "Vorstellungen," referring to formal protests or earnest remonstrances</note> of his wife; in vain did Adelaide’s tears lead him back from his confusion; the power of habit tore apart again the sacred bonds that chained him to his family, and he plunged once more into the abysses that he dug for himself beneath his own feet.

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's description of Adelaide's father's descent into vice and the failure of his family's pleas to save him, this page describes his final illness and Adelaide's intense mourning.</meta>

His weak health sank under the burden of debauchery; a poisoned fever seized him, and the most shameful illness <note>In the moralizing literature of the late 18th century, a "shameful illness" (schändlichste Krankheit) almost always refers to a venereal disease, serving as a warning against "excessive" lifestyles.</note>, the inevitable consequence of dissipation, delivered him into the arms of death.

What imagination would be descriptive enough to depict Adelaide’s sorrows as she, stretched out upon the corpse of her father, felt the grief of a tender soul. A flood of tears rushed down Adelaide’s cheeks, and had the Deity not made the Angel of Death deaf to the pleas of mortals, he would have returned her father to Adelaide.

Her blonde hair lay scattered upon her breast, her hands reached toward the heavens, and sighs heaved her innocent bosom, which was flooded by a stream of tears.

Thus, during roaring thunderstorms, the rainwater rushes down from the cliff and drowns the lilies in the meadows.

<meta>catchword: Soon</meta>

<summary>Adelaide's father dies from the physical consequences of his dissolute life, leaving her in a state of profound, poetic grief. The passage uses dramatic, sentimental imagery to contrast the father's "shameful" end with Adelaide's pure and overwhelming sorrow.</summary>
<keywords>Adelaide, Death of the Father, Grief, Dissipation, Fever, Sentimentalism, Nature Metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's description of the father's death from a "shameful illness," Adelaide awakens from her initial shock to a second, more intense wave of grief, as indicated by the catchword "Soon" from the preceding page.</meta>

Soon Adelaide recovered again, and threw herself with stronger feeling upon the corpse of her father, pressing him to her bosom as if she wished to reanimate the lifeless shell with half of her own soul—she spoke as if his ear could still hear—she kissed him as if his lips still felt the kiss of love. In vain—Adelaide could say to herself, this was my father—"was my father," she cried, "—was—and is therefore no more, hears my sighs no longer, feels my kiss no more." — — Pallor covered her cheek—Adelaide sank down unconscious, and half-dying, she was removed from the object of her filial love.

Oh, noble image of filial piety! <note>The German *kindliche Frömmigkeit* refers to the classical concept of *pietas*: the dutiful love and respect a child owes a parent, regardless of the parent's character.</note> You are as beautiful as you are rare. — Adelaide is inconsolable at the loss of a father who was, after all, so little worthy of such noble tears—and yet, how many daughters are unfeeling at the death of the noblest of all fathers. —

Oh Nature, burst forth from your circles—mingle the elements—chain the thunder to the sea, and feed the sun with our
<meta>catchword: Earth-</meta>

<summary>Adelaide experiences a second, more profound wave of grief over her father's body before falling unconscious. The narrator praises her "filial piety," contrasting her devotion to an unworthy father with the indifference shown by other children to virtuous parents.</summary>
<keywords>Adelaide, filial piety, grief, mourning, father, nature, sentimentalism</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's dramatic monologue from the previous page, completing the sentence ending in "earthball." It transitions from the death of Adelaide’s father to the immediate financial ruin of her family.</meta>

earthball, only let me not experience the most terrible thing in nature—the ingratitude of a child. — — —

The hour in which Adelaide lost her father was the beginning of her misfortune. Scarcely were the first days of mourning past, and scarcely had Adelaide paid the last of the honors to her father’s ashes <note>The "ashes" here refers poetically to his remains or memory, as traditional burial was the norm, but "ashes" was a common literary term for the deceased</note>, when a crowd of creditors forced their way into the house of the unfortunate family and seized all their belongings.

With tears in her eyes, Adelaide saw the cruel devastation. Bare, empty walls <note>The original "Geräthlose Wände" literally means walls without furniture or tools</note> surrounded the unfortunate women, who were very soon forced to leave their house as well.

Already Adelaide wandered about at her mother’s side like a beggar woman, seeking some humble shelter just to protect themselves from the wind and rain.

The friends of good fortune <note>A reference to "fair-weather friends" who disappear when wealth is lost</note> had already fled from Adelaide, and wealthier relatives no longer recognized those who had fallen into such poverty. Proudly they looked down from their heights,

turned their backs, and passed by without feeling; and those—who themselves once lived off their fortune <note>The original "Vermögen" implies both their wealth and their means of living</note>—cast their eyes to the ground at the sight of them, as if they had never seen the unfortunate women.

Such is the common course of the world: rich people, raised in abundance, do not know the suffering of poverty: because their stomachs are always bursting with food, it does not occur to them that anyone else in the world can still be hungry.

Only he who has been unhappy and poor can come to know mankind; for to him who always lives in abundance, this benefit <note>The term "Wohlthat" (modern: Wohltat) usually means a blessing or a kind deed; the narrator suggests here that knowing the "true" (often ugly) nature of people is a grim sort of gift</note> is not granted.

## 2.
# Continuation

# Adelaide’s Story.

She was already about to carry out her plan, already grasping the instrument of the deed <note>A tool or weapon intended for suicide.</note> that her melancholy had been brooding upon, when by chance her mother stepped into the room—and one look—a look of natural affection <note>original: "ein Blick der Natur"; in the literature of this period, "Nature" often refers to the innate, biological bond and emotional pull between family members.</note> disarmed the unfortunate girl once more. “I was about to leave you!” cried Adelaide. “I—you—tenderest of mothers—I was about to leave you alone, without support, without a friend in this vale of tears <note>original: "Weltthale" (valley of the world); a common 18th-century poetic term for the earthly world as a place of suffering and trial.</note>—no, I cannot! Begone, most terrible of thoughts—nature triumphs—see, see the tears falling from my mother’s eyes! Love calls to me; I must dry them.” With these words, Adelaide embraced her mother and kissed the tears from her careworn cheeks.

“You are killing me, Adelaide!” her mother replied. “Stop lamenting our fate so vividly; your tears tear my soul apart—soon death will [me]

Nothing in my life was so striking to me as the peculiar way in which most people comfort the unfortunate.

Their ice-cold expressions, their tearless eyes, and their wooden souls often increased the misfortune of a sensitive <term>sufferer</term><note>The original German "empfindsamer Leidender" evokes the 18th-century movement of "Sensibility" (Empfindsamkeit), which valued deep emotional responses and empathy as signs of a refined soul.</note> rather than pouring comfort into his beating heart.

"Just think!" they say, "you are not nearly so unhappy as this person or that one," and is this comparison supposed to be comfort?

Insensitive man! If my unfortunate fellow human is even more miserable than I, is this thought supposed to comfort me? You inhumane creatures! Do you believe then that the unfortunate no longer have a heart to feel for others? Am I to seek my comfort in the torments of others? Be silent, cruel ones! Barbarity is your consolation—insensitivity your encouragement. Do I suffer any less because my fellow man suffers even more? Will the rags that my—

One must have a wicked heart, or at least a predisposition toward evil, if such arguments can provide relief to our soul. To me, it would be much more of a comfort if someone could tell me that all the people around me were happier than I; to share in the fate of my brothers would be a balm for my suffering heart.

In my journey through this world thus far, I also became acquainted with a type of person who takes almost no interest in the wretched. The foundations of their <term>Philosophy of Comfort</term><note>The narrator is likely satirizing a brand of popular philosophy or "Stoicism" common in the 18th century that dismissed physical suffering as irrelevant to the mind.</note> consist entirely of this: everything for a person exists only in the <term>imagination</term><note>The German "Einbildung" here implies "fancy" or "mental representation." The manner in which Bodevil brought this news to the unfortunate ones distinguished his excellent heart.

"You have," he said to Adelaide’s mother, "always deemed me worthy of your friendship; do not be angry with me if I have ventured something on the account of your good heart." Here he fell silent and handed over the assurance of the annual pension. A blush of shame <note>Bodevil blushes because, in the 18th-century culture of "sensibility," accepting charity was often seen as a potential humiliation for families of a certain social standing. His embarrassment demonstrates his empathy; he feels the weight of their perceived "degradation" alongside them.</note> spread across his cheeks, and a tear flowed from his eye—as he continued—"Forgive me that I dared to humble you so—I have never yet insulted your friendship, nor ever made myself unworthy of your esteem. This is the first mistake I have dared to commit against your indulgence, and my heart will justify me." With these words, he stood up quickly and left the unfortunate family to spare them a word of thanks, which for many a sensitive heart <note>The original German uses the word *empfindsam*, a key term for the 18th-century "Age of Sensibility." It describes a person who is highly attuned to emotion and the suffering of others.</note> could only too often renew the awareness of their sad circumstances.

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with Bodevil leaving the room quickly after securing a pension for Adelaide and her mother, sparing them the embarrassment of expressed gratitude. This page continues the philosophical reflection on the proper way to give charity without wounding the recipient's dignity.</meta>

Being charitable, and being charitable with feeling, are very different; the person who makes his suffering brother feel his charity—is a cruel benefactor. Giving without humiliating, bestowing without offending a sensitive heart, that is true greatness of heart, true goodness of the soul, and is the art of feeling. Very few people are charitable in this way.

I knew a man, to whom an unfortunate soul once entered the room while several people were present. The poor man blushed immediately and cast his eyes deep toward the ground; but the philanthropist <note>original: "Menschenfreund" — literally a "friend of humanity," a term popular in the 18th century to describe a person of deep empathy and civic virtue.</note> knew how to make use of his distraction with goodness of heart. "You have no reason," he said, "to blush, for you really do not disturb me, and I was just about to send for you this very hour: for when you were with me yesterday at the game <note>likely a card game or social gambling, which provided a perfect excuse for having a purse of money involved.</note>; you forgot your purse, which I wanted to send back to you." Here he gave him the purse, and spoke so much that he left the poor man no time to thank him, and no one in the company knew a word of the good deed except for me, because the unfortunate man told me of it.

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the narrator's moral reflections on the ethics of giving before returning to the story of Adelaide and her mother, who have just been helped by Vodevil (spelled "Bodevil" on the previous page). Our heart must never let the unfortunate person know that we attach a value to the gift they receive from us.

In our eyes, they must read neither contempt nor humiliation, and our gaze must not wound them with reproaches for their misfortune; otherwise, a good deed <note>original: "Wohlthat" — can also be translated as an act of charity or a benefit.</note> ceases to be a good deed if it must be bought so dearly.

I also do not like to hear the boasting of philanthropists <note>original: "Menschenfreunde" — literally "friends of humanity." This refers to the 18th-century Enlightenment ideal of a person who performs selfless acts for the sake of human progress.</note>: "I did this for that person, and that for this person"—if you have done good, do not boast before men: He who knows the hearts of mortals knows your good deeds—and no one else but He should know of them.

Prostrate on the earth, Adelaide thanked Heaven for the unexpected support, and feelings of gratitude for Vodevil <note>The spelling here is "Vodevil," though the previous page used "Bodevil." Such variations are common in manuscript transcriptions of this era.</note> welled up in her soul toward Heaven.

If only girls knew, or rather felt, how modesty <note>original: "Eingezogenheit"; a recurring theme in this text referring to a lifestyle of virtuous privacy and reserve.</note> enhances youthful charms, they could never tear themselves away from it. An innocent brow, an eye full of soul, a mouth that coquetry <note>original: "Koquetterie"</note> has not yet distorted through wasting — a cheek where virtue still plays with the rose that adorns it. Alas! What a contrast to the shameless nature of the seductress <note>original: "Buhlerinn"; a derogatory term for a woman of loose morals or a mistress.</note>. Her misshapen eye, her smiling lips, her brow stripped of the flowers of virtue, her painted cheeks where falsehood reigns and deceit hides beneath makeup <note>original: "Schminke"; in the 18th century, heavy makeup was often associated with moral corruption and the hiding of one’s true character.</note>. Alas! What a picture! To the shame of nature, vice has drawn it, and the brush of the consequences of crime disfigures it with the blackest of its colors.

Adelaide’s portrait was not this: her features were those of innocence and virtue — a multitude of lustful libertines <note>original: "Wollüstlinge"; men driven by sensory pleasure and lack of moral restraint.</note>, upon whom nature bestowed nothing but wealth, quarreled over Adelaide, and each wished to assure her of more brilliant offers <note>original: "Anerbiethungen"; referring here to financial or social proposals intended to entice her into a relationship.</note>.

Adelaide, who knew the value of virtue and loved honor more than wealth, rejected every one of their offers with noble pride.

"People," Adelaide cried out, "must truly be quite wicked—for when I was rich, they showed me reverence, and now that I am poor, they despise me and dare to dishonor me—oh, you wretches! How corrupt your hearts are!

You forget the sanctity of poverty <note>In 18th-century literature, poverty was often portrayed as a "sacred" state that tested and proved a person's true moral character.</note>, and with souls devoid of honor, you dare to make proposals to a poor girl that must make my cheeks blush."

...souls seek out: money, the intention to force one's way into a family—self-interest is the driving force <note>original: "Triebfeder" — literally the "mainspring" of a clock; a common Enlightenment-era metaphor for human motivation.</note> of men when they choose girls to be their wives. O Adelaide! What will become of you?

Nothing more was needed to increase the magnitude of your misfortune than that you also had to fall in love. Poor heart! What will become of Adelaide? — Could Bervil not also be one of those false men, who perhaps knows how to cover his ignoble intentions with the varnish <note>original: "Firniß" — a metaphor for a thin, polished layer of social grace used to mask one's true character.</note> of a good education? Who—but no—it would be an insult to Bervil—Bervil will not be a deceiver.

Adelaide spent the night in these thoughts, until the rising sun woke her from her reverie and announced to the girl that the spring morning was already delighting the farmer <note>original: "Landmann" — the text contrasts the simple, honest labor of the peasant with the complex emotional suffering of the urban protagonist.</note>.

Adelaide left her bed and hurried in the early morning out of the city into the open fields, seeking freedom for her soul and rest for her wounded heart.

### 3.
## Continuation
->of<-
## Adelaide’s Story.

The same feeling dwelled in Bervil’s heart; like Adelaide, he was already awake with the morning sun and sought out solitary places to nourish his love.

The purest sympathy <note>In the 18th century, "sympathy" referred to a resonance or spiritual connection where the internal emotions of a person and the external world act in harmony.</note> exists between nature and love. Noble love gives the soul its most beautiful attunement; it transforms our entire being and makes our heart receptive to the impressions of every flower.

Nature takes on new charms for those in love. Benevolence for every living creature awakens in the gentle soul, and harmony exists within the whole.

Love is the noblest of feelings. To love and always to love is the bliss of spirits and the great destiny of mortals. The Godhead wrote this into the Book of Nature <note>The "Book of Nature" is a philosophical concept suggesting that God's will and wisdom can be read directly from the natural world, as if it were a second set of scriptures.</note> with indelible strokes, and sanctified the mortal who reads and feels it.

<meta>catchword: Love</meta>

<summary>The narrative shifts to Bervil, who shares Adelaide’s sunrise vigil, exploring the philosophy that noble love harmonizes the human soul with the natural world. It portrays love as a divine destiny written into the fabric of nature itself.</summary>
<keywords>Bervil, Adelaide, Sentimentalism, Sympathy, Book of Nature, Philosophy of Love, Romanticism</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 33: The narrator continues the philosophical reflection on the nature of love as experienced by Adelaide and Bervil, expanding on the distinction between physical lust and spiritual devotion.</meta>

Love is the most powerful of the passions. Had legislators always known its power; had they known how to guide the hearts of humanity through it, the world would be far less unhappy.

But this love of which I speak is not that which the libertine <term>Wollüstling: a person driven by purely physical or sensual desires rather than emotional connection</term> calls love: I do not mean that degrading feeling of animalistic pleasure <note>original: "thierischen Genußes"</note>, which causes so much evil among mortals; rather, I mean that gentle sensation of kindred souls, that harmony of two creatures, that divine feeling that elevates our existence, that magnetic force that acts from soul to soul, which worthily exalts our imagination, which gives our intellect a fiery momentum, which beautifies the nature around us, and attunes our soul to compassion for every creature. In a word: I mean that pure flame of the soul, which the Deity itself placed in our hearts to let us know the greatness of His goodness, and which one day—when we have cast off this bodily husk <note>A common Romantic metaphor for the physical body as a temporary shell or "hülle" for the eternal soul</note>—will lead us toward spiritual unification. —

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 33: The narrator continues his philosophical critique of society, contrasting the "pure flame" of spiritual love discussed previously with the superficiality and moral decay he observes in the youth of his day.</meta>

This is a language that few will understand, for only to a few is it granted to taste the sweetness of this quintessence of human feeling.

Lust <term>Wollust: In this context, it refers to excessive sensual indulgence or debauchery that was thought to weaken both body and character</term> has made the hearts of our young men weary; the nerve <note>In the 18th-century "Age of Sensibility," nerves were seen as the physical messengers of the soul; "loss of vigor" implied a numbing of one's moral and emotional capacity</note> has lost its vigor, and is no longer receptive to gentler impressions. Coquetry has corrupted the souls of most of our young women; their spirit is entirely material, and soulful feeling <note>original: "Seelengefühl"</note> cannot operate beneath such a weight.

I shudder at the sight of so many figures like unto statues, which are indeed beautifully carved, yet so entirely devoid of soul. Their hearts hang upon dappled ribbons and plumes of feathers—hearts that have never yet opened to the gentler sensations of nature.

There they stagger about in boisterous dances, completely throwing into disorder those few fibers in their weak brains that might still be receptive to a rational impression.

You know it, maiden of my heart! that my whole soul clings to you, that I love you, love you as much as any mortal has ever loved; for a long time you have already striven to tear the secret from my heart, which until now has strewn my days with melancholy <note>original: *Wehemuth* (modern: *Wehmut*), a bittersweet sadness or yearning central to the 18th-century "Sentimentalism" literary movement</note>; well then! so know my whole misfortune — here, read and pity your poor Bervil — with these words Bervil handed his maiden the letters he had received from his father. — Adelaide read through them with a dry eye, and with a coldness of heart <note>original: *Herzenskålte*</note> that drove Bervil to despair. After a moment’s reflection — she called out — leave me, beloved Bervil! stifle an unhappy love that would embitter your days; follow the will of your father, for the rights of nature <note>The "rights of nature" (*Rechte der Natur*) here refers to the natural law of filial obedience and the duty a child owes a parent.</note> are the most sacred.

Bervil. I know no more sacred rights than those of love — I live for you, Adelaide — I want to die for you. —

Adelaide. How the passions <note>original: *Leidenſchaften*</note> can blind a man, poor Bervil! strengthen your weak heart, and do not forget your duty — we shall see each other no more, Bervil! how cruel it would be if I embittered the remaining days of your father’s life — no, Adelaide desires the peace of a venerable old man <note>The sentence ends abruptly here; she is likely stating she does not wish to disturb the father's tranquility.</note>

<summary>Bervil reveals letters from his father that oppose his union with Adelaide; while Bervil declares his eternal devotion, Adelaide reacts with surprising detachment and urges him to obey his father.</summary>

<keywords>filial duty, romantic love, Bervil, Adelaide, parental authority, Sentimentalism, heartbreak</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the dramatic dialogue between Vervil (referred to as Bervil on the previous page) and Adelaide. It completes the sentence from page 47 regarding Adelaide’s refusal to disturb the peace of Vervil's father.</meta>

man's peace. <note>The word "Greisen" (old man) began on the previous page; "sen" is the conclusion of that word.</note> Vervil! You shall not see me anymore—I will it so.

---

**Vervil.** I am to flee from you! Very well, then flee with me; a wilderness shall receive us—a benevolent rock protect us from the persecutions of men—I will plow the earth with my own hands—force nature to open her fertile bosom <note>original: "Schooß" - meaning womb or lap, here used metaphorically for the earth's fertility</note> for us; we will be poor, but happy. — It is for you, Adelaide, that I work—and can you leave me?

**Adelaide.** You are dreaming, <note>original: "schwärmen" - this suggests Vervil is caught up in wild, idealistic, or visionary enthusiasm</note> Vervil—you love me, I am convinced of it, but your passion heightens your imagination through dazzling fantasies. — Do not forget virtue, Vervil! nor the respect you owe to a poor girl. — I will never leave my mother—farewell, forget Adelaide—believe me, Vervil! my heart suffers as I tell you this. Farewell—virtue demands our separation. "Farewell," cried Vervil—but "no," he said again—and wanted to embrace Adelaide, who, however, had already been gone for a while and had retired to her room.

Vervil had completely lost his composure— — and like a man in despair, he hurried back to his dwelling; and the "farewell" he heard from Adelaide’s mouth still echoed in his ear like the pronouncement of a death sentence in the soul of a condemned man.

### 4.
# Continuation
### of
## Adelaide's Story.

Bervil sat there for a while, completely stunned; <note>original: "voll Betäubung" — a state of being dazed, numbed, or in a stupor from emotional shock</note> he did not know whether the happy days he had enjoyed in Adelaide’s company were dreams or reality.

"Where am I now?" he cried out—"and where have the hours of my happiness gone? Where is Adelaide? Has she vanished from me forever? — Poor Bervil! To what boundless misery has your fate condemned you! — So all these beautiful days of love were merely daydreams for me! — All the happiness that my imagination <note>original: "Einbildungskraft" — the faculty of forming mental images; here it suggests his mind painted a future that reality has now shattered</note> presented to me in such vivid colors—a delusion! —

Did Adelaide not tell me then that she loved me? Loved—oh no—Adelaide has never loved; can one love and then leave? — Wretched as I am! All hope is lost to me—but not my love. This love still beats in every pulse, still flows out in every tear, still breathes in"

<summary>Following Adelaide's departure, Bervil falls into a state of stunned despair, questioning whether their time together was a dream and doubting the sincerity of her love. He laments his fate and the loss of the future he had imagined, though his passion for her remains undiminished.</summary>
<keywords>Bervil, Adelaide, heartbreak, imagination, despair, fate, unrequited love</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues Bervil’s monologue from page 49. The previous page ended with the word "every," leading into "every sigh" here. The tone remains highly sentimental, typical of late 18th-century German literature.</meta>

every sigh — let me then lament, cruel girl! even if you do not see my tears, even if you do not hear my sighs. — The world is already desolate for me, like the grave; everything around me is a sad, lifeless solitude, and only death is still my hope; daily I sigh toward it. What should I do in a world where an Adelaide shall no longer be for me! — why, and for what purpose should I still live? To sigh, to shed tears, to wish for death every day? — Ah! if only I had never been happy; ah! if only I had never known Adelaide! — how quickly you have fled, you sweet, pleasant dreams! — with what darkness you have ended, you spring days of my life! — where are they, the beloved fantasies of the future; where are the days where every minute was golden <note>original: "gülden," an archaic poetic form of "goldenen"</note>, every moment was blessed! — Thus a deceptive dawn <note>original: "Morgenroth," literally "morning-red" or aurora</note> announces the most beautiful day to the carefree countryman, and suddenly <note>original: "gähling," an archaic variant of "jählings," meaning abruptly or suddenly</note> clouds rise up, and a thunderstorm disturbs his rural joy — disturbs — yes, but only for a time — and for me, my happiness is destroyed forever — lost forever. —

<meta>This page transitions from Bervil's emotional monologue on page 50 to a scene where his father finds him in a state of physical and emotional collapse. With this exclamation, Bervil collapsed onto his bed, and the magnitude of his grief <note>original: "Schmerzens," an archaic genitive form of pain or sorrow</note> stripped him of his senses for a time. —

During this interval, Bervil’s father arrived in the city; he sought out his son and found him in the post-house <term>post-house</term> (an inn or station where mail was delivered and travelers could find lodging) in this state.

For a considerable while, Bervil’s father stood before his son, who still lay there beside himself <note>original: "außer sich," literally "outside himself," indicating a state of being overwhelmed or unconscious</note> as if in a deep slumber, and he did not know what to think. — The paleness covering Bervil’s cheeks, the sorrow etched upon his forehead—the tear still trembling in his eye—all of this led him to fear the worst.

The old man sat down by Bervil’s side, took the young man's hand in his own—and whispered softly into his ear: — Bervil, your father, whom you love, is seeking you. —

Bervil opened his eyes, stared fixedly into his face, gathered his strength, and threw his arms around his father's neck—

"O my father! My father!" — this was all that he said. —

Unfortunate child! — In what a state must I find you! — Vervil, how haggard you have become! <note>original: "entstaltet," meaning his physical appearance has been ruined or disfigured by his emotional distress</note> — Have you abandoned virtue, you wretched soul?

**Vervil.** No, father, no — I was always faithful to its principles; I have never felt the value of virtue as much as I feel it now; — but I am unhappy, extremely unhappy — I am in love. —

**Father.** I know that you are in love, and that is why I am here: to rescue you from the snares of a harlot <term>Buhlerinn: a derogatory term for a woman of loose morals or a temptress</term> who is dragging you into ruin.

**Vervil.** Do not insult Adelaide with such an expression, father; she is the most virtuous girl; she herself commanded me to leave her and follow you — but father! — if you have ever felt love — have mercy on me — and spare my suffering heart.

Thus lamented Bervil’s father, lifting his eyes toward heaven. One moment he approached his sick son, the next he placed Bervil’s hand in his own, then he kissed it and moistened it with tears. Everyone present was moved by this scene—but now was not the time for useless weeping. The circumstances demanded a rescue; this was pointed out to the weeping father, and he hastily resolved to travel to the capital to fetch Adelaide.

Bervil’s father was now with Adelaide. With the warmest emotion, he described the unhappy condition of his son; he implored her, by the love she felt for Bervil, to follow him. Adelaide sank into a faint. <note>In the literature of this period, a "faint" was a conventional way to signal that a character possessed a "sensitive soul" capable of profound feeling.</note>

The love she felt for Bervil gave wings to her heart, and the wish arose in her soul to save her beloved; but her <term>virtue (Tugend)</term> <note>In the 18th century, "virtue" was a central social concept for women, encompassing not just morality but also the strict maintenance of a chaste reputation.</note> and the respect she held for her mother held her back. "Find a means," she began, "to bid my heart be silent to the call of my duty; I am ready to do everything you desire of me, but protect me from <term>slander (Nachrede)</term> and from a biased interpretation of my actions. The world looks for the slightest appearance of impropriety to plunge us into ruin, to damage our reputation and our honor. — What would people judge of me, if I left my mother to [follow] my beloved..."

-> a story. <-

# **H**ans was the only son of a grey-haired, retired <term>abgedienten: This term usually refers to a soldier who has completed his service or a laborer who is "worn out" from a lifetime of work.</term> father, for whom the child now had to take the father’s place as provider. He also had a sister, Margareth, who, besides the housework, was a careful caretaker for her old father. Their small field meagerly nourished three noble people, a pair of cows, and a goat. Yet they faithfully gave what belonged to the Lord <note>original: "dem Herrn"—this likely refers to the feudal landlord to whom they owed taxes or tithes, though it carries a dual religious meaning.</note>, and were more concerned with being able to pay their dues on time than they were for their own lives. — But when does the virtuous person, who trusts in God and works gladly, ever lack for anything? — Hans worked gladly; from his youth, he had been accustomed to labor by his industrious parents, and love for his father and necessity nourished his joy <note>original: "Freunde" (friends); this is likely a typesetter's error for "Freude" (joy), which would more naturally fit the context of "nourishing a joy for work."</note> for work. The first rays of the rising dawn already found him in the field, and even when the sun had long since crossed over the mountains, Hans still tended to a small plot of land. Often he worked by moonlight, and God allowed his diligence to prosper.
[[margin: E]] -> When <-

[[vocabulary: Hans, Story, Father's role, Margareth, Dawn, Moonlight]]

<summary>This page introduces Hans, a dutiful son who supports his elderly father and sister through tireless agricultural labor. It serves as a moral portrait of the "virtuous peasant" who balances feudal obligations with religious faith and family devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Hans, Margareth, Piety, Diligence, Feudal Dues, Family Duty, Agriculture, 18th-century Narrative</keywords>

[[page number: 66]]

When he then sometimes exhausted, and entirely covered with sweat in the glowing July <note>original: "Julius," the Latin name for the month, which remained common in German usage during the 18th and 19th centuries.</note>, consumed his meager midday meal in the coolness of a shady linden tree; all his nerves had lost their tension, and Nature herself sank him into a refreshing sleep which he could no longer resist, then he always prayed upon awakening from the depths of his heart to God:
> "Dear God! Grant me again the lost
> "strength of the previous day, and
> "do not let me fall ill, so that I
> "can work, so that my good father,
> "who also worked for me,
> "may not suffer want in the eightieth of his years!"

— When does such a prayer ever ascend to heaven from the cities? There in the grand houses, where bread is lavishly discarded—bread that the poor farmer <note>original: *Landmann*. This reflects the 18th-century "physiocratic" ideal that the farmer is the primary producer of a nation's wealth.</note> must provide with months of labor: there, where days are squandered and nights are spent in carousing, and one only rests from idle leisure, one does not think to ask God for strength and blessing.

So Hans always worked bravely onward, and nourished his good father even into his ninetieth year. Now the good
-> old man <-

<summary>Hans offers a humble prayer for the health and strength necessary to support his father, while the narrator pauses to criticize the perceived wastefulness and ingratitude of wealthy city dwellers.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the description of Hans’s dedicated care for his ninety-year-old father. The narrative now shifts from the labor of care to the peaceful passing of the old man, using his "natural" death as a moral lesson for the reader.</meta>

[[page number: 67]]

-> ——— <-

The old man approached closer and closer to the goal; his strength left him, the parts of the body no longer worked toward a common purpose, the machine fell apart <note>original: "die Maschine zerfiel." During the 18th century, it was common to describe the human body as a "machine" or a complex mechanism, reflecting the scientific and philosophical views of the Enlightenment.</note> — the father ceased to live. What a blessed death! —

As cheerful and quiet as his life had been, he passed away. No agonizing reproaches, no terrifying images frightened his soul; his passing was not accompanied by hideous convulsions, nor by any of the signs of a painful death brought upon oneself <note>This likely refers to diseases or physical decline caused by "excessive" or "sinful" living, such as alcoholism or debauchery, which the author contrasts with the father's "natural" end.</note>. He had reached the goal of his years, having lived to the very limits of nature.

O you! Who wantonly throw your lives away, wasting years to enjoy a single moment of pleasure <note>original: "wollüstige Minute," implying sensory overindulgence or vice.</note>, look upon the terrible death of one of your own; and then go, and look upon an old man at the end of his path, who never indulged in such desires.

Hans wept bitter tears — tears of the deepest grief over a beloved father. Many, whose hearts cannot grasp such high virtue, would consider it a stroke of luck to be relieved of such a burden; <note>The "burden" here refers to the physical and financial weight of caring for an elderly, unproductive parent.</note>
-> E 2 <- -> and <-

<summary>Hans’s father passes away peacefully after a long, virtuous life, providing the narrator an opportunity to contrast this "natural" death with the agonizing ends of those who live dissolutely. Hans deeply mourns the loss, further proving his exceptional moral character.</summary>
<keywords>death, virtue, filial piety, Enlightenment, morality, mourning</keywords>

and the young gentlemen, who have long waited with impatience for the <term>hard-pinched usury<note>The original German "erkargten Wucher" implies wealth accumulated not just through interest (usury), but through extreme, miserable stinginess.</note></term> of their fathers, in order to be able to squander it just as shamefully as it was acquired—they laugh when their father, in the summer of his years, makes them rich heirs through his death.

But Hans wept from an honest heart, and time, which indeed soothes all pain and often makes one forget, had to spend many days just to dry the <gloss>tears<note>The OCR reads "Zäher," an archaic or misread form of "Zähren" (tears).</note></gloss> of this good man; yet in the depths of his soul, the secret grief still preyed upon his peace.

After a few weeks, his neighbor’s son arrived—a blooming, vigorous lad whose father possessed a handsome piece of fortune, but was nevertheless a sincerely good man. Velten was his name, and Franz was his son. For a long time, he had admired the tender care with which the two children, Hans and Margreth, tended to their dear father; their industry, and their quiet, innocent life. They were good neighbors together, and Velten did them many friendly services. The lovely, cheerful Lise, Franz’s sister, could now no longer hide her feelings: she ran to her father and begged: "Oh! — if Hans must serve as a hand, then please take him into your own service, so that he does not end up with some farmer who treats him harshly."

"No, no! He shall keep his **little estate**," Margreth replied. "And accept what I intend to give him," Franz chimed in, and so the quarrel began anew. Lise was the least satisfied of all with this. She stood in a corner of the room, held her apron over her face, and wept.

Never perhaps was a bond more nobly tied than this; never perhaps was found such true, blessed harmony of hearts, a soul so full of heartfelt joy, as at this rural union. Their hearts were full of the most blessed bliss, and what flowed forth was the most joyful, warmest gratitude to the All-Father <note>A poetic Enlightenment-era term for God (German: *Allvater*), emphasizing His role as the benevolent creator of all humanity.</note> of goodness. The whole neighborhood loved the good people, and all the neighbors in the region, who very soon learned what good fortune had befallen Hans and Grete <note>The text here refers to "Grete," though the previous page named the daughter "Lise." So it went the second and third year, and all his diligence flourished into a rich <term>harvest</term> <note>original: "Erndte"; an archaic spelling of the German *Ernte*.</note>.

Hans found his <term>little estate</term> <note>original: "Gütchen"; a diminutive of *Gut* (estate), referring to a small, manageable farm.</note>, when he bought it, completely desolate; its previous owner had cared for it poorly, and let it go to ruin through negligence; but through Hans's diligence, it was in a short time restored to the most flourishing state.

His example instructed all the neighbors and encouraged them to work. Every time a child was born to him, he planted a shade-providing tree around his farmstead <note>This likely refers to the traditional German custom of planting a "birth tree," often a fruit tree or a sturdy oak, to grow alongside the child.</note>. He cultivated a beautiful orchard, of which one saw hardly a trace with his predecessor, and from time to time replaced the withered little trees with new <term>saplings</term> <note>original: "Sprößlinge"; meaning young shoots or offspring.</note>. In addition, as much as his work in the fields and with the flock allowed time, he devoted himself to <term>beekeeping</term> <note>original: "Bienenzucht"; in this period, beekeeping was highly valued not just for honey, but for the wax needed for candles.</note>, was successful with it, and thus soon showed the other farmers how useful it was.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing narrative of Hans, the industrious farmer. There were many who were less wealthy than he, even poor men indeed, and yet they still went to the inn, and often, when they traveled to the city, they consumed half of their **proceeds** <note>original: "Losung" — the money earned from selling goods at market</note>; but Hans never went for beer, and lived as frugally as before.

He used to say to some of the young farmers: "Dear brothers! How can you give away the fruits of your sweat for something that is so unnecessary to you? — There you sit for half a day in the inn and drink; you do not drink alone, you also gamble, and tell me: has any one of you never quarreled while gaming? — What consequences! — You desecrate God’s holy day with bloody enmity, ruin your health, rob yourselves of the strength for work and the fulfillment of your duties, shorten your lives, and steal the last **emergency pennies** <note>original: "Nothpfennige" — small savings kept for times of dire need</note> from your children. You do this not only by drinking and gambling away your bit of money, but also because your quarreling leads to fines in court, and you must likely also pay a handsome **secondary bill** <note>original: "Nachzeche" — literally a second tab; here it refers to the additional costs resulting from a brawl</note> to the **barber-surgeon** <note>original: "Baader" — a traditional practitioner who performed minor surgery, bloodletting, and treated wounds from fights</note>; and then at last, what remains of your foolish pleasure? Nothing but bitter regret!" —

<meta>This page continues the moralizing monologue of Hans, the industrious farmer. See! I stay nicely at home, and when I want to **treat myself** <note>original: "mir gütlich thun" — to indulge or enjoy oneself with good food and drink</note>, I have sweet milk and fresh butter brought to me—which you also have—and eat my **evening meal** <note>original: "Abendbrod" — a simple supper, typically consisting of bread and cold accompaniments</note> with it alongside my wife and children, and I am quite heartily content in the **bosom** <note>original: "Schooße" — figuratively, the heart or protective circle</note> of my family. — Or I go with all my people to my old father-in-law’s, where I find Franz and my dear sister; there we all sit out in the greenery; tell each other our stories of the day and the funny pranks of our **mischievous boys** <note>original: "losen Buben"</note>, take joy in them, and are so merry there, so—ah! certainly happier than you in the noisy **tavern** <note>original: "Schenke"</note>. — Thus the evening passes for us in cheerful jesting; we go to bed lighthearted and healthy, having gained new strength for work, which you willfully **squander in revelry** <note>original: "verschwelget"</note>.

And how foolish you are when you consume your money in the city! — Ha! people say: "Can the **scoundrels** <note>original: "Schufte"</note> still put on such a grand show? Things must not be going so badly for them yet"—for there, people are always accustomed to **judge thousands by five** <note>original: "von fünfe auf tausende zu schließen" — a proverbial expression meaning that people estimate a person's total wealth based on small, visible displays of spending; here, Hans warns that spending a little in the city makes people think the farmers are secretly rich, which could lead to trouble or higher taxes</note>.

-> ——— <-

Hans’s well-intentioned advice, accompanied by his own example, was not without effect. Here and there, someone took it to heart, reflected upon it, and found that Hans had spoken only too truly, and improved themselves. Many a person had him to thank for their advancement <note>The original German "Aufkommen" implies both recovery from debt and rising in social or financial status.</note>, and this growing number of examples proved enticing to others. To be sure, the innkeeper and the bailiff <note>original: "Amtmann". This was a high-ranking local official or magistrate who managed an administrative district. Both he and the innkeeper profited from the villagers' vices—the one from drink, the other from fines and legal disputes.</note> were very dissatisfied; they would have gladly taken revenge on Hans for this, if they could have gotten a hold on him. Oh, how hard it is that these people must live off the transgressions of others! —

Over time, the family continued to grow larger, and the little estate <note>original: "Gütchen", a diminutive of "Gut" (estate/farm), indicating a modest smallholding.</note> would soon no longer have sustained them. — Oh, how Hans rejoiced then in his thriftiness! — With the modest sum he had saved up through careful housekeeping <note>original: "erhaust", a specific term for accumulating wealth through frugal domestic management.</note> during that time, he bought a couple of fields and a large meadow to add to his estate, and he now found himself in the fortunate position of not having to suffer any want with his children. Indeed, supported by his industry and God’s abundant blessing, Hans often enjoyed the blessed pleasure of doing good for others.

-> Oh, how <-

<summary>Hans's virtuous example begins to improve his community, while his personal frugality allows him to expand his farm and provide for his growing family. The text highlights how his sobriety and hard work lead to prosperity, much to the chagrin of local officials who profited from the villagers' previous vices.</summary>

<keywords>thriftiness, community influence, social reform, agriculture, wealth accumulation, bailiff, innkeeper, family growth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Hans's speech to his family. On the previous page (78), we saw how Hans's example influenced the village and how his frugality allowed him to expand his small estate to support his growing family. He now reflects on the moral value of work over dependency.</meta>

-> ——— <-

“Oh, how good it is,” he often said to his wife and children, “to be industrious and thrifty <note>original: "wirthlich". While this can mean hospitable, in this context it refers to being a good manager of a household—frugal and efficient.</note>. You see, without this, we would have to suffer want <note>original: "darben", meaning to live in extreme poverty or to starve.</note>, or rely on the kindness of another to make up for what we lacked in diligence and economy. — Oh, how hard that would be for me! — Children! It is better to work until the sweat flows in streams from your brow than to go to your neighbor's door and beg for the remnants of his industry. Look! I have no need for that now; I have, instead, come so far that I can relieve <note>original: "steuern". In archaic German, this specifically meant to steer against or counteract a problem, such as poverty.</note> the distress of another. — How my heart would ache if I had to see the tear of an unhappy soul in need, wept in vain, sink into the sand because I could not dry it with my own hands. What is a month of work compared to a single sweet quarter-hour in which I feel the bliss of having soothed another’s gnawing grief, of having encouraged him to new activity <note>original: "Thätigkeit". In this era, this implied more than just 'action'; it suggested being a productive, busy member of society.</note> through my small gift, and—God’s blessing was always present in it—of seeing him so happy again that he no longer required any help. — Children, work! — Work keeps us healthy and makes life bearable.”

-> Se <-

<gloss>wirthlich: thrifty/economical; darben: to suffer want; Schweiß: sweat; Noth: distress/need; Gottessegen: God's blessing; Thätigkeit: activity/industriousness</gloss>

<summary>Hans delivers a moral lesson to his family on the virtues of hard work and frugality, emphasizing that the greatest reward of labor is the ability to provide charity to those in need. He frames self-sufficiency as a matter of pride and work as the key to both physical health and emotional fulfillment.</summary>

Thus Hans often spoke to his children,

### 5.
## Continuation
# Hans
### a story.

**B**ut Hans did not always live in joy; gloomy days also mingled among the bright ones, and embittered a thousand happy hours for him. There were worthless good-for-nothings <term>Taugenichtse <note>A common German term for people perceived as lazy or lacking moral worth.</note></term>, who had squandered their own property <note>original: "Haab", an archaic spelling of "Hab," referring to one's goods and possessions.</note>, and now envied Hans for the fruits of his diligence. He had to sigh, like many a good honest man, under the pressure of persecution. Twice, villains set fire to his house, and twice it burned down from its very foundations. The malice of men pained him; he shed bitter tears <note>original: "Zäher", an archaic spelling of "Zähren," a poetic or dated word for tears.</note> over the loss, for the sweat of many years burned away with it; he suffered, and did not grumble against God's fate. In his fiftieth year, he also experienced the bloody misery of war. Like all inhabitants of the village, he had to provide quarters <term>Quartier <note>The compulsory housing and feeding of soldiers in private homes, a common burden for peasants during European wars.</note></term> to friends and enemies, and friend and foe united in the country's ruin. Oh, how painfully the arrogance <term>Uebermuth <note>Archaic spelling of "Übermut." In this context, it refers to the insolence, recklessness, and lack of restraint shown by the soldiers.</note></term> of these unruly men fell upon him! How heavy it weighed on his heart when he [saw] the

Among all the envious ones who were hostile toward good Hans’s honest progress, Märten was the worst. — A coarse, hostile man, full of wicked malice, for whom the greatest joy was to incite the peasants against one another and to stir up unrest <note>original: *Meutereyen*; refers here to civil disorder or local uprisings among the villagers</note>; he lay in wait for every opportunity to disturb the peace of any happy family, and then laughed into his wicked fist <note>original: *Schelmenfaust*; an idiom similar to "laughing up one's sleeve," but with a more malicious, "rogueish" connotation</note> with fiendish glee when son fought against father, wife against husband, and brother against brother, provoked by him; and when happiness and peace fled from a house where he practiced his evil nature. With a dark, gloomy gaze, which he never dared to lift toward heaven, he crept about the streets, shunned the encounter of an honest man, and took his path upon untrodden ways.

Lazy and inactive toward all that is good, he left the care of his fields to hired farmhands, who then, following the example of their master, did not allow themselves to work too hard either; and so, things were bound to get worse and worse for him. Moreover, he caroused daily from one tavern <note>original: *Schenke*; a local inn or public house where alcohol was served</note> to another, and held company with gamblers who possessed no better hearts

The domestic Lise was just preparing the porridge <note>original: "Brey"; a thick cereal or vegetable mash that was a staple of the 18th and 19th-century German diet.</note> in the kitchen for breakfast, and fetched curdled milk from the cellar for it; and her two little daughters, Trude and Hannchen, carried wood into the kitchen for her, and water, and waited attentively for their mother's signal for new tasks. — Then little Trude snuggled up to her mother, flattered her, and pleaded: "Oh Mother! Put an extra piece of butter on my plate for the porridge today; you know how much I love it: you will, won't you?" — and a favorable nod from her mother delighted the girl so much that she hopped and cheered as if she had won gold. Now she ran briskly, scrubbed the room, and set the table.

Now Hannchen also came, encouraged by the mother’s readiness with which she had indulged Trude, and pleaded; but Hannchen pleaded for more. — "Dear Mother!" she said, "you are very good and love me too: Look, Bronchen <note>A diminutive name, likely for Veronika or Bronislawa.</note>—as you know—is sick, but not so very much that I should be afraid; and she loves me so, and I her too, that she asked me to come to her through the senior maid <term>Großmagd<note>The 'Großmagd' was the head female servant in a rural household, responsible for supervising other maids and overseeing the dairy or kitchen.</note></term> yesterday, and I promised her I would come; by word... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "bey Mund," likely part of the phrase "bey Mund und Wort" (by word of mouth/by my word).</note>

I promised it with mouth and hand <note>original: "Mund und Hand versprach ichs"; a traditional idiom indicating a solemn promise made both verbally and confirmed with a physical gesture, such as a handshake.</note>; and what if I did not keep my word today? — But first, I want to bring father his midday meal <note>original: "Mittagmal"; the main hot meal of the day, usually delivered to men working in the fields.</note>, and he will surely let it be.

Still, the mother’s kind eye did not yet look a "yes" toward her; and sighing, Hannchen raised her pleading gaze up to her mother and seemed almost ready to cry. "May I now?" she said again and tugged gently at her skirt: "If you do not let me go, I will cry for you all day long. Poor little Bronchen!" — Hannchen wept. — "Now," said the mother, "for the sake of the aunt <term>Baase<note>An archaic German term for a female relative, such as an aunt or cousin, or sometimes used as a respectful address for an older woman in a village.</note></term> and Bronchen, I will let you go; but you must no longer weep defiantly when you do not immediately get what you want." Quickly, Hannchen’s eyes looked bright again, the tears were dried—alas! if only tears of sorrow dried so quickly!—and joyfully the lively girl skipped off to her morning chores.

In the mother's heart <note>original: "Busen"; literally "bosom," used here poetically to refer to the seat of emotions.</note>, too, joy beat for her good daughters, and a tender tear rose to her eye, and a wish toward heaven: "Oh! Father of mankind! let them [grow] in innocence and joy into bri-" <note>The text ends mid-word at "Bräu-", which most likely continues as "Bräute" (brides) on the following page, suggesting the mother is praying for their future marriages.</note>

**T**his sad event <note>Original: "Begebenheit." This refers to the arrest of Hans described on the previous page.</note> was made known that very same day throughout the entire circle of Hans’s neighborhood; and a colorful, confused chatter of truth and falsehood, of good and evil, circulated in the area, as the news reached the ears of now a well-meaning person, now an envious, base slanderer <term>slanderer [Verläumder] — someone who makes false and damaging statements about another</term>. Most pitied the honest man, and all who knew him did not want to believe it for a moment. Franz, his brother-in-law <term>brother-in-law [Schwager]</term>, was the first to learn of the lamentable fate of his friend.

When Lise recovered from her initial stupor <note>Original: "Betäubung" — a state of being dazed or numb from shock.</note>, into which the most raging pain had thrown her, she took her children and ran—this was her first thought—to her brother-in-law and lamented her loss to him. Yet all of this could not release Hansen from prison <term>Gefängnisse</term>.

Neither Lise’s piteous pleading, nor the wringing of hands, nor a stream of tears: even if the entire village had vouched for him, he would not have been saved.

The official <term>Beamte; a government administrator or magistrate</term>, as sorry as he felt, and as much as the nearly universal voice of Hans’s fellow citizens <term>Mitbürgern</term> seemed to persuade him of the man’s innocence, could not—for the sake of his own standing and for the sake of justice—retract the step already taken without a formal investigation.

No human would have been able to rescue the unfortunate man if Providence <term>Vorsicht; here used in the historical sense of Divine Providence or the watchful eye of God</term> had not taken up his cause—she whose task it is to avenge offended virtue <term>Tugend</term> and to bring malice <term>Bosheit</term> to shame, even when it already believes its victory is certain.

The two accomplices <term>Gehülfen: Here meaning criminal confederates or hired hands</term> whom Märten used for his abominable villainy <note>original: "Schelmenstück" — while this can sometimes mean a "prank" in modern German, in this context it refers to a heinous criminal act</note> were impoverished peasants, without a roof of their own, without food, and entirely without the prospect of a happier future. Through the humiliations they were forced to endure from others in their state of poverty, they were robbed of all feelings of honor, human dignity, and the striving for higher sentiments. Indeed, through the very harshness with which the wealthy are accustomed to insulting the poor, they were at odds with humanity itself. They were thus ready to welcome any opportunity that would help them satisfy their needs, in whatever form it might present itself. Therefore, they were easily persuaded to a deed from which, at another time and in different circumstances, they would have recoiled at the mere thought.

Thus Providence <note>original: "Vorsicht"; in this context, it refers to Divine Providence or the guiding hand of fate</note> blessed the final active days of the good man with quiet, domestic peace, which helped him forget in joy the bitter days during which he had wept. —

The few years that labor, worry, and tribulations had still left to him passed by without anything further remarkable occurring in his life story.

He wandered through this earthly life on the very same path he had trodden since his youth, and continued with the same steady step. —

-> Quietly <-

Calmly and without any premature longing, he awaited the sign from Providence <note>original: "Vorsehung"; the guiding hand of God or fate</note>, which would tell him when he had reached the goal. Then the sign of the Most High <note>A common reverent term for God</note> came and showed him the finish line of his life’s path <note>original: "Laufbahne"; literally a racetrack, used here to describe the course of one’s career or life</note>, which he had reached with honor. He followed that sign of Providence gratefully, just as a son rushes from a great distance into the arms of his father.

He devoted the final moments of his life to the welfare of his fellow citizens <note>original: "Bürger"; the people of his community</note>. To his children, who stood around him immersed in the deepest grief, he recounted the story of his life. In the face of his own sad circumstances, he showed them God’s benevolent intentions for mankind and exhorted them to remain steadfast in virtue <note>original: "Tugend"; moral excellence, a central theme in 18th and 19th-century German literature</note>.

He who is loved in life is mourned in death. — Both child and man mourned for Hans <note>original: "Hansen"; a dative form of the name Hans</note>. —

The funeral procession was stately and moving: not magnificent, like the funeral processions of noble gentlemen in the cities, for it had no idle people in its pay <note>This refers to "hired mourners," a common practice where people were paid to walk in a procession to make the deceased appear more important.</note> who, draped with the coats of arms of his ancestors and wrapped in mourning crape, would have accompanied him to the grave with half a dozen stinking pitch torches. No gawking rabble <term>Pöbel: a derogatory term for a crowd of onlookers or the "common masses"</term> followed him to feast their eyes on vain shimmer, nor to occupy their gossiping tongues with little anecdotes <note>original: "Histörchen" — implying trivial, often scandalous stories or rumors</note> from the life story of the deceased. — In such cases, one often admires the expense and the costliness of the burials, looks upon the insignia of honor on the bier <note>original: "Baare" — the stand on which a corpse or coffin is placed</note>, yet curses the spirit <note>Referring to the poor character of the person being buried, despite the expensive ceremony.</note>.

Of all that pomp with which human vanity still boasts even in death—seeking to dazzle even there where Truth asserts her rights—none of that pomp was to be seen in Hans’s funeral procession; but it was stately and moving. —

Behind him followed brave sons and good daughters, whom he had gifted to the state, having raised them as useful fellow citizens <term>Mitbürger: a term emphasizing civic duty and belonging to a community</term>, and whom he now left behind as worthy heirs of his virtues. They seemed to suffocate under the weight of their suffering hearts and their tears. They had lost a beloved father who was everything to them. His good wife, who had found all her wishes fulfilled in him, was now left with no comfort other than that of seeing him again one day—there, where no human malice <note>original: "Bosheit der Menschen"</note>, no misfortune, and no death can ever tear him away from her again.

Now followed his relatives, who were also all his friends, and their hot, abundant tears bore witness to their warm, sincere friendship.

A long, endless line glorified the procession and gave the most eloquent proof of the general respect and love that Hans had earned from everyone through his integrity <term>Rechtschaffenheit: a moral quality combining honesty, uprightness, and a sense of duty</term>.

He was covered with a few clods of earth, and no monument preserves his name for his descendants; no memorial tells his story or his merits to the wanderer in the distance of future years.

But it had to be so! The good and the noble of humanity must not count on tombstones or marble memorials.

### 8.
### Herwing and Lindenholm.

# **W**
hen has any human ever examined their heart so closely that they know all the secret impulses toward evil which lie hidden within it, and which often turn traitor even to the best of hearts? Pride and self-interest are mostly the mainsprings <note>original: "Triebfedern" — a mechanical metaphor referring to the driving springs of a clock.</note> of our actions—only too often the secret source from which the most abominable crimes flow. We are, however, usually too confident in our virtue, too gullible toward ourselves, to get to know our own hearts more closely; and self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe" — implies a sense of vanity or self-delusion that prevents honest self-reflection.</note> is always busy hiding us from ourselves. Therefore, a person often becomes a shameful villain before they would even believe it of themselves—that they were on the way to it—because it happens gradually, step by step, without their knowledge. Hardly is the first step taken, then the following ones hurry after, and we have already come so far that return is almost no longer within our power.

How much depends, then, upon our knowing ourselves—on learning to uncover the secret passages of our hearts, to seek out the seeds of the passions <note>original: "Leidenschaften" — in this historical context, "passions" refers to strong, often irrational emotions or desires that were thought to overwhelm reason if not disciplined.</note>, and not to pamper them through self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe" — a recurring theme in this text, referring to a vanity that blinds one to their own faults.</note> as a foolish mother pampers her child. If we do, we raise monsters within them that will tyrannize us as grown men, robbing us of our peace and happiness.

Your own experience, young man! <term>Jüngling (a youth or young man)</term> will, alas, make you acquainted with even more such examples—with examples that are far more detestable. Let them be a powerful lesson to you, and never extinguish from your soul the terrifying image <term>Schreckenbild (an image of horror or a cautionary tale)</term> that such a story has imprinted there.

Herwing and Lindenholm were the closest playmates in their childhood days, and the tenderest friends in their youth. The proximity of their houses, their identical age, their shared occupations, and the friendship of their fathers tied a bond that seemed inseparable and able to defy all storms. They were indispensable to one another, and neither could enjoy a pleasure in which the other did not take part. They

He did not yet dare to risk a public break with Herwing, for his father’s love was too firmly bound to Herwing’s friendship. Consequently, he could not, and dared not, venture an attack on his friend without simultaneously offending his own father and Herwing’s father in the most sensitive way, and without drawing upon himself the hatred of all the noble people who were Herwing’s friends.

Thus, he required dissimulation; he now adopted friendship as a mask behind which he hid his bitter <note>The German "gallsüchtig" literally means "addicted to gall" or "bilious," used here to describe a rancorous and resentful disposition.</note> intentions. His mouth still offered friendly endearments, while his heart swelled with the venom <note>original: "Geifer," meaning slaver or froth, used here metaphorically to describe the "poison" of envy.</note> of envy. He pressed his friend to his breast, while within it a treacherous traitor plotted schemes to secretly undermine his friend’s peace of mind.

Only a year ago, Lindenholm was the noblest youth, the most tender friend, who would have given life and blood for his Herwing; and now, for a mere nothing—for a phantom of his imagination <note>original: "Puppe seiner Einbildung," literally "the doll of his imagination," referring to a trivial or hollow fixation or a vanity-driven delusion.</note>—he tramples friendship underfoot, banishes every feeling of it from his heart, and turns that heart into a for-
