It is a terrible picture—and yet how weak, how indistinct compared to the truth itself! — Herwing, whose noble heart beat with warm, active <note>German: *thätiger*; implying a friendship that manifests through helpful deeds and practical support.</note> friendship for Lindenholm, saw with pain the change that was taking place in his friend. He saw it, felt it—was the only one who felt compassion for the unfortunate man, and did not know that he himself was the one whom Lindenholm hated. He alone dared to try to heal his sick friend. He gathered reasons in his heart, reasons of friendship, in order to calm him. — He strolled with him on many a beautiful evening into the open air, took Lindenholm’s hand in his own, pressed him to his sensitive breast, and said: Lindenholm! — Friend! — Why do you not wish to show me the wound in your heart where it bleeds? — How have I deserved your mistrust? — Tell me—for you are telling it to your friend—how you are to be helped, even if it should cost everything—everything—my life; if only it is in my power to restore peace <note>German: *Ruhe*; in this context, it refers to peace of mind or emotional tranquility.</note> to you once more. —

### 9.
### ( Continuation. )
### Herwing and Lindenholm.

**W**ith what impatience did the two fathers long for the final day, which would bring their sons back into their fatherly arms after their completed academic years <note>The "academic years" (akademischen Jahren) refers to the traditional period of university study, often a time of significant transformation for young men in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note>? — And the closer they were to it, the more impatiently they called for it to hasten, yet the further their impatience seemed to distance them from it. — At last, it appeared nonetheless. Five years were swept away by the unstoppable current of time and poured into the ocean of the past <note>original: "Vorzeit" — literally "primeval times," used here poetically to describe the vast, unreachable expanse of time that has already passed.</note>. Five years — how long you seem to many a father! — How unnoticed you slip away from the youth who pays you no mind! — Five years — vanished forever! —

Herwing himself saw, with a joyful heart, a time coming to an end that had been, until now, the most unpleasant in his life. — He was not accustomed to the people whom he

The only comfort that still flattered his **pride** was that he felt himself to be better, and that he could boast of the **wealth** and prestige of his father. He was rich and had many friends at the **court**. <term>Hof: The seat of a prince or nobleman where political influence was exercised and where career-making "friends" or patrons were essential for advancement.</term>

With these thoughts, Lindenholm approached his hometown within a few hours’ distance; there, both fathers came to meet them and, **weeping with joy**, folded them into their arms.

Herwing’s father, who felt no less tenderly for his son, and who wished to see him in at least the same circumstances of fortune as his friend, felt the slight <note>The German "Kränkung" implies a wounding of one's honor or feelings, in this case caused by Lindenholm's promotion and subsequent arrogance.</note> too deeply for the natural spark of human pride not to be awakened in him as well; this pride then spurred him most vehemently to venture everything possible for his son.

He traveled with him to the city, sought out friends and recommendations, and found them; he did not lack the knowledge of human nature or the cleverness required to find the various paths by which one can avoid intrigue <term>Cabale: a secret plot or the use of private influence and "backroom deals" to achieve political or social ends</term> and persecution.

Herwing was his own best recommendation. His fine cultivation and pleasant demeanor, his modesty among the great and his graciousness <term>Herablassung: in this historical context, this does not mean "condescension" in the modern negative sense, but rather the virtue of a superior being kind and accessible to those of lower social rank</term> toward those of lesser status, won everyone over to his side, and everyone was pleased to intercede on his behalf.

In a short time, Herwing’s father had achieved his goal and saw his son in possession of a respectable state position. <note>original: "Staatsbedienstung" — a civil service appointment or government post.</note> —

Herwing remained behind in the city, and his father returned home with the most manifold feelings of joy. — The consciousness of having satisfied his tenderness, of having compensated his son for the offense <note>original: "Bekränkung" (Kränkung), referring to the slight or injury to his honor caused by the rival Lindenholm.</note> he had suffered, and of now seeing him in a sphere of activity <term>Wirkungskreise: literally "circle of effect," referring to the professional environment where one's talents are exercised</term> where his talents and his heart would have enough room, where general respect and honor would reward his merits and pave the way for him to higher levels—this consciousness now took full possession of the good father, and would certainly have made him the same sort of fool that Lindenholm’s father had become, had his good heart and strength of mind not led him back into his proper track. Reason moderated the surges of his heart; with manly modesty <note>In this period, "manly" (männlich) described a virtue of emotional self-control and dignity.</note> he spoke of the matter among his family when he arrived home, and did not allow the slightest trace of victorious joy to be seen in him.

The news of Herwing’s good fortune at court instantly struck down Lindenholm’s triumph as if by a bolt of lightning, and even the most tragic news would not have affected his heart as powerfully as this did. For the first few days, he was unfit for any business, incapable even of reflecting on how he might cast his rival <term>Nebenbuhler: a competitor or antagonist, often in a romantic or professional context</term> down from the height he had attained over him.

To everyone else, the behavior of this young, envious man was inexplicable—except to Herwing’s father, who immediately identified the source of this behavior and was therefore extremely concerned for his son’s peace. He therefore wrote to him about the entire incident and warned him to be wary of any ambiguous step and to guard himself as cleverly as possible against all snares <term>Fallstricken: literally "falling-ropes," referring to hidden traps or plots</term>, for he knew in advance that Lindenholm would do everything in his power to bring about his downfall.

Herwing read the letter in a way that very few would have read it. He had no reason to regret any of his actions, and therefore nothing to fear. He felt no anger toward the un-

On a beautiful summer evening, which had followed a hot, sultry July day, Herwing went walking alone in his usual way, and rejoiced at the sight of benevolent nature, and admired its Creator, and his heart felt full of the urge of joy. — <note>This scene reflects the 18th-century "Sentimentalist" literary movement, where characters often found spiritual connection to the divine through solitary walks in nature.</note>

Herwing asked about the source of their misery <note>original: *Jammer*</note> and kindly offered them his help. But the poor woman remained silent, gathered her children up from the ground, and tried to go inside the door. Then the good man held her back by the arm and said: "Dear woman! ## Karl von Lindenberg
### a Tale.

**K**arl von Lindenberg was a good youth; he possessed a sensitive heart and a noble soul. <note>The German term "empfindsam" (sensitive/sentimental) refers to the 18th-century literary movement of Sentimentalism, which emphasized emotional vulnerability and empathy as signs of moral character.</note>

Goodness of heart is a gift of creation, and a sensitive soul is a blessing for humanity. How beautiful and noble is nature, how cheerful the days of spring, how pleasant the evenings after a sweltering day—how charming the quiet nights when the stars shine in the heavens, or the moon spreads its gentle rays of light! How the wanderer longs for rest after a day of toil—and what bliss cools the breast of the weary traveler when a benevolent linden tree <note>The author likely intends a play on words here between the protagonist's name, Lindenberg, and the "Linde" (linden tree), which was a traditional German symbol of community, justice, and peace.</note> takes him into its shade. Benevolence among men is a sign from nature; the happiness of our days lies within our hearts. In feeling, in compassion, there dwells a strength—and a greater strength than in balsamic potions. <note>original: "Balsamgetränken," referring to restorative medicinal cordials or tonics common in the period.</note> Lindenberg felt all of this in his heart; already early in his youth, he took an interest in the fate of the
[[margin: un-]] <note>The margin note "Un=" is a syllable catch-phrase, indicating the start of a word that continues on the next page, likely "Unglücklichen" (the unfortunate).</note>

[[vocabulary: Karl von Lindenberg, Tale, Creation, Nature, Compassion]]

<summary>This page introduces Karl von Lindenberg, a young man defined by his noble character and sensitivity. The text uses evocative descriptions of nature and the restorative power of compassion to establish the moral framework of the story.</summary>
<keywords>Karl von Lindenberg, Sentimentalism, nature, compassion, virtue, Enlightenment literature, benevolence</keywords>

[[page number: 149]]

-> ——— <-

unfortunate ones <note>original: "Unglücklichen"; this completes the sentence from the previous page's marginal note "un-"</note>, and his soul opened gladly to those who suffered, just as the night-violet <note>the *Hesperis matronalis*, a flower known for opening and releasing its fragrance at dusk</note> opens to receive the final hours of the dying day into its beneficial bosom.

In quiet regions, in desolate forests, Lindenberg wandered about alone, contemplating the fate of humanity, and tuning his heart to sensitive emotions at the song of the nightingales.

Suffering and compassion were his occupation, and yet no consuming grief was etched upon his brow. A gentle sympathy for all that was unfortunate was clearly reflected in his soulful eye.

A quiet longing for friendship and love enhanced the charms of his gentle physiognomy <term>physiognomy: the historical practice of assessing a person's character or soul from their outer appearance, especially the face</term>; his welcoming expression and his kind gaze heightened that noble sadness common to sympathetic souls, casting all the charms of sweet melancholy upon his form.

So it was for the good Lindenberg as well; he felt the void in his heart, and often wished for an object <note>meaning a person or companion to receive his affection</note> into whose friendly bosom he could pour out the innermost depths of his heart — he wished for a friend.

A friend you have wished for, poor Lindenberg! A friend! — oh, how I pity you, that your soul tortures itself with these thoughts. — Friends for a feeling heart, where are they? — Friends of pleasure, of lust, of the banquet, and of debauchery — such friends you can find in abundance, good Lindenberg, but friends such as your heart desires, where do you hope to find them? — Renounce, I pray you, these thoughts; otherwise they will disturb the peace of your days; your good heart will create an ideal for you that has no existence. The clever deceiver will trick you, will don the mask <note>original: "Masque"</note> of friendship, and then unmask himself as a monster, just when you believe you are resting in his arms in the hours of the night. — Nothing here below is so rare as friendship. — The few honest ones, to whom the Divinity has given a cap-

pable heart for this impulse, the few honest ones live lonely as if in a wasteland <note>original: *Einöde* - refers to a desert or a solitary wilderness, a common metaphor for social isolation</note>, and do not know one another because the experience of being deceived, and having been deceived, has piled up a dividing wall between them, and broke the chain through which nature once bound man to man.

Beware, dear Lindenberg, of friends, and tremble at the choice in our days. Live in solitude, do good to every human being, demand gratitude from no one, nor feeling or friendship, and you will live happily. Do not picture humans to yourself as they should be, but as they are—contemptible—ungrateful—unfeeling—and yet, love them, these contemptible, these ungrateful, these unfeeling people, because the Creator gave them to you as your brothers. Follow my advice: I have made a covenant <note>original: *Bündniß*</note> in my heart to be the friend of every person, and to desire no reciprocation of friendship from anyone; I have resolved to love humans in order to be hated by them, to do good in order to experience ingratitude, to forgive insults in order to be newly in-

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 151, completing the word "be-schimpft" (insulted). The narrator continues to mentor Lindenberg on a stoic approach to life, suggesting that virtue is its own reward regardless of the malice of others.</meta>

[[page number: 152]]

-> ——— <-

to be insulted <note>This completes the word "be-schimpft" from the previous page.</note>: such is now my way of thinking <note>original: *Denkart*</note>, and I wish to complain of the injustices of mortals to no one but Him who sees the souls of men and knows the reasons why He permits all of this.

We shall be content, Lindenberg! and even if our heart rebels, even if a tear as hot as embers flows down our cheek at the thought of suffering humanity—we can nonetheless be content that we demanded nothing from monsters except what monsters can give. If I were to pull a snake from a pit and it then killed me, that is indeed the nature of the poisonous worm—was my deed any less good, my action any less charitable? Can its poisonous bite rob me of the peace of my conscience, the conviction of my feeling? Oh no, Lindenberg!—only here in the heart is peace—only here is the reward of virtue; demand no other. I confess to you, noble mortal! that I too sometimes in the evening, like you, gaze upon the stars rolling above me, and wish to know their inhabitants, and to know whether they also resemble our brothers in this valley of the world <note>original: *Weltthal* — a poetic term for the earthly realm or the cosmos as a valley.</note>.

<summary>The narrator argues that virtue should be practiced for its own sake, using the metaphor of a snake's nature to explain that the malice of others does not diminish the goodness of one's actions.</summary>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's moral exhortations to Lindenberg. The opening word "Comforting" (Tröstend) completes the partial word "Trö-" noted in the margin of the previous page.</meta>

-> ——— <-
[[page number: 153]]

The thought of human **malice** <term>Bosheit: In this context, the speaker refers to the inherent moral failings or ill-will of others as a fact of life.</term> is not **comforting**, but it is nonetheless calming for an honest soul. — It leads us back to God, who is the only true friend of **virtue**.

If you can do good to someone, do not miss the opportunity; however, if your **fellow man** <term>Mitmensch: A term emphasizing the shared bond between all human beings.</term> tries to assure you that he will be grateful to you, do not accept that assurance in your heart. — Believe me, it could be a lie, and why would you want to make a guilty person even more guilty, and add to his **shameful deeds** <note>original: "Schandthaten"; refers to outrages or scandalous moral failures.</note> this one as well: that while he offends against what is holy in nature and humanity, he should also turn himself into a liar. —

Do the same with friendship. Be a friend to every person, and demand friendship from no one; then you will have no reason to complain against anyone. —

Certainly, good **Lindenberg**! all of this is not so easy; yet it is a **necessity**. People are simply this way; **experience** teaches us to know them.
[[margin: I]]

[[vocabulary: Malice, Virtue, Fellow man, Shameful deeds, Friendship, Necessity, Experience]]

<summary>The speaker advises Lindenberg to practice virtue and perform good deeds without expecting gratitude, as human nature is prone to deceit. By demanding nothing from others and finding peace in God, one can avoid the pain of betrayal and resentment.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, gratitude, human nature, friendship, Lindenberg, morality, God, malice</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's moral philosophical reflections addressed to a person named Lindenberg. The previous page established that human nature is flawed and that one should do good without expecting gratitude; this page expands on that by framing this disillusionment as a path toward divine likeness and purer motives.</meta>

[[page number: 154]]

-> ——— <-

I well remember a time when I did not think this way, when I always judged people to be better than they actually are; however, they clearly proved my delusion <note>original: "Betrug"; here referring to the speaker's self-deception or the mistaken belief they held about human goodness.</note> to me and healed my dim <note>original: "blöde"; in the 18th century, this meant weak or dim-sighted rather than the modern meaning of "stupid."</note> eyes; I see more clearly now. This current way of thinking of mine also leads me closer back to the Creator and distances me further from created beings; it ennobles my actions more because fewer intentions of self-interest and self-love mingle within them. Believe me, Lindenberg! Do not love people because they are lovable, but because the Divine <note>original: "Gottheit"; can be translated as Godhead or the Divine.</note> commands you to love your brothers; do good to your neighbor, not because he deserves it, but because doing good is a process of **becoming like the Divine** <term>Aehnlichwerdung: A theological concept suggesting that by performing acts of selfless goodness, a human approximates the nature of God.</term>.

Do good while renouncing any claim to gratitude; do good out of love for Him who is Love itself.

Thus I would have spoken to Lindenberg if I had known him, that noble man; yet the language of the Divine and of **Reason** <term>Vernunft: A central concept in Enlightenment philosophy, referring to the human capacity to find truth and moral law through intellect.</term> is audible to anyone who wishes to hear it.
[[margin: <margin>Lin-</margin>]]

<summary>The author explains that realizing the flaws of humanity has purified their motives, leading them to do good for the sake of divine command rather than human reward. They argue that true virtue lies in imitating the selfless love of the Creator through the use of reason.</summary>
<keywords>disillusionment, divine likeness, altruism, Lindenberg, Enlightenment morality, self-interest, reason</keywords>

Lindenberg heard them <note>Referring to the "language of the Deity and of Reason" mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> as well, and arranged his way of thinking according to these principles. He did good, and experienced the most shameful ingratitude, yet he did not grow weary of doing good. His pleasure was solitude, and his joy was the enjoyment of the bliss of nature <note>The "bliss of nature" (Wonne der Natur) is a common theme in 18th-century sentimental literature, suggesting that true happiness is found in the natural world rather than in society.</note>.

One day, late in the evening, Lindenberg was returning from the village, which he visited often to perform good deeds. An old man, who could no longer support himself through labor, was the object of Lindenberg's visit. With this man, Lindenberg spent many hours, comforting him in his misfortune. When Lindenberg, as mentioned, left the old man and returned toward the city, a rural family, who were eating their evening meal under a linden tree <note>The linden tree is a significant symbol in German culture, often representing the center of communal life and peace. It also plays on the protagonist's name, Linden-berg.</note>, captured his full attention. A man with a sun-browned face <note>A "browned face" was a sign of a laborer who worked in the sun, often used in literature of this period to denote an honest, hardworking commoner.</note> sat by the linden tree, wrapping his arm around the neck of his rural wife, and the children played with flowers in his lap; milk was in the vessel, and bread was upon the grass.

"Kind Creator!" <note>original: "Gütiger Schöpfer!"</note> he called out then, "how

a dejected soul, as if a guardian spirit <note>original: "Schutzgeist." In the 18th century, this often referred to a "genius" or a protective supernatural entity watching over an individual.</note>

Lindenberg well knew the difference
between a noble and soulful girl’s soul,

Lindenberg, he said to himself, be on your guard, lest you fall into the snares of a sentimental <term>empfindsamen: relating to "Empfindsamkeit," an 18th-century movement that prized intense emotional sensitivity</term> hussy <note>The German "Dirne" at this time could mean a young woman of low station, but Lindenberg uses it here with a tone of moral warning.</note>. — Learn to distinguish well between a good heart and a weak one, and make use of experience for your study of girls. Do you not know the wife of the honorable *Silvander*? — how magnificent is her spirit, how noble her expression, how full of feeling are many of her actions — and yet, yet she is nothing but a dreamer <note>original: "Schwärmerinn." In the 18th century, this was a derogatory term for someone—often a woman—who was prone to excessive, irrational, or "overheated" emotional and spiritual enthusiasm.</note>. She has reason, she has feeling, but her passion weakens her reason and robs her feeling of its soul. — Weakness, not goodness of heart, is her character; affectation <term>Empfindeley: a mocking term used by critics to describe performative or "fake" sentimentality</term>, not true feeling. Does a good heart forget its husband, its children, in order to stoop to a lowly love affair? Does one call debasing oneself, throwing oneself away, "feeling" or "affectation"?

O benevolence <note>original: "Menschenfreundlichkeit." While often translated as philanthropy, in this context it refers to a general kindness or openness toward others that could lead a woman into social or moral danger.</note>! How dangerous you are in the heart of a woman! Under your mask, debauchery so gladly creeps in, and kind condescension often sinks down to the level of degradation. Our foolish self-love, which is always accustomed

Lindenberg was well aware of all these dangerous principles of the fair sex <term>Frauenzimmers: historically a neutral or even polite term for a woman or women in general, though here used with a touch of moralizing wariness</term>, and he trembled at the thought of a sentimental wife.

The thought is utterly dejecting for a sensitive <note>original: "empfindsamen." The choice of a wife is more precarious today than it ever was. Where is the sincere German girl? Lindenberg pondered all of this with deep reflection and recorded many observations about German womanhood <note>original: "Frauenzimmer." As distressed as Lindenberg’s soul always was by these thoughts, the gentle impulse of nature still held too much power over his heart for him to fully resolve to renounce love. "Perhaps," he often said, "Heaven, which knows my heart, will guide my feelings; it will bring me to know the girl who is meant to make me happy." With these thoughts he often wandered about in solitude, communing with the ideal of a girl to whom his vivid imagination <term>Einbildungskraft: the creative power of the mind to form mental images or ideals</term> gave existence.

## II.
## Continuation.
# *Karl von Lindenberg*
### a tale.

# **O**n a sweltering summer day, just as
Lindenberg was sitting under the tree
once more, dark clouds gathered in the
sky; a heavy rain surprised the
good youth, <note>The term "good youth" (guter Jüngling) is typical of the era's literature, emphasizing the protagonist's moral purity and sensitive nature.</note> who was now forced to
wait for a better hour to make
his return.

In the distance, fiery lightning snaked
through the clouds; Lindenberg watched this
magnificent spectacle of nature, and wor-
shipped God’s omnipotence in its majesty.
<note>This scene reflects the "Sublime"—an aesthetic concept popular in the 18th and 19th centuries where the power and scale of nature inspire both awe and religious devotion.</note>
The thunderstorm grew more violent, the rain in-
creased; a small goat-herd also took refuge
under the sheltering tree, and Lindenberg
soon made the boy’s acquaintance.

"Good lad!" Lindenberg began, "your
clothes are as soaked through as if one had

**Shepherd.** <note>original: "Hirt"</note> Dear Sir! Those days have passed; the dear God has now sent me clothing and linens, shoes and stockings; look here, my dear Sir! — I have three more such coats at home in my chest <note>original: "Kasten." **Lindenberg.** Who was then so loving, good boy! and had all this made for you?

**Shepherd.** I will tell you that: My parents <note>original: "Äeltern," an archaic spelling of "Eltern."</note> have been dead for a long time — and I have no friend or blood relative left, at least I know of none;

I was certainly much happier than before, yet the three guilders <term>Gulden: A currency used throughout German-speaking lands during this period; three guilders represented a very meager annual wage for a servant.</term> in wages that he gave me were not enough to provide me with any clothing—day and night I was under the open sky—I was covered in filth and vermin, and in the harsh winter I had to be glad if my master allowed me to sleep on the straw in the goat-stable <note>Original: *Geisstall*.</note> with my goats—but dear Sir! As sad as my fate always was, I never grumbled against my dear God, and thought, things will surely get better, and if they do not get better, Martin!—so I said to myself—then the dear Lord God will surely take you to Him in Heaven one day.

When I was in the field and saw the birds with their beautiful feathers, the woolly sheep, and my dappled goats, I would sometimes sing a song like the one I am about to sing for you. Here Martin sang:

> Dear Lord God! You who clothe
> Every bird so well and fair,
> You who lead my goats to graze,
> Hurry also to assist me.

> See, I am a poor boy,
> I have done nothing to harm You,
> Because I have no human to turn to,
> Take me, Father! as Your own.

So I sang—and as I sang so, one tear followed another <note>Original: *schlug eine Zähre die andere*. The archaic term *Zähre* for "tear" emphasizes the poetic and sentimental tone of the period.</note>—I often wept bitterly, and yet something always comforted me, and whispered in my ears: "Be comforted, Martin! God will care for you," and He has indeed cared for me.

It was on one of the most beautiful days of May of the year, when I drove my goats to the pasture early in the morning, and happily—delighted by the sight of the magnificent morning—sang my little song to the Creator, and resolved in my heart to truly enjoy this beautiful day, so that I felt quite grand <note>Martin describes a rare moment of dignity and self-worth (*mich groß dünkte*), momentarily forgetting his social status.</note> and forgot my misery: then I sat down under a shady hazel

shrub <note>original: "sträuch." This completes the word "Haselgesträuch" (hazel shrub) from the previous page.</note> by the stream, at whose friendly murmuring I have often fallen asleep when weary with sorrow, and was so happy that I was always sorry when I woke up again.

And everything all around me, as far as I could see and hear, was in good spirits and rejoiced in the beautiful day. The birds sang quite wonderfully and sweetly together, and never grew tired of singing, hopping from branch to branch, from tree to tree, and were so friendly and so kind to one another—the linnet and the finch, the titmouse <note>original: "Maise," a variant of the modern "Meise."</note> and the robin—that I took my heartfelt joy in them. ### Lindenberg. 
You are a truly good-hearted boy, dear Martin! How few in your position would think this way — how few even with far better fortune! — But you were going to tell me —

### Shepherd. 
I see clearly; I am making it too long for you, dear sir! But forgive me, I cannot stop myself once I start talking about it; it is also the best thing I have, my only happiness, all my joy. Now, just have a little bit of patience, I will make it brief.

So, more than half of this beautiful day had already vanished for me, and in my thoughts I was still so far behind it that I still had its morning in my head, when the little clouds, which were scattered here and there across the sky, drew closer and closer together until they finally all together made up one large, dark cloud that hid the sun from the entire region.

It was not long before everything was night, and it flashed and thundered so terribly that I truly began to be afraid. — My God! I thought to myself, surely I am not in the world just to have joy, and the thought made me sad, so that I wept and became completely weary of my condition.

I strove as much as possible to get under cover with my goats; but suddenly the rain overtook me, and so powerfully — oh, it was — as if they had overturned all the fire-buckets <term>Feuer-Eymer: historical firefighting buckets, usually made of leather or canvas, used in bucket brigades; here Martin uses it as a metaphor for a torrential downpour</term> up above — I could hardly catch my breath anymore; and that

I would have liked to continue on, but then it hailed <term>hailstones<gloss>original: "Schlossen," an archaic German term for large, heavy hail</gloss></term> as large as hazelnuts. I kept feeling my head, for it pained me as if I had run it against a wall. In a short time, the ground was covered a foot deep with hail, and it froze so that I believed the very embodiment of Winter was there. With not a single healthy spot on my whole body, soaking wet, and as frozen as if I had lain on the ice overnight, I wept and wailed incessantly until I reached the village; my goats must have been in better spirits than I, for I no longer paid them any mind, and yet I did not lose a single one of them.

In this "fine attire" <note>Martin is speaking ironically here; he is actually soaking wet, bruised, and dressed in rags.</note> I saw a girl standing under the threshold of her house, also shaking the rain and hail off herself. She was well-dressed and could not have suffered much from the weather. I looked at her, and now it felt doubly painful to me that I was so poor. — The good child had pity on me when she saw me so tattered and so badly battered by the weather, and she said: "Oh, you poor boy! How you look! Do make [[margin: haste,]]" <note>The margin contains the end of the girl's sentence, "geschwind," meaning quickly or with haste.</note>

**Shepherd.** That you shall, my Lord! and you will find joy in it. But just let the rain pass by first; meanwhile, I will tell you how it continued. The best part is yet to come. — She really came to me the next morning — it was a Sunday — and brought me, wrapped in her apron, two more coats <note>original: "zween Röcke"; in this period, "Röcke" could refer to long coats or tunics for men/boys as well as skirts for women. Given the context of page 171, these are likely her brother's clothes.</note> and many other pieces of clothing, and I was so heartened by them that I believed I would never need anything else in the world for the rest of my life. From her pocket she pulled out cake and a large piece of white bread <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, refined white bread was a luxury often reserved for the wealthy or for special occasions, unlike the dense, dark rye bread common to the working class.</note>, such as I had never seen in all my born days; she sat down beside me and said very kindly: "Now, enjoy this, and be of good cheer; things will surely get better in time. I do not yet know your name; what are you called, then?" —

### Lindenberg.
Indeed, there are few such noble people. But I would like to see them, Martin! The rain will no longer do us much harm; besides, it is already late in the day. It is a rare happiness to see good people, and the delight of a philanthropist <note>original: "Menschenfreundes" — literally a "friend of humanity," referring to the Enlightenment ideal of universal love for one's fellow man.</note> to know them. Come!

# Continuation.

## **Karl von Lindenberg**

### a tale.

**I** found that she might well be right, and I resolved so firmly never to sleep again that I would have staked my head on it <note>original: "meinen Kopf daran gesetzt," an idiom meaning to be absolutely certain or to risk everything on a resolution</note>, but—there, I have already often overlooked it. How I startle then, when I wake up and see her before me—Ha! an ice-cold shiver runs over my whole body; I would not be so afraid if my master, the farmer, saw me, with whom it would not pass without a "schock" of blows <term>Schock Prügel: a "Schock" was an old German unit of measurement meaning sixty; Martin is implying he would receive a very long and severe beating</term>, but I fear more to offend the good girl through my laziness, to make her angry; and that would hurt me much more than blows and scoldings <note>original: "Auszanken," a thorough dressing-down or verbal reprimand</note>.

**Lindenberg.** Good Martin! I did not think that a person like you could be so happy. But now you are quite satisfied with your station in life? <note>original: "Stande," referring to one's social class or position in the world</note>

| :--- | :--- |

**Shepherd.** Oh yes, I am—am so with all my heart. Now it may storm, it may rain, hail, snow, and freeze stick and stone together; I have clothing in abundance <note>original: "die Hülle und die Fülle," an idiom meaning more than enough</note>, and in winter I no longer have to sleep in the goat stall <note>original: "Geisstal"</note>, for the parents of the good girl have also promised me to take me into their house, and there I will receive a bed and a warm room. Now I truly wish that it were already winter.

**Lindenberg.** Happy boy! To be so happy with so little!—so entirely innocent, such entirely unspoiled nature. Lindenberg!—You will never again be as happy as a shepherd boy, for you are already outside of his circle; you feel too many needs <term>Bedürfnisse: in this context, Lindenberg refers to the artificial desires and requirements created by high society</term> that surround you, and their satisfaction—alas! they were surely devised for man's torment <note>original: "Pein"</note>, these needs!—

**Shepherd.** Hm! What did you say just now? <note>The shepherd uses the dialect word "ißt" for "jetzt" (now).</note>

**Lindenberg.** You heard it, didn't you?

**Shepherd.** Heard! I heard speaking, to be sure, but what—

**Lindenberg.** Better luck for you that you do not yet understand this language <note>original: "Sprache"; here used metaphorically to mean the language of social discontent, envy, and the awareness of one's own poverty compared to others</note>. Oh, that I did not understand it either!

**Shepherd.** Is it then bad, if one understands it?

**Lindenberg.** Have you never heard your farmer complain that things are not going well for him? Have you never seen him weep, and heard him wish that he were as well off as his wealthier neighbor <term>Nachbar: neighbor</term>; that he could live as well as his masters <term>Herrschaft: the nobility or employers of the farmer</term>, or something of the sort?

**Shepherd.** Very often; and yet I rarely see him at home. --- Page 190 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the dialogue from page 180, where Lindenberg explains the psychological root of the farmer's discontent. He argues that wealth does not bring happiness if it only increases one's awareness of unmet desires.</meta>

-> ————— <-

[[page number: 181]]

**Lindenberg.** Now listen! With what you currently have, you are secure, and because you do not know of anything more, you do not desire anything more. But your farmer, who has to look after his household <term>Hauswesen: the management of a home, farm, and staff</term> and his people, knows of more things that promote our comfort <term>Bequemlichkeit: physical ease or luxury</term> and desires <term>Begierden: strong cravings or passions</term> than you do; and because he does not have the means to provide for himself what could make him satisfied, he is therefore just as dissatisfied, and almost as unhappy, as you were before you had clothing and the hope that things would soon go better for you. Now you will likely see that people who have and know more needs <term>Bedürfnisse: the things one feels are necessary for life</term> than they can satisfy are much worse off than you, who have very few, and those are satisfied, and beyond these you know no others.

**Shepherd.** I think so — I mean, I would like to grasp it; but I do not quite understand it yet.

**Lindenberg.** Oh, may the time never come when you will learn to see it only too clearly! You will be sent to a school where you will be read many a lesson <term>Lektion: a formal teaching or a moral lecture</term> on this chapter. Things that in nature do not e-
-> ven <- <note>The catchword at the bottom indicates the next page begins with "even" (German: "mal"), completing the phrase "not even".</note>

[[vocabulary: household management, comfort, desires, needs, lesson]]

<summary>Lindenberg explains the paradox of desire, arguing that the Shepherd is happier than the farmer because his needs are few and fully met, whereas the farmer is plagued by "needs" he cannot satisfy.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of happiness, desire, social class, contentment, education, pastoral dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page completes the sentence from page 181, where Lindenberg was explaining how the "school of the world" teaches people to desire things that do not exist in nature.</meta>

[[page number: 182]]

-> ————— <-

even have a name—have a monstrous existence <term>Unwesen: literally "non-being" or "disorder"; here Lindenberg refers to artificial, unnatural habits or concepts that haunt society</term>, have been made indispensable to people in society. To possess these, a thousand daily wishes fill the heart of the vain mortal, for which he sacrifices his peace; and once he has them, he is unhappier than before. This is because his vanity and greed always leave some wishes remaining, because ever-new needs surround him, and new trifles <term>Tändeleien: frivolous toys, social games, or superficial ornaments</term> stir his desire.

Even the wise man does not escape this misery. He must concern himself with things his heart does not want—things it even despises—because he cannot live in society without also being forced to be a slave to convention <term>Konvention: the unwritten social rules, etiquette, and expectations of "polite" society</term>. He is bound to circumstances from which he cannot possibly tear himself away without making himself, his family, and everything dear and precious to him unhappy.

Amidst this talk, Lindenberg walked on for quite a distance. Martin looked him in the face from time to time, gawking at him from head to toe, and could not for the life of him figure out what to make of him.

[[page number: 183]]

When they had come quite close, Martin spotted the girl being led by the hand by her older sister; Martin left Lindenberg and the goats behind and hurried toward them.

"Look," Martin said to the girl, "this gentleman is very eager to see you. I have told him how good you are, and how things have gone with me since then, and he took great pleasure in it. He seems to me to be a very good, kind gentleman; you will come closer to him, won't you?" And with this, Martin led them along the path while Lindenberg himself, guided by an impulse <note>original: "Trieb," suggesting a natural, instinctive urge rather than a conscious choice</note> unknown to him, came toward them. Lindenberg was now surprised by a sight for which he was not prepared. He had imagined the good, affectionate girl of whom the shepherd boy <note>referring to Martin</note> had told him so many beautiful things, whom he had already in his thoughts kissed and pressed to his heart; now, with the most heartfelt longing, he hurried toward this joy to be able to actually do so—to be able to tell her, amidst a thousand blessings, how much he wished for the happiness of such a daughter for himself, and more of what his heart felt,
-> what <-

[[page number: 184]]

what his noble spirit told him. — But all these thoughts, all the feelings that he, moved by Martin’s story, had gathered in his heart, were extinguished by a single sight. Lindenberg stood motionless and speechless there, when he saw the good girl’s sister, Rosine, standing before him.

He stared at her, and devoured her with his eyes. Scarcely did enough presence of mind tear him out of his soul-faintness <term>soul-faintness: (Seelenohnmacht) a 18th-century psychological term for a state of being so emotionally overwhelmed that one becomes dazed or paralyzed</term> that he could notice his own behavior and realize the impropriety <note>original: "Unschicklichkeit," suggesting he realizes his intense staring is socially inappropriate</note> of his situation.

He then turned to the little girl, took her hand, and told her—though with great effort—that Martin’s story had stirred in him the irresistible desire to personally meet such a noble girl, whose early virtue already seems to promise so much for her whole life. But everything he said to her was no longer the direct stream of his feelings; it no longer possessed strength or fire. It was no longer the enthusiasm into which he usually fell when enchanted by the image of a noble soul.

**"Sir,"** the little one <note>Referring to the younger sister, whom Martin had previously praised for her character.</note> finally interrupted him with great modesty, "Martin may well have exaggerated here and there. Because of the small favors my parents showed him, he loves them and all of us more than his own life; he has a truly good heart, and that easily carries him away into flattery."

**Lindenberg.** "No, my dear! Good Martin has perhaps said too little. Your modesty just now gave me new proof of it." —

**The Girl.** "I beg you, you do me too much honor — I have often heard it said that one must not allow praise to be spoken to one's face, for that would be vanity; and I truly find it so myself. If I am indeed no worse than others, and truly deserve a portion of your praises, then I owe it all again to my good, dear sister, who taught me to do good, who tells me when I fail or am naughty, and who shows me the beauty of virtue <term>Tugend: a central Enlightenment concept referring to moral excellence and the conscious practice of doing good</term>."

Lindenberg was once again, and more strongly than before, carried away <note>The German *hingerissen* suggests being overcome by emotion or enchantment.</note>.

The Creator had formed Rosine as the most excellent masterpiece of His art. In every part of her body reigned the most perfect symmetry <term>Ebenmaaß: an Enlightenment aesthetic ideal referring to harmony, proportion, and balance as a reflection of inner goodness</term>; everywhere there was an uninterrupted proportion. All the features of her face were mild, not deeply etched, and flowed gently into one another. Her gait and posture were straight, full of noble decorum. Visible upon her face sat the dignity of the noblest, purest virtue. Her appearance was solemn—majestic, yet not proud; the sublime <term>das Erhabene: a philosophical concept popular in the 18th century, referring to a greatness that inspires awe and surpasses all imitation</term> was mixed with an inimitable grace. Her eyes spoke—not boldly—not with vain wickedness—but gently and kindly. One had to approach her with reverence, and with admiration one found graciousness <note>original: "Herablassung"; in this period, it meant "affability" or the kindliness shown by a superior person to others, rather than the modern negative sense of "condescension."</note> and confidence hovering upon her lips. In her lay the highest study of female beauty; in her soul, the highest beauty of human virtue. Any description of her can do no more than sketch a shapeless silhouette <term>Schattenriß: a silhouette or shadow-portrait, a popular 18th-century method of capturing a person's essence through their profile</term> of what Rosine truly was.

Such was the appearance of the sculptress who shaped her little sister's heart, and made her into the best, noblest girl, who one day [would be]

**Lindenberg.** I do not understand—how so?

**Father.** You are a young gentleman whom the world has not yet given much to worry about; but just put yourself in my position one day— In these times, even the best girls are left behind <note>original: *sitzen bleiben*, an idiom meaning to remain unmarried or become a "spinster."</note> if they have beauty, intellect, virtue, everything, everything—except money; and that is exactly the catch <note>original: *Hacken* (modern: Haken), meaning the snag or obstacle.</note> with my daughters as well. …able friends, and is an honest, brave <note>original: *biederer, wackerer*, terms describing a man of traditional integrity and solid character</note> man. He will be my son-in-law, and with that, we are all helped.

It struck Lindenberg's heart like the point of a dagger and left a deep wound behind; yet he comforted himself with the thought that Rosine would be happy, for whom he was willing to sacrifice everything—both his happiness and his peace of mind.

He went with Rosine and her sister into the garden, which they had asked him to view. Lindenberg strolled through several paths without a single word escaping his lips. He looked now at Rosine with a constricted <note>original: *beklommenen*, describing a gaze full of anxiety or a heavy heart</note> gaze, now at the earth and the sky, and remained silent. Unfeeling and without offering praise, he passed by the most beautiful flowerbeds. He was in the garden and yet did not see it; even Eden itself would have made no impression on his senses. The flowers, which Rosine’s little sister had tied into a neat bouquet and offered to him, were accepted with indifference and not even graced with a single glance. Finally, Rosine broke the silence. "Mr. von Lindenberg," she said, "you do not like it here in the garden."

**Lindenberg.** Oh yes, very much so.

**Lindenberg.** I enjoy it quite well.

**Rosine.** And yet, something here displeases you. You find very little pleasure <term>Vergnügen: In the 18th century, this term often referred to a refined, moral, and intellectual enjoyment of life and nature.</term> with us; also, the day today is not at all pleasant.

**Rosine.** Whom I cannot love. A coarse, impetuous blusterer <term>Polterer: a person who is habitually loud, noisy, and prone to outbursts</term>, a widower and father of two degenerate, malicious boys, from whom I can expect only the lowest treatment.

speak! Oh, how beautiful are these wild roses, the children of your love—how busy they are, through lavish scents, to give us bliss <term>Wonne|A word denoting intense delight or spiritual ecstasy, frequent in the literature of "Sensibility" to describe a soul's reaction to nature.</term> and joy!

To these kind flowers here, I will entrust it: that I love Rosine, that I love her more than my life—holy shall this place be to me from this hour forward; and if Rosine spends her days in the arms of another, Lindenberg will pray to the Godhead here daily for happiness and bliss upon Rosine’s head. And by the time winter strips this bush of its leaves, perhaps Lindenberg will also be no more—for if, in a future spring, Rosine should glide across this region and see my burial mound <note>original: "Grabhügel," referring to a simple grave mound, a common image in the era's "graveyard poetry" which focused on mortality and mourning.</note>—and you, good rosebush—you who shade my ashes—you shall also be dear to her.

Thus Lindenberg raved <note>The German verb "schwärmen" used here conveys an ecstatic, romanticized outpouring of emotion typical of the "Sturm und Drang" movement.</note> daily, and the intensity of his passion stole the roses from his youthful cheeks; one saw the melancholy <term>Schwermuth|A state of deep, soulful sadness or depression, often regarded in this period as a sign of a refined and sensitive nature.</term> in his eye, and his fading health made him similar to the flower that slowly withers and dies.

Rosine was the first to be concerned about Lindenberg’s circumstances; the girl loved the youth, and an equal grief <term>Gram|A deep, consuming sorrow or affliction of the soul, a central theme in 18th-century sentimental literature.</term> consumed her tender soul.

Since their last conversation, Karl and Rosine spoke to each other very rarely; they stood there speechless when they encountered one another, looked at each other, and wept. Soon, there was no one in the area who did not notice the killing sorrow that consumed the loving girl; only Rosine’s father, old Blumeneck, and Squire <term>Junker|A title for a young nobleman or landed member of the gentry, often used here to denote a suitor of higher social standing than the hero.</term> Velten, her intended bridegroom, did not notice it.

The creature he seemed to love most was Pandur, a large poodle <note>original: "Budel," an archaic spelling of "Pudel."</note>. Blumeneck and Pandur now hunted bravely about during most of the seasons. But it happened, unfortunately, that Pandur tracked down a large wild boar <note>original: "wildes Schwein."</note>. Meanwhile, poor Pandur recovered somewhat from his loss of blood, stood up, and tried to follow his master on trembling legs; but the poor dog was so exhausted that he could go no further than Nobleman Velten’s <note>original: "Junker Velten." Nobleman Velten recognized the dog of his future father-in-law at once; but instead of welcoming him, he drove the poor animal out of his castle with a beating, threw stones after him, and—as he put it—did not want this "piece of carrion" <note>original: "Schinderaaß," a derogatory term referring to an animal fit only for the knacker's yard.</note> to die <note>original: "krepiren," a blunt, often disrespectful term for an animal dying.</note> on his doorstep. Thus Lindenberg prayed, and where is the lover in our times who prays as he does? Piety and virtue were merely actions of a bygone age <term>bygone age (Vorwelt): literally the "pre-world," referring here to a lost, more moral era of ancestors</term>; today, people are ashamed of virtue and take pride in vice. Moral purity is rarely the ornament of the young man anymore, and virtue is no longer the maiden’s dowry. Ulterior motives and money are the suitor’s incentives; most marriages are marriages of convenience <note>original: "par Raison" — French for "by reason," referring to marriages arranged for social or financial gain rather than love</note>, marriages in which the heart plays no part. Lindenberg, unfortunately, knew this—but in his way of thinking, he transported himself back a century. He wished to choose a maiden just as the "old German" of the past—with his pointed hat, his handlebar mustache <note>original: "Knebelbart" — a traditional styled mustache or goatee</note>, and his laced shoes <note>original: "Bundschuhen" — traditional footwear made of a single piece of leather tied with thongs, often associated with the common folk or peasants of earlier centuries</note>—would have chosen. "It is better," he said, "to follow the customs of the times when a man was still a man, when a handshake meant friendship and a man's word was his bond <note>original: "das Wort die Sache bestimmte" — literally "the word determined the matter"</note>." It is better, he said, to follow those customs than the customs of these times, in which virtue has so little value.

-> To be continued. <-

# 14.

## Continuation and Conclusion.

# Karl von Lindenberg
### a story.

**S**o Lindenberg told himself, and walked slowly through the large grove, until he reached the hazelnut bush <note>original: *Nußstrauche*</note>, where the dying poodle lay.

There, the whimpering of the suffering animal disturbed his thoughts; he looked around and saw the poor poodle <note>original: *Budel*, an older spelling of *Pudel*. In this era, poodles were often used as working or hunting dogs</note> lying in blood. Compassion took possession of his soul; he bent down under the hazelnut bush, pulled his handkerchief from his pocket, and bandaged the wound of poor Pandur <note>The dog's name, "Pandur," refers to a class of fierce frontier soldiers in the Austrian army, a popular name for a brave or spirited dog at the time</note>, then he took the wounded animal into his arms and hurried with it toward his dwelling.

"The good man is also good to his beast," says the excellent Mr. Abbot <note>likely referring to Thomas Abbt (1738–1766), a prominent German philosopher of the Enlightenment who wrote extensively on civic virtue and the "goodness of the heart"</note> in his book on the *Goodness of the Heart*. Nature, he continues, must, for every well-organized human <note>in the 18th-century sense, a "well-organized" person was someone whose physical constitution and moral spirit were refined and in harmony</note>, [cause a reaction] to the convulsions of a

| D | dy- |

<summary>Lindenberg discovers a wounded dog named Pandur in the woods and, moved by pity, tends to its wounds and carries it home. The narrator uses this act of mercy to cite Enlightenment philosophy regarding the inherent compassion a virtuous person feels toward suffering animals.</summary>
<keywords>Karl von Lindenberg, compassion, animal welfare, Pandur (dog), Thomas Abbt, Enlightenment virtue, goodness of heart</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the philosophical reflection on the wounded dog Pandur from page 209. The previous page ended mid-word with "dy-", which is completed here as "dying." The text cites the philosophy of Thomas Abbt regarding the connection between animal welfare and human morality.</meta>

[[page number: 210]]

-> ————— <-

dying animal sensitive <note>The text continues from the previous page: "Nature, he continues, must, for every well-organized human, [cause a reaction] to the convulsions of a dying animal..."</note>, because our **nervous system** presents the similarity to us only too clearly.

Kind nature has strung the **chords of pain** <note>original: *Saiten des Schmerzens*. This musical metaphor was central to the 18th-century "Age of Sensibility," suggesting the human soul is like a stringed instrument that vibrates in sympathy with the emotions of others.</note> within the human soul, and when such a chord is touched in another creature, the human’s own must necessarily vibrate. Woe to us if we stifle this feeling! Woe to the suffering human! —

He who can torture an animal in cold blood has **cruelty** in his soul. — Nature does not mock us; this sign is unmistakable — the tenderness which nature has given us for the suffering of animals, for their final **death-throes** <term>Todeszuckungen: the final involuntary muscle spasms at the moment of death</term>, is a true hallmark of a sensitive heart. Woe to humanity from that mortal to whom nature has granted this disposition more sparingly, or whom upbringing <note>original: *Erziehung*. In this context, it refers to the moral and emotional cultivation of a young person's character, not just formal schooling.</note> has hardened! — What can the unfortunate soul hope for from a person for whom the **death rattle** <term>Todesröcheln: the gasping sound produced by someone near death</term> is a pleasure, and witnessing death is a joy? — After all, a person in suffering can be nothing

to whimper, writhe, and groan when his soul is in misery and his life in torment; and if any creature can ever accustom itself to the whimpering of a suffering being, oh, then this being itself should cease to be a <term>being</term> <note>original: "Wesen"; here meaning a sentient or existing entity within the moral order of the world</note>, so as not to bring a curse upon <term>Creation</term> <note>original: "Schöpfung"; a common Enlightenment-era reference to the natural world as a divine or ordered system</note>. —

Ah! Do not be ashamed, O mankind! To have mercy for suffering animals—this noble feeling does not dishonor you. The righteous man, says the <term>Scripture</term> <note>A reference to Proverbs 12:10: "A righteous man regardeth the life of his beast: but the tender mercies of the wicked are cruel."</note>, takes care even of the life of his beast; but the heart of the godless is without mercy.

On the black tin plate that the beadle <term>Büttel (a minor court official or bailiff)</term> fastened to his chest, these words appeared in large white letters:

# -> Bloodthirsty Monster! <-
<note>original: "Blutdürſtiger Unmenſch!"</note>

With a braided wire whip, a second beadle then dealt him 25 lashes, after which the first beadle read the following aloud:

> "Here, young monster! Feel just a portion of the pain with which you tortured your fellow creature <term>Mitgeſchöpf (literally 'co-creature')</term> in its hour of death; and if you ever wish to plead for mercy from the Lord of all creatures in your own final hour, then become more humane." Then the second beadle gave him the remaining 25 lashes, regardless of all the whimpering through which the monster sought to beg for mercy from his judges at the town hall <term>Rathhauſe</term>.

This punishment for inhumanity was carried out at just the right time to put an end to the increasingly frequent cruelties against poor animals. For during the summer, it was the custom of Finnish boys to [take] young sparrows alive—

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 213, completing the description of common cruelties practiced by Finnish boys and transitioning into a moral and theological reflection on the proper treatment of animals.</meta>

[[page number: 214]]

to nail [them], and then to shoot at them with blowguns; and from then on, this practice ceased.

In all the hours of their lessons, the boys of Finland had never so vividly felt the divine truth that whoever feels compassion shall receive mercy from Him who has mercy on all; and they seemed never to have known that it is a mark of a true worshiper of God <term>Gottesverehrer</term> to have mercy even upon animals.

Only insofar as we need animals for our food and sustenance, and insofar as our self-preservation <term>Selbſterhaltung</term> requires their reduction against their harmful overgrowth, is man permitted to kill them: by no means do God and Nature grant us the right to torture them, to torment them, and to destroy them out of wantonness.

It remains always a loathsome barbarity <term>Barbarey</term> to burn out the eyes of an eagle-owl <term>Uhu</term>, or to pluck a poor hawk <term>Habicht</term> alive, to "make a mill" <note>This refers to a cruel historical pastime where children would pin a beetle, often a cockchafer, so that its fluttering wings resembled a spinning windmill</note> with a beetle, or a—

| | —n |
<note>The word "—n" is a catchword, likely the suffix for "einer" (a/an) continuing on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a story about the reform of Finnish youths' behavior toward animals, arguing that while humans may kill for food or safety, any unnecessary cruelty is a violation of divine and natural law.</summary>
<keywords>animal welfare, Finland, moral education, theology, hunting ethics, 18th-century ethics, animal cruelty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of cruel acts from page 214, specifically describing the torture of insects and cats. The text transitions from criticizing animal cruelty to cautioning against excessive sentimentality that neglects human suffering, arguing for a balanced "sensibility."</meta>

[[page number: 215]]

-> ————— <-

...to tear all the legs off a gnat <note>original: "einer Mücke"</note> and let it fly again, to nail a cat to a board and leave it to the mercy of the floods, or even to hang bladders around a poor tomcat <term>Murner: a traditional name for a cat in German folklore and fables</term> and let him fly from a tower. These barbarities against animals dishonor humanity, degrade our character <term>verwildern: literally "to grow wild" or "to brutalize"</term>, and turn us into monsters <term>Unmenschen: literally "non-humans" or "inhumane beings"</term>.

Such cruel amusements are always the beginning of greater cruelties; first, a mischievous boy tortures a gnat, then a dog, finally his neighbor’s child, and ultimately, if he ever attains a position in the state, he torments and tortures all those who stand beneath him.

Yet the sensitivity <term>Empfindlichkeit: in the 18th-century sense of "sensibility" or "compassion"</term> that man owes even to the animal must not overstep its bounds—the feeling for the animal must not stifle the feeling for humanity. It is an injustice against mankind to feed dogs with chicken meat while poor fellow humans stand before our doors with nothing to gnaw on. It is an injustice to drive a poor girl out of service if she, unfortunately...

...to have stepped on the paw of the lady's lapdog. It is an injustice to forget brotherly love and to sacrifice the love of mankind for a foolish animal.

Our Lindenberg knew all of this; he loved humanity, and since he also loved animals, this love did not lead him to forget the former—the duties of humanity and religion.

We left our Lindenberg as he carried the wounded poodle <note>original: *Budel*, an archaic spelling of *Pudel*</note> home. While we were moralizing here, Lindenberg was already applying every care to restore the sick animal to health. A woolen blanket was prepared for his bed; Pandur's <note>The dog's name, likely referring to the historical "Pandur" soldiers, known for being fierce and loyal</note> wound was washed with wine and vinegar <note>A common historical antiseptic treatment for wounds</note>, and Lindenberg spared no expense to restore poor Pandur.

"You are the faithful friend of a man," said Lindenberg, "who is unspeakably dear to me; he is the father of the best of girls! He is the father of my Rosine, dear Pandur! Recover soon—for then I will bring you back to your master, and heartfelt joy shall..."

Three full weeks passed, and Blumeneck was still inconsolable over the loss of his Pandur. "Ah!" he often said to his daughter, "if the wild boar <note>This suggests Pandur was a hunting dog who saved Blumeneck during a hunt</note> had completely slaughtered my Pandur, then I would not grieve so; but as it is—that the poor animal, I know not where, without help, without support, and still pursued by the worst of men, has perhaps ended his life in the most painful way—that is what pains me. Oh, poor Pandur! You saved my life; oh, how gladly I would have returned <note>original: *erwiedrigt*, an archaic form of *erwidert*</note> your love! Nature gave you your clothing <note>A poetic reference to the dog's fur</note>; were the few bones I gave you a reward for your faithful services?" Thus Blumeneck lamented, and Rosine shared in his pain.

Blumeneck and Rosine approached the linden tree <note>The linden tree often symbolizes the heart of a village or a place of peace in German culture.</note>, and the goat-herd was just then driving his goats toward the village; he was playing on a shawm <term>shawm: a medieval and Renaissance woodwind instrument, a precursor to the modern oboe</term> and singing a song.

"Let us," said Blumeneck, "hide ourselves beneath this bush, and as the herder drives the goats past, we shall listen to what he is singing."

Rosine and her father were already hidden, and the herder moved past with his goats, singing this song:

> The garment that I wear upon my frame,
> Is a gift given in Rosine's name,

After three days the wedding took place, and Blumeneck praised this day as the happiest of his life.

Lindenberg showed every imaginable care for his father-in-law, and all his love for Rosine. The blessing of the Deity was upon Lindenberg’s house, confirming the truth of the proverb of wisdom:

The righteous man cares even for the life of his beast; but the heart of the wicked is without mercy. <note>A translation of the biblical proverb (Proverbs 12:10), which serves as the moral of the story: kindness toward animals is a sign of a virtuous soul.</note>

-> 15. <-

# -> Herman. <-

-> A Story from the Times of Knighthood. <note>original: "Ritterſchaft" – This refers to the era of chivalry or the social class of knights.</note> <-

**D**eeply silent lay nature in nocturnal
stillness; the firmament was shrouded thick in clouds,
and in raven-garb, the mournful hour of gloomy
midnight approached majestically and earnestly. Solemnly
the mute landscape trembled, and a holy
dread quivered on every leaf; the roaring valley
was covered with darkness, and nocturnal
horror pitched its dark tent to
rule unrestrictedly over the hemisphere.

A deep silence, like the stillness of
death, spread around, and besides the
owl’s plaintive song, the mortal heard no
voice.

On such a night, Herman wandered through
unknown paths: his only companion was
his sword, for with this he paved
a path for his staggering steps.

[[vocabulary: Herman, Knighthood, Nature, Midnight, Valley, Owl, Sword]]

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a new Gothic-style story about a knight named Herman, opening with a dramatic and atmospheric description of a dark, silent midnight journey.</summary>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 225: Herman continues his midnight journey through a dark, Gothic landscape, stumbling upon a ruined castle and a scene of macabre horror.</meta>

[[page number: 226]]

A half-dilapidated castle, which in ancient times had served some for malice and others for protection, rose up in the region where Herman had lost his way. Amidst the rubble, moss-covered vaults rose up, where in the twilight hours the mournful night-bird <note>original: "Nachtvogel" (night-bird), likely referring to an owl</note> made its roost and built its desolate home. Lizards and toads crept along the walls, and adders hissed dreadfully at the traveler. Ivy rustled against the crumbling walls and wound its way up the overhanging towers, which, overgrown with grass, loomed precariously over black cliffs. In this terrible place, where paths rarely guided a mortal—in this haunt of wild beasts and venomous creatures, which even a tiger <note>The author uses a tiger as a symbol of ultimate predatory ferocity to emphasize the extreme danger of the setting.</note> would not dare to approach—there Herman wandered in the hours of midnight. Skulls and the bones of murdered men obstructed the traveler’s steps, and skeletons still hanging in iron chains <note>likely a reference to gibbets, where the bodies of executed criminals were displayed in cages</note> spread a sense of horror throughout this fearsome place. Fear would have surely killed him, had the merciful night not veiled these scenes of horror from his trembling gaze.

Here was Herman — exhausted, the hero who had fought bravely in Palestine <note>A reference to the Crusades, establishing Herman as a knight returning from the Holy Land.</note> sank down against a rock wall, and exhaustion and sleep robbed him of his senses.

The morning was already dawning, and owls and bats were returning to their caves before the rising sun. With a ghastly noise, they fluttered over Herman’s head and woke him from his slumber with the rushing of their wings.

Herman awoke, and how his astonished eye was struck with horror when he saw a large snake before him, staring fixedly into his face and flicking its tongue. He searched for his sword; it lay near him, but three fearsome adders <note>original: "Nattern" (vipers or adders), common symbols of treachery and danger in 18th-century literature</note> entwined it as their prey and contested the hero’s claim to it. Cold sweat poured through his limbs; anxiously he cast his timid glances around him, and from every side, dread and terror rushed to meet him. There he saw the bottomless heights he had traversed; there the threatening rocks that bent their giant bodies over the crown of his head and seemed to do battle with the clouds. "Immortal Godhead!" <note>An archaic, dramatic exclamation addressed to the Divine.</note> he cried

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 227, where the knight Herman has just woken up in a cavernous, snake-infested landscape after his exploits in Palestine. The text transitions from his initial terror to a moment of religious resolve and heroic action.</meta>

...up. "Where am I? Are these the outer courts of Hell, or the dwellings of death and destruction? Is it an evil specter that has led me astray into this region? These skulls <note>original: *Todtenſchedel*</note> lying all around me, these decaying bones of my brothers, are the terrible witnesses that no mortal has crossed these thresholds unpunished—but Herman! Why do you tremble? Can these rocky vaults perhaps shut in your voice so that it does not rise up to the Eternal, or do these marble mountains <note>original: *Marmorgebirge*</note> hide you, and does the gaze of the Almighty not penetrate down into these depths? Weak mortal! Take courage; the eye of eternal Providence <note>original: *Vorſicht*; used here in the archaic sense of divine foresight or protection</note> accompanies you on your paths—and what have you to fear if God is with you?"

So spoke Herman, and courage flowed through his veins; he kicked the adders from his sword with his foot and, in a brave struggle, slew the snake that hissed hideously at him. Foam and blood flowed from the wound of the slain worm <note>original: *Wurm*; in medieval and early modern German, this term was frequently used to describe a dragon, lindworm, or monstrous serpent rather than a common earthworm</note>, and the earth itself trembled under the hero’s bold foot and marveled at his courage. —

Exhausted from the struggle, Herman sought out a height upon which the sun shone; there he sat down and girded his sword to his side. "You faithful companion of my life!" he cried out, "you shall never leave your Herman again! Through you, the enemies of the faith <note>original: *Feinde des Glaubens*; a reference to the Saracens during the Crusades</note> fell in Palestine—divine protection <term>Gottheitſchutz: the protective power of the Deity</term> hallowed your strength—until now you raged against the enemies of Herman’s God; now, defend the hero as well, who fought for his faith and whom the Divinity has perhaps destined for even higher deeds, because it still spared his blood, which Herman would have so gladly shed for it."

So spoke Herman, and his fiery eye looked majestically around him; the winds rustled less, the shuddering dread <note>original: *Schauer*; a personification of the creeping horror felt in the cave</note> fled from the rocks and crept into the dwelling of darkness before the sight of the hero.

Herman finally rose when the sun was at noon and sought refreshment in the barren <note>original: *nahrungsloſen*; literally "food-less"</note> region. With boldness, he climbed up a high rock and found there some hazel bushes overgrown with nuts,

After he had wandered through the area for a considerable while, Herman came to a spring. Fresh water bubbled out from the charitable <note>original: "gutthätigen" – implying the rock is personified as being kind or generous</note> rock, providing refreshment for Herman’s thirsty lips.

The midday meal was over, and now Herman occupied himself with the bones of the dead and skeletons that lay scattered about this region. "It is in vain," he said, "I am still too tired to seek a way back from this place—I will set up my night camp here today and bury these bones." <note>Burying the abandoned remains of the dead was considered a high act of Christian mercy and piety in medieval-themed romances.</note> He walked about for a while, and beneath a rock wall, he found various pieces of armor! Helmet, breastplate, and swords, pickaxes, hoes, and shovels. Herman seized a hoe and a shovel, took these tools upon his shoulders, climbed back up the rock, and looked for a place to dig a pit.

"Here," he began, "I will make a resting place for you, for you, whose skeletons

Herman began his work; in vain he struggled to dig, for the hard stones demanded the pickaxe: <note>original: "Bickel" (Pickel) – a heavy iron tool for breaking through rock.</note> he struck, and a muffled tone filled the entire area beneath his blows, so that Herman could easily see that there must be a hollow in the rock. Herman trembled at the sound of this voice; his cheeks grew pale, and cold sweat dripped from his brow.

It is a part of nature when facing unusual things; the instinct for self-preservation <note>original: "Selbsterhaltung"</note> often turns a hero into a coward <term>Memme: a derogatory historical term for a faint-hearted person or "muff"</term> in the moment where physical sensation outweighs the courage of the soul. —

There are things in nature that exceed our expectations, and he who can look upon extraordinary things with indifference in the first moment must possess neither human feeling nor a soul.

Fear is inherent to mankind; only reflection and reason weaken its impact and give rise to courage and bravery. So it was with our Herman as well; his soul soon composed itself again. "You, whoever you may...

Herman gathered all the skulls, bones, and skeletons he could find and cast them into the womb of Mother Earth. It was already late in the evening when Herman had finished his work as a gravedigger; then he rolled a large stone over the opening and spoke to those he had buried: "Rest in peace!"

Tired from his day's work, Herman now sought rest; he chose an elevated spot for his resting place so that the toads and adders <term>Nattern: a general historical term for snakes, often referring to venomous vipers or common grass snakes</term> could not disturb him during the night. — A few nuts were the simple fare <note>The original German text "Gerüchte" is likely a typesetter's error for "Gerichte," meaning dishes or a meal.</note> of his evening meal, and a hollowed handful of water was his drink. —

Already his night-camp of moss was prepared, already the hero lay stretched out and rested until midnight.

This fearsome hour stepped onto the earth with majesty. Ghosts with dragon-wings and raven-claws wandered about in lonely wildernesses for the fearful; will-o'-the-wisps led the traveler astray in swampy places, and with magic spells the necromancer <note>original: "Teufelsbeſchwörer," literally a "devil-conjurer."</note> already stood at the crossroads.

The gathering <note>original: "Baal." This may be a play on the word "Ball" (a dance) or a reference to a pagan deity, suggesting a demonic assembly.</note> was already opening on the Blocksberg <note>The Blocksberg, or Brocken, is the highest peak in the Harz Mountains and is famous in German folklore as the site of the Witches' Sabbath.</note>, and amidst the shrieking of cats and the howling of dogs, the witches' dances were already being celebrated; black tomcats with fiery eyes already played owl-concerts and "meow-nades" <note>original: "Miaunaden," a pun on "serenades."</note> at this magnificent feast.

In the forests the hunting horns resounded, blown by enchanted hunters; the Wild Hunt <term>Nachtgejaid: A piece of European folklore involving a ghostly or supernatural group of hunters passing through the sky or woods in a wild pursuit.</term> cheered in the valley and made a noise with neighing horses and the barking of ghostly dogs.

Couriers on swift billy goats <note>original: "Geisböcken." In German folklore, the devil and witches are frequently depicted riding goats to their gatherings.</note> traveled through the air, and a venerable cavalcade of grave ladies with flying hair followed after them on brooms and pitchforks.

In this fearsome hour Herman awoke, and a sudden <note>original: "gäher," an archaic form of "jäher," meaning abrupt or precipitous.</note> noise startled him from his slumber.

Undaunted and without prejudice, as he was, he did not see all the terrifying images <note>original: "Schröckenbilder."</note> that the fearful man sees, which have their existence only in a corrupted imagination; he looked around undaunted. The stars sparkled in the sky—but how his courage sank again—how his heart trembled, when he saw in the distance a completely unnatural figure rising up out of the earth, coming closer and closer to him; he stared fixedly at this being as it approached him, and could not explain to himself whether it was meant to be a human or an animal. Finally he took the risk, drew his sword, and went toward this adventure; but he had hardly completed a few steps when a supernatural coldness shot through all his limbs—his hair stood on end, and a death-sweat <note>original: "Todesſchweiß," a cold, clammy sweat associated with extreme terror or the agony of dying.</note> was

[harm] what would you, weak spirit! do to my immortal soul? If Herman can contribute anything to the alleviation of your suffering <note>original: "Leidweſens"; an old-fashioned term for a state of grief or affliction.</note>; then count on him, poor spirit! Do not disdain the friendship of a poor mortal!"
