It seemed as if the ghost were friendlier. "Follow me," it cried, "Herman! Follow me!" Herman followed the ghost as well. This spirit led him to the peak of a rock, where there was a large opening. "You have offered me your friendship," cried the ghost—"well then! You shall be my friend, and remain my friend." Here the ghost wrapped its ice-cold arm around Herman’s neck, pulled him with it into the opening, and like an arrow they sped deep down beneath the earth, almost to the **outer courts of hell** <note>original: "Vorhöfe der Hölle." This refers to the vestibule or the regions immediately preceding the entrance to the underworld, a common image in literature of the period.</note>.

Here!—yet I do not wish to tell my readers just yet what befell the bold Herman—good or evil. He shall tell us himself when he emerges from his hell again; and if he comes out no more, then we shall pity him, the noble knight <note>The narrator uses a "cliffhanger" device here, a popular technique in 18th-century Gothic fiction to build suspense before shifting the focus of the story.</note>.

Three whole days had already passed, during which Herman’s friends searched for their companion in vain. The gruesome thunderstorm was over; the rain and the hail, which had scattered the heroes, subsided. But all effort was in vain; no one knew where Herman was.

Reinhold was one of the bravest knights, who fought even at the holy walls of Jerusalem, and at Herman’s side broke many a lance and received many a wound. Common misfortunes and like-minded hearts bound these young men to one another, making them inseparable friends.

When the sun rose in the morning and scattered the shadows of the horrific night—the same night in which Herman had been separated from Reinhold’s side—Reinhold sat sadly upon a hill. Tears rolled down his tanned cheeks and wetted his scarred face.

"Ah!" he cried out, "if only we had fallen at Jerusalem’s bulwarks <note>original: "Pollwerken," an archaic spelling of *Bollwerken*, referring to defensive walls or ramparts.</note>, or if the flashing steel of the Saracens <note>A term used by medieval Europeans to describe Arab Muslims, particularly during the Crusades.</note> had split my skull while I still fought at Herman’s side! Oh, how happy I would have been! But what will Ludmilla say when Reinhold returns without Herman?"

Was it not she, the most excellent of maidens, who at our departure [offered] me her breast—

### 16. ###
## Continuation ##
### of Herman’s Story. ###

**W**hen Reinhold reached the depths of the forest, the densely overgrown paths prevented him from moving forward with his horse. He dismounted, tied the steed <note>Original: "Gaul," a common term for a horse, often implying a sturdy or aging workhorse.</note> to a tree, and intended to search further for his [friend] Herman. However, Reinhold was unexpectedly surrounded by a band of robbers. Reinhold drew his sword; but his defense was too weak—he was overpowered and defeated. Already the robbers were leading the poor, bound Reinhold into their cave and were holding council <note>Original: "ſchloſſen Rath," meaning they deliberated or held a formal meeting to decide a verdict.</note> as to whether they should let him live or kill him.

The death sentence had already been pronounced upon Reinhold when one of the robbers raised his voice and began speaking to his brothers in this way:

"What! Are you not ashamed, men! You who until now placed your glory in your arm,"

Silence reigned throughout the entire assembly, until finally the speaker, after a considerable pause, began again:

"You are silent, and your hearts feel the conviction of the truth. Step closer to me then, young hero, and receive your freedom; but before you leave this place, go as far as the rock where a large fir tree stands and the path forks; there you will find a slain man <note>original: "Erschlagenen" — literally "one who has been struck down."</note>. Strip him, bury his body, and bring his clothes here to us. This I demand of you, that you shall do this before you leave us, and you shall give me your word on this, and swear to me by your sword to fulfill my commission."

"I will do so," Reinhold replied, and swore by his sword. Then the robbers gave him back his helmet, his armor, and his cuirass <term>Harnisch (cuirass): the piece of armor protecting the torso</term>, and kept only that which seemed to them to have some intrinsic value <note>presumably gold, jewels, or coins</note>.

Reinhold was free, and already he was moving away from the robbers' cave.

Reinhold went toward the place the robber had described to him, and when he reached the large fir tree, he saw the lifeless man <note>original: "den Entseelten" — literally "the de-souled one"</note> lying in the moonlight. Was it friendship or love that guided your steps? — You are no more — you have survived the tribulations <term>tribulations: "Drangsalen" refers to the severe hardships or sufferings of life</term> of this life — well for you — but here, here you shall not, by my soul, remain lying. Your noble body shall not be the prey of wild beasts, and the fodder of the bird of prey. Also naked, like a beggar, I will not bury you. Ah! why did I give my word to rob you of your garment and bring it to the robbers? — I will go, will leave them mine, will bind a goat skin around my loins, so as not to make myself guilty of the misdeed that I have deprived a lifeless one <note>original: "Entleibten" — literally "the de-bodied one," a poetic and somber term for a corpse</note> of his clothing. O, thoughtless me! — should I not have immediately seen, not immediately felt the abominableness of this deed, since my heart is not capable of carrying it out?

**Ludmilla.** So fulfill your oath then. Were it love, were it friendship, I could offer you protestations; but now it is duty, and I would not be worthy of you if I could keep you from the fulfillment of your given word. Go, Reinhold, and return soon. —

“You!” he began, “you! who like beasts of prey, secluded from all mankind, live in this wilderness, inhabiting a cave that the light of the sun never warms! You, who have hardened your hearts like the rocky stones that arch over your heads, hear the voice of a young knight: and if even a single one of your nerves is still receptive to the impression of a feeling <note>The mention of "nerves" as the seat of emotion is characteristic of the 18th-century "Literature of Sensibility," which suggested that moral character was linked to physical sensitivity.</note>, if compassion and sympathy can still penetrate your souls, then listen to me, and grant me my request. —”

I gave you my word to undress the slain man, to bring his clothing to you, and to bury his body. A girl of noble soul and courage—and my betrothed—was disguised beneath the armor of the young knight whom you robbed: her wounds are dangerous, and her death will be certain if you should be inexorable in refusing to take her into your cave and pour oil and wine <note>A traditional medicinal treatment for wounds, famously referenced in the Parable of the Good Samaritan.</note> into her wounds. Do not be astonished at my request, for I still credit you with humanity; even if your hearts are as hardened as marble that has defied the seasons for centuries, I still have hope that your wild souls will yet be receptive to the chisel with which human misery has carved many a trace of feeling even into the hardest hearts. But should you deny me this request of mine, know then that my life hangs upon Ludmilla’s life, and that I shall venture everything to avenge your cruelty.

This sword will provide me satisfaction: <note>The term used here, *Genugthuung*, refers to a sense of "satisfaction" or "reparation" common in the code of honor and dueling of the period.</note> and even if I succumb, and even if I sink down into the pit, <note>A poetic or biblical reference to the grave.</note> yet my fall shall also tear some of you monsters into destruction, and avenge the insult which you have inflicted upon humanity.

All were astonished at the youth's fearlessness; mercy and compassion stirred their souls, and they granted the knight his request. Then Reinhold threw his sword away and embraced the robbers. "Now I have no more need of the sword among you," he cried out: "for your hospitable act makes you my friends." —

How astonished Reinhold was, however, when he no longer saw Ludmilla. In vain he searched through the wilderness; in vain he called, Ludmilla! — Ludmilla was no longer there.

Two mountain ash trees <note>The rowan tree, often associated with protection in folklore.</note> stood at the entrance, and hazel bushes bordered the small garden that provided nourishment to its inhabitant. Not far from there, a stream rushed from a rock, plunged roaring down over stones, and lost itself in a blooming valley in winding brooks. To the side, various shrubs arched into a pleasant walkway, and blackbirds and thrushes built their nests within them.

Towards the south <note>The original German uses "Gegen Mittag" (literally "towards noon") to denote the south.</note> stood a majestic oak; upon it the bearded vulture <term>Jochgeyer: A historical name for the bearded vulture (Gypaetus barbatus), the largest bird of prey in the Alps.</term> often rested when returning weary from its prey, and the golden eagle often surveyed the surrounding valleys from its heights with its piercing gaze.

Towards the west <note>The original German uses "Gegen Abend" (literally "towards evening") to denote the west.</note>, a withered oak towered from the midst of black fir thickets; birds of prey and ravens often sat upon the leafless treetops, crying their harsh song down into the melancholy landscape.

Often he sat there melancholy; he leaned his gray head upon his hand and pondered the malice of mankind. — I am happy! he often exclaimed, I who am like one deceased to the living swarm of men; my surroundings enclose me like the grave, and in all parts of the world I am forgotten, like a man who is interred in the earth. Yet, this solitude led me into the temple of wisdom and showed me the truth in its light, which shadows and darkness shroud throughout the world far and wide. — What is man? Created for infinite things, in his capacities like an angel, and yet often, through his deeds, as lowly as a worm and as wicked as a serpent. — His soul soars like the eagle toward the sun, and his heart sinks into the deepest mire <note>The original German "Pfütze" literally means a puddle or pool, but here it metaphorically describes a moral swamp or "slough of despond."</note> of loathsome passions. The seed of his happiness lies in his heart, but malice tears the root of virtue from the soul and plants poisoned thickets of vice in the empty place.

# **Continuation**
## **of Herman’s History.**

**W**eak, like a marsh-reed <note>original: "Moosrohr," likely referring to a common reed or bulrush that bends easily</note> that yields to the breath of the winds, is man; and like a shadow and a phantom <note>original: "Luftgesicht," literally an 'air-face' or apparition</note> the imagined greatness of mortals vanishes.

Uncertain of his life, man hovers every hour at the edge of the pit <note>a common metaphor for the grave</note>; and he, the slave of a single minute, devises plans for eternity!

Mortal, like his brothers, the hero seeks new conquests daily; beneath his sword dying men sink down, and with their blood they purchase larger circuits of the earth for one whom a few shovelfuls of filth <note>original: "Koth," meaning mud, dirt, or mire; used here to emphasize the baseness of the physical earth</note> will cover within a span-long space.

Ah! how my soul breathes freely, away from the clamor of the world, during those hours of rest when it is permitted to wander through melancholy thoughts by the dim light of a lamp! Holy Solitude! You too are the confidante of my heart; your noiseless hours are more blissful than the feast days of kings, where vice often struts in silk, and virtue goes begging in rags! — Night falls, the stars sparkle in the heavens, sleep spreads its wings far and wide, and kings and slaves alike submit to its scepter <note>The "scepter of sleep" is a poetic personification suggesting that sleep is a sovereign even more powerful than earthly monarchs, as it levels all social ranks.</note>. You, who possess feeling, give me your hand and wander with me through these lonely hours, and let us visit the resting places of sleeping mortals. But no, do not visit them! — You would recoil in horror at the plans of malice that lie within the bedrooms of many a villain. Do not visit the sleeping mortals; you might wake the wicked man to new deeds of shame <term>deeds of shame: 'Schandthaten', a strong term for disgraceful or criminal acts that bring dishonor.</term>! — Thank heaven that he rests! At least for a few hours, humanity is safe from him.

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 259: The hermit concludes his nighttime meditation on how the world is safe from villains only while they sleep. This page continues that theme, contrasting the "wise man" with the "courtier" and "judge" during the quiet hours of the night.</meta>

[[page number: 260]]

-> ————— <-

The Divinity <note>original: "Gottheit"—a term common in Enlightenment-era literature to refer to God in a more philosophical, deistic sense</note> gave these hours to the wise man, so that he might indulge in sacred contemplations undisturbed, seeking the paths of virtue. Sleep fetters the arm of the villain; his hand no longer reaches out to steal the property of a poor orphan, or the fortune of a desolate widow. The courtier’s mouth is silent; flattery and falsehood depart from his lips and await the daylight hours to take possession of his tongue once more.

Silence reigns in the temple of justice, and the noise of foolish advocates <note>original: "Sachwalter"—historically referring to legal representatives or stewards</note> no longer drowns out the voice of innocence or buys the hired verdicts of unprincipled judges.

Oh, how light one's heart feels to be able to say: now, for a few hours at least, fewer follies will be practiced and fewer vices committed! Yet, how sad is the thought that half of mankind must sleep just to grant the virtuous man peace from the assaults of malice! But—to what end are my reflections? The morning is already dawning, and

the people will soon awaken again to evil deeds; unmindful that death will one day chain their bodies in a longer slumber, they will carry out acts of malice as if they had no avenging Divinity <note>original: "rächende Gottheit"—reflecting the 18th-century view of a moral universe overseen by a higher power that punishes vice.</note> to fear. Noble Creation! How disfigured you appear! I will turn my eyes away from the disgraceful deeds that mortals commit; I will seek out my hermit again—amidst fearsome cliffs, far from palaces, searching for the abode of virtue.

But, where am I? The wilderness <note>original: "Einöde"—this can mean a desert, a wasteland, or simply a place of profound solitude.</note> where I was is no longer there. A muffled vault surrounds me, my steps glide through a dark passage, a gloomy lamp spreads its mournful glimmer. An emaciated, wretched old man sits here on a piece of rock; a goat-skin wraps around his loins, and a long, ice-gray beard shadows his breast. A young, noble knight sits by his side; he listens attentively to every word the old man speaks. I will step closer. Heavens! What do I see!—It is Herman.

> "Your courage pleases me, young hero! And your noble conduct gave me proof of your good soul. I am neither a spirit nor a specter, but a human being just as you are. Twenty full years I have spent here, secluded from all people in this wilderness, to weep for the most horrific of all deeds that humanity ever saw. Listen to me! I will tell you my story, and if you are capable of pouring peace into my sorrowful soul, then I will consider you an angel whom the Divinity has sent to me to announce the forgiveness of my sins. Sit down on this stone and listen to my story."

The knight sat down, and with a compassionate hand, he dried the tears from the eyes of the old man <note>original: *Greis*</note>, who lifted his gaze to heaven and, after a while, began thus:

Not far from my fortress <note>original: *Veste*; a fortified castle or stronghold</note> lived another young knight whom the whole region revered for his courage. [[page number: 265]]

My happiness, good knight! was of short duration.

For eight months I lived at Ema’s side, and Ema already gave me hope of receiving the most beautiful gift of love <note>A delicate reference to Ema’s pregnancy</note>: when a neighboring nobleman held several knightly games <term>tournaments or jousting competitions</term>, and among others invited me to the festival; ah! had I but followed my Ema—had I heard her pleas, I would not have fallen into the abyss of ruin. "Do not go, beloved!" she said, "and spare your precious life; it is no longer yours alone; your Ema and the being I carry beneath my heart have claims upon it. Why will you risk our happiness for the sake of an imagined honor, and entrust everything dear to your Ema to the infidelity of a lance?" "Stay!" But no, I did not stay; with new fire, the flame of ambition glowed within my breast. I had given my word to appear at the knightly games, and I could no longer revoke it: I shall never forget the day when I stood before my Ema in full armor; she bound the sash <term>Scharpe; a decorative silk scarf or belt worn over armor as a token of a lady's favor</term> around my waist, which her love had em-

Many nobles and knights were gathered there, including, among others, **Horvart**. The contest began, and **Horvart** won the prize; there was no knight whom he did not lift from the saddle or throw from his horse at full gallop, and none was his equal in strength and skill. That was enough for my envious heart; the old enmity awoke once more in my breast, and I hated **Horvart** more strongly than ever. Ema was extremely worried about me; that same evening she sought me out and went as far as Horvart’s fortress <note>The original term 'Veſte' (Veste) refers to a fortified castle or stronghold.</note>, believing she would find me there. Horvart, who was truly my friend in his heart—and whom I hated without cause—welcomed Ema with great kindness. He assigned her a room, had his horse saddled, took several men-at-arms <note>The original 'Reiſige' (Reisiger) refers to mounted, armed retainers or soldiers in service to a lord.</note> with him, and spent the night searching for me, thinking perhaps I had lost my way.

Ema lay sleepless in Horvart’s fortress, her thoughts occupied with the memory of me. Meanwhile, I, who knew nothing of all this, crept into Horvart’s castle. Since everything was silent, I approached the room where I had previously heard voices—the room where Ema was—and I mistook it for Horvart’s bedchamber. With a drawn dagger, I opened the door! She died, and full of despair I cursed myself—I, who murdered my wife <note>The original "Gattinn" is a formal term for a spouse or consort.</note> and my child—into the depths of this wilderness, where I have since nourished myself with sighs and quenched my thirst with tears. Forever unforgettable to me will be the scene of that shuddering night in which I committed the most terrible deed humanity has ever seen. It still seems to me that I see how Ema’s bedchamber was gloomily lit by the moon; it still seems to me that I see the black shadow that a large fir tree, which stood outside the window, cast into the center of the room; I still see Ema lying in blood, and I still hear her voice: "Wretched man! You kill your wife and your child."

Ah! Who is more miserable than I, Knight! And can the Eternal One <note>A common period reference to God or the Divine.</note> forgive me for my sinful deeds?

"Yes," replied Herman, "He forgives you, for He gives your Ema and your children back to you; listen! bow low to the earth, and worship eternal Providence <note>The original German uses 'Vorſicht,' which in this historical context refers to divine Providence or God’s foreseeing care, rather than its modern meaning of 'caution.'</note>."

"Your Ema still lives; the wound you inflicted upon her was not fatal; through Horvart's care she was completely restored, and after a month she gave birth to a daughter and a son, who also still live and have often wept for their father." "What are you telling me?" said the old man, "is it a dream-vision, or the truth?" "It is truth, truth! For the one who now embraces you, who now presses you to his breast—this young knight whom you now see before you, upon whose heart you now place your hand—he is Herman, your son; it is I."

Tears and embraces were the only expressions and words here, and the old man could hardly recover from his astonishment.

-> \*     \*     \* <-

Beneath the cloak of fairy tales <note>original: 'Mährchen'—folklore or wonder-tales</note> lies many a true story hidden; and regions that are rumored to be haunted by ghosts were, for the most part, the dwelling places of unfortunate people. He who has courage can discover many a riddle of nature; but who has courage, true courage? Only he who possesses true virtue.

**T**he sun gradually brought the autumnal day to a close, greeting the peaks of the distant mountains once more with its final rays in a reddish glow, beckoning the evening to arrive. Leonore still sat beneath the linden tree—proud in its many years <note>original: 'jahrestolzen'—suggesting a majestic, aged tree</note>—which stood before her cottage, anxiously awaiting the return of her husband, who had gone to the nearby little town to attend to a small matter of business. Carefree and unaware of their suffering mother’s worry, her children played in a circle around her, idling away the anxious hours for which the tender wife waited, sad and full of foreboding <note>original: 'ahndungsvoll'—an archaic spelling of 'ahnungsvoll,' meaning filled with a sense of impending trouble or intuition</note>.

Only Albert, the first joy of their happy love, nestled against his mother, caressed her rosy cheeks, and did not stop asking: "Ah! is Father not coming soon? — Hey Mother, when is he coming?"

Ah! still no comforting glance could satisfy the boy, for he still saw his mother’s brow clouded, and now—for the first time—his good, innocent heart, which no internal sorrow had ever wounded, was tormented by anxiety and a trembling longing. Albert wept. Leonore pressed him to her bosom, kissed him, and a hot tear—one of the thousands that a hostile fate had wrung from her—fell from her eye. — Poor child! she sighed, when heaven gave you to me, those were still happy days, and now—with me a victim of persecution, the prey <note>original: "Raub," literally "the robbery" or "the spoil"</note> of misfortune, what terrible sufferings yet await you? —

Already the neighboring moon was spreading its pale light—borrowed from the benevolent sun—over the dewy earth; the stars adorned the bluish sky, and Harwort still had not come.

Meanwhile, Harwort took the bandage from the stranger's forehead, then washed and anointed <note>Applying salves or oils was a primary method of treating open wounds before the widespread use of modern antiseptics.</note> his wounds. Now he also wished to undress him; but the man's arm was already so badly swollen by this time that they had to cut the sleeve from his coat. Now Lorchen also arrived, and both busied themselves in the service of the sick man. He spoke little, and what he did say were merely expressions of pain, or wonder at his own condition and the unknown place and people he saw occupied around him; for in his initial stupor <note>original: "Betäubung"—refers to a state of being dazed or unconscious due to trauma</note>, he had known nothing of what was happening to him, and even now his shattered senses were capable of no clear understanding.

They laid him in a humble bed <note>original: "schlechtes Bette"—in 18th-century German, "schlecht" often meant simple, plain, or poor, reflecting the family's modest social standing, rather than "bad" in quality.</note>, the only resting place available for the unfortunate, in order to provide him with some recovery.

During this time, Lorchen stayed at Harwort's side, telling him how anxious his long absence had made her; and after this, her first priority was to ask him who the stranger was, how this misfortune had befallen him, and how Harwort had come to find him.

"I cannot tell you his name yet," Harwort replied, "but I will tell you everything else.

I was delayed in Pinau against all expectations for so long that I only set out toward evening; yet I did not regret it, for my business <term>Gewerbe: Harwort's trade or commercial dealings</term> went more successfully than I had hoped; but more on that another time. I hurried as much as I could, spurred by joy and love, and when I had come about a quarter of an hour's distance closer, I heard someone in the distance, about a hundred paces off, riding at a thundering gallop over 'stone and stick' <note>A variation of the German idiom "über Stock und Stein," meaning to travel cross-country or over very rough terrain regardless of obstacles.</note>; I looked around to see, and suddenly—the rider tried to leap over the deep ditch that lies between the fields and the main road <term>Heerstraße: literally a 'host road,' a major public highway designed for the passage of armies and heavy traffic</term>; the horse fell, and both steed and man vanished from sight as if the earth had swallowed them up. Good Lorchen! How wondrous, how incomprehensibly winding are the paths upon which Providence <note>original: "Vorſicht"—while this can mean 'caution,' in this period it specifically refers to Divine Providence or the guiding hand of fate.</note> leads mankind!—The man likely never thought in his life that he would one day spend the night in this hut—with us—and yet—yet he must. We, too, would likely never have expected this guest.

Never, Lorchen replied, least of all in this tragic state.

Lorchen! How gladly our heart, which has so much room for flatteries, lets itself be deceived by sweet illusions!—We are moved, we feel pity, we weep tears of the tenderest sympathy—and often so mechanically—merely a necessary effect of external impressions upon our senses. Your heart still suffers at the sight of this unfortunate stranger, he is still worthy in your eyes; but what if he were a villain <term>Böſewicht</term>?—Suppose the case that you knew him, and he were one of our enemies—the author <term>Stifter</term> of our hardships—would your heart not recoil before him? Would the pitied, unfortunate man not become a monster <note>original: "Scheuſal"—a term for something or someone abhorrent or loathsome.</note> in your eyes?

**Frenthof** did not have an evil heart; but it was very weak in resisting dazzling temptation. ## 19.
# **Valentour,**
### *or the Power of Education over the Heart.*

**V**alentour lived at court. In the colorful bustle of so many kinds of people—scoundrels, wise men, and fools; in the circus of countless, constantly crossing interests, where everyone runs toward their goal with free reins; on this stage of human characters, where all the passions of man and their masks reveal themselves, Valentour learned to know man, the world, and the course of things. His practiced gaze penetrated the innermost depths of the human heart, and the language of souls—which, for those who know how to read it, is drawn upon the forehead in unmistakable letters—was as understandable to him as the language of his own country <note>This refers to the 18th-century fascination with physiognomy, the belief that a person’s character or "soul" could be read through their facial features and expressions</note>. 

Thus he proved to the dark, reclusive philosopher that man studies his fellow man much more reliably among people than in a lonely hut, or in a **garret** <term>Dachstübchen: a small, often cramped room directly under the roof, typically associated with the "starving artist" or the isolated scholar</term> by a dim lamp, where the philosopher only thinks, and does not see; where he judges the course of the human heart according to his own, and thereby misses the thousand times thousand nuances [ta-]

-> T <-
-> ern <-

The <term>cultivation</term> <note>original: "Bildung" — a central German concept referring to the holistic development of a person's character, intellect, and soul</note> of his son was now Valentour’s foremost focus. —

He soon discovered the seeds of vice within the child, and trembling, he reflected on the choice of that rare man who was meant to shape the offspring into a future man, husband, father, citizen, and servant of the King. He knew all too well the carelessness and the emptiness of heart found in the majority of those people who call themselves <term>tutors</term> <note>original: "Hofmeister" — these were private governors hired by noble families; at the time this was written, they were frequently criticized in literature for being pedantic, unqualified, or socially disconnected</note>; men who, being neither fathers nor husbands, and rarely true citizens of their country — often appearing as links torn from the social chain — are entirely inexperienced in paternal or domestic cares, civic duties, and love of country. He knew their ignorance beyond a few academic subjects, the impure motives that drive them to this profession, and often the lowliness of their character, far too well not to tremble at the great risk of entrusting his child — the most precious thing held in a father’s heart — to a hireling.

The capital city, into which all idle and useless folk crowd, swarmed with an

Here, one has feigned the trust of a venerable matron, who then recommends him everywhere; there, the chambermaid carries the future <term>tutor</term> <note>original: "Hofmeister" — a private governor for noble children</note> in her apron to the gracious lady, whose sleepy husband—out of a love for ease—leaves the choice of all <term>servants</term> <note>original: "Domestiquen" — a term from the French widely used in German courts for domestic staff</note>, and consequently the tutor as well, to her. That one forces his way into the nursery through <term>intrigue</term> <note>original: "Kabale" — social plotting or secret schemes</note>; this one through subservience, or some talent that recommends him to the fashionable world. He dances beautifully, understands music, chatters most charmingly in social circles, and prattles to the point of enchantment—and so many other such "skills." Some owe their good fortune to <term>mistresses</term> <note>original: "Maitressen" and "Buhlerinnen" — women of the court who held influence over appointments</note> and paramours; others to a kinship with the coachman or valet, who happens to be the master’s favorite or—accomplice. Very few undertake this heavy burden out of a true sense of calling.

The sheer multitude of <term>applicants</term> <note>original: 'Kompetenten'</note> only increased his fear regarding this dangerous step and made the choice difficult for him in the extreme. The wisest thing he could do under the circumstances was to take—none of them.

St. Luce possessed extensive knowledge; he was no stranger to any science, was acquainted with the fine arts, well-versed in linguistics and ethnology <note>original: "Völkerkunde"—the study of different peoples and cultures</note>, and with his irreproachable character, he seemed to exceed every expectation.

His outward appearance was not very engaging and did not reflect a man of the world; his brow was constantly clouded, and a certain morose demeanor seemed to have banished all social joy from his heart. However, people were willing to view all of this as a consequence of his tragic misfortunes, and hoped that by improving his circumstances, his spirit might be brightened once more. Nevertheless, in light of his other virtues, he remained the only suitable choice among countless others.

How could the restless human spirit—for which an entire world lacks sufficient room—find peace within the narrow room where the young Valentour <note>Referring here to the son of the elder Valentour mentioned on the previous page</note> was constantly confined? **Valentour.** "Now, my dear sir! I have you right where I wanted you. Man requires a guide; I sought one in you for my son, and I hope I have found him. He will press through the dangers of the world unharmed, guided by the hand of his mentor; he will see follies, and once you show him that they are indeed follies, their false glitter <note>original: 'Schimmer' — referring to a deceptive or superficial glow</note> will no longer be able to seduce his heart. He will abhor vice because you have made the value of virtue more enchanting to him. Then, if you combine history with this experience, you cannot fail in your purpose. History alone, without practical interaction with the world, is not only insufficient; it is actually harmful. It leads a person into a world that is no longer our own; it makes him acquainted with dead people, yet we live among the living. Thus, even with the deepest historical insight, a man remains a child in the contemporary world."

# Valentour;
### or
## the Power of Education over the Heart.
### Continuation.

**V**alentour directed his primary attention during the <term>Erziehung</term> <note>upbringing and education</note> of his son toward gaining the child’s trust, and binding the boy's heart so closely to his own that no gap could exist between the two. And how easy was this for him? He met him with gentleness and avoided, as much as possible, scaring him away with harsh reproaches; no pleasure, no small kindness could be enjoyed by the boy that he did not see being offered from his father’s own hand. Punishments—suitably and rarely applied—were always administered in such a way that they never appeared to be a consequence of anger, but were always seen as arising necessarily from the transgression itself, so that the boy viewed both the crime and the punishment as his own doing. He withdrew him as much as possible from the company of the <term>Domestiquen</term> <note>domestic servants; at the time, educational theorists often worried children would learn "low" habits or improper language from household staff</note>, and in the arts and sciences, he provided him with wise and honest
| U | ne |

<summary>Valentour begins his son's education by focusing on emotional bonding and gentle discipline, ensuring that the child views his father as the source of all joy and his own actions as the source of his punishments.</summary>
<keywords>educational psychology, father-son bond, discipline, moral development, Valentour, 18th-century pedagogy</keywords>

...men chosen who, in addition to skill in their discipline, also possessed character and propriety <note>The original German 'Wohlstand' here refers less to material wealth and more to a 'well-ordered' or 'respectable' state of life and conduct.</note>, and were strictly bound to the father’s instructions. Valentour, who understood both the physical <note>original: 'thierischen' — literally 'animalistic,' referring here to the biological and instinctual aspects of human nature common in Enlightenment thought.</note> and the moral nature of man and the relationship between the two, achieved his purpose with ease. He followed the path of nature and avoided all those other side-paths—those systems and methods created for the timid and sluggish mind. On this path, he found all those pedagogical aids <term>pedagogical aids|educational tools, such as instructional manuals and simplified textbooks</term> to be unnecessary—tools which are currently distributed so abundantly, yet with so little success, to parents and children. From his good heart sprang the most tender love, which never grew weary of his darling child; and his great, prejudice-free spirit showed him the truth, preventing his heart from becoming overly sentimental. The inexhaustible treasure of experience and the "great book of nature" served him in place of all other volumes. He also denied his son any useless, burdensome clutter of books; instead, he directed his attention toward the objects themselves that occur in nature around mankind, letting him observe more than read. For one knows only too well—and more so than in the cent-

Man develops in stages; gradually the nobler spirit escapes the shackles of the animal body <note>original: 'thierischen Körper' — referring to the biological, instinctual, and physical nature of humans, a common theme in Enlightenment philosophy.</note>, gradually the boy matures into a youth, the youth into a man. How important are these stages in the history of a human being, and how vital to the attention of the educator! These are epochs where the development of the seeds <note>original: 'Keime' — used here as a metaphor for innate potential or character traits.</note>, the growth, and the dominance of inclinations become most visible to us.

Valentour observed these correctly; he saw the seeds of good and evil ripen and the passions break through the shell in which they lay dormant during childhood. The young Valentour now entered an age where the mind—too great to be led by leading-strings <note>original: 'gängeln' — refers to the straps used to guide toddlers, used here as a metaphor for intellectual or moral tutelage</note>, and yet still too weak to trust itself—exposes the heart to every danger. Passions reside in the <term>Temperament</term> <note>In 18th-century psychology, "temperament" referred to a person's innate biological and psychological disposition, often linked to the four humors.</note>; the rational person does not seek to eradicate them, but rather to guide them; for the seed of virtue also lies within them. Even if the determination of one's temperament is involuntary—even if it is a gift of nature and climate—man's free will and the means to lead him toward the good, which religion and <term>Sittenlehre</term> <note>Literally "teaching of customs," referring to ethics or moral philosophy.</note> provide, are also gifts of the Godhead. Often passions merely slumber, then wake again and sweep man away with irresistible fury if the principles of virtue do not maintain his balance.

The many paths taken by educators in this case have mostly led astray. Out of youths with the most hopeful potential to become great men, they have raised spiteful characters, hypocrites and secret villains, impetuous wildings or sluggish, inactive machines, in whom all spirit and all fire has been extinguished—<term>Automaten</term> <note>Automata; a popular Enlightenment metaphor for people who have been so strictly disciplined that they act like clockwork machines without independent thought.</note>, who no longer act of their own power, but move mechanically according to

the direction given to them by an external force.

Here too, Valentour found his path mapped out by nature. Pressure and counter-pressure establish balance, and this must also, as much as possible, be established among the passions. One must oppose the lower passions with nobler ones; for example, [oppose] the inclination toward <term>Wohllust</term> <note>In this context, "Wohllust" refers to carnal lust or excessive sensual pleasure.</note> with honor, self-love, tender feelings toward others, respect for one's fellow man, the nobility and dignity of one's station, and even a certain degree of <term>Seelenstolz</term> <note>Literally "soul-pride"; an Enlightenment concept referring to a sense of inner spiritual dignity or self-respect that prevents a person from acting beneath themselves.</note>; so that evil can never—or at least less often—outweigh the good. A <term>Selbstgefühl</term> <note>Literally "self-feeling"; an internal moral intuition or the ability to feel the state of one’s own soul.</note> of good and evil is the surest way to learn to recognize both. Valentour therefore frequently placed his son in situations where he could feel for himself the consequences of anger, <term>Nachsucht</term> <note>Indulgence or a lack of self-control; the tendency to yield to one's cravings.</note>, vanity, and other wicked inclinations. He made use of every opportunity where he could—mostly through immediate examples—illustrate the sad consequences of prejudice, the follies of fashion, and vice. Then he also instilled in him those principles that strengthen the character of a nobleman; principles
-> of <-

[[vocabulary: balance, carnal lust, soul-pride, self-feeling, Valentour, anger, indulgence, prejudice, vice, character]]

<summary>This page describes Valentour’s educational philosophy, which uses the physical principle of "pressure and counter-pressure" to balance base passions with noble virtues. He favors practical experience over abstract theory, allowing his son to witness the negative consequences of vice and social follies firsthand.</summary>

of honor, integrity, philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe," referring to a general love of mankind or benevolence</note>, gratitude, self-respect, human dignity, and so on. To be sure, these virtues do not always maintain the same power with which they move the heart at the moment they are first presented; yet they are never entirely lost from the soul. One recalls them again when specific cases arise, and then they resist the opposing passions with renewed strength.

Because of the trust the son placed in his father, every word was sacred to him. No doubt or contradiction could make his father's teachings seem suspect to him. Through the tenderness with which he saw himself loved by his father, and with which he returned that love, he would not have abandoned those principles for anything in the world, nor acted against his father’s will. And if he did occasionally err out of weakness or frivolity <note>original: "Leichtsinn," implying a lack of reflection or carelessness</note>, he was less sorry for the harm he brought upon himself through his mistake than for having offended his father’s tender affection.

Indescribable, felt only by a father, is the joy that Valentour felt over his son. No earthly happiness could have moved his heart to more heartfelt bliss <note>original: "Wonne," a word implying intense delight or rapture</note>, nor raised his soul further above all the hardships of life, than seeing his care and love so richly rewarded in his noble son. Many a father—who was too lazy or too comfortable to devote those hours left over from his professional duties <note>original: "Berufsgeschäfte"</note> to the education of his child—now looked with tearful eyes upon the happy Valentour. He regretted too late that he had heartlessly and neglectfully left his child in the hands of a stranger, who had formed of him a hopeless good-for-nothing <note>original: "Taugenichts"</note>, an ill-mannered fellow, or even a villain.

Soon Valentour <note>Based on the context of entering public service, this likely refers to the son, though he shares his father's name</note> held a significant position in the state, where he was envied and loved, generally esteemed, and won the approval of the monarch and his nation in full measure. Still guided by the hand of his wise leader <note>his father</note>, who alone possessed his complete trust, he walked through the midst of the dangers of the court, where from all

sides where slander, intrigue, harassment, and seduction stood in his way, he remained unhurt in his heart, his fortune, and his honor.

Whenever a passion occasionally threatened to tear him from his tracks, a nobler one held him back, and the watchful eye of his father, which he never escaped, never allowed him to stray from his path.

Young Valentour was the darling of social circles; his charming cultivation <note>The original German 'Bildung' implies a combination of formal education, social etiquette, and the refinement of character</note> and his sublime spirit undoubtedly earned him this favor. He also valued the approval of the fair sex, for how could he remain indifferent to it, given his own strong inclinations?

— But this also gave rise to many unpleasant incidents and tempted him toward many a step that would have been his next move toward ruin, had he not been held back in time by the memory of his father's principles and the mental images of the terrors of vice <note>original: 'Laster'</note>, which his father had etched into his heart with indelible strokes.

Among all the beauties who dazzled at court, the Marquise de Baliere pursued him most dangerously. She was beautiful, possessed great wit <note>The original German 'Geist' here refers to a combination of intelligence, charisma, and social cleverness</note>, and was still in the prime of her life. Being well-practiced in the arts of love, it was easy for her to attract a fiery, love-struck youth <note>original: 'Jüngling'</note> whose heart was still innocent.

Valentour, completely swept away by her captivating charms and his own passion, betrayed the secret story of his heart with every glance they shared. However, the cunning beauty—who took pleasure in seeing the young man brought to such a state—acted as though she knew nothing of the stirrings of his heart. She avoided his tender gazes and provoked him with such apparent indifference <note>original: 'Nachlässigkeit', implying a calculated lack of attention</note> that Valentour was on the verge of making a formal proposal to her. One evening, as Valentour was leaving the theater—where a sensual <note>The original German "wollüstige" carries a strong connotation of eroticism and carnal desire, which the author frames as a dangerous "inflaming" of the imagination.</note> scene had just inflamed his imagination and reawakened the passion that had rested in his bosom for a long time—he encountered a young gentleman of his acquaintance, who was an immoderate and already shameless libertine. It cost him few words to persuade the young Valentour to go with him and partake in his pleasures. Lavignon, as he was called, led him to a small, secluded house at the outermost edge of the city. An old wretch, grown gray in shame and crime, lured poor, innocent girls—whom fortune had cast out—into this den, and hid them from the eyes of justice until they fell as sacrifices at the altar of shame. Shame and despair then bound them to the very vice to which they had been forced. Valentour stepped inside with his companion; his step faltered, but he lacked the strength and resolve to flee. ### 21.

# Valentour,
### or
### the Power of Education over the Heart.
### Continuation.

**S**o Valentour spent the days of his blossoming life; at times, passions tore him toward the most dangerous abysses. Yet the power of education <note>original: "Erziehung" — in this context, it refers to his moral upbringing and the character-forming guidance he received in youth</note> triumphed over his temperament and held the wavering man back so that he did not fall.

Man is the slave of time and circumstances; he must never boast of his virtue, for a single moment of fiery passion can destroy the edifices of years that morality and reflection have built. In many a person who seems virtuous, the seed of the most repulsive vice lies hidden in the heart, often merely awaiting the right moment for its development. He who trusts in his own strength is weak and already near to a fall.

There are wealthy people, and one admires them for being honest—that is to say, they do not cheat, they do not steal, they do not murder—

-> ✠ <-

-> the <- <note>This is a "catchword," a common feature in old printing where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom right to help the binder and the reader.</note>

[[vocabulary: Education, Temperament, Morality, Slave, Virtue, Vice]]

<summary>This page reflects on how Valentour's moral upbringing (Erziehung) protects him from his own volatile passions. It offers a philosophical warning that virtue is often fragile and dependent on favorable circumstances rather than innate strength.</summary>
<keywords>upbringing, moral character, passion vs. reason, fragility of virtue, human nature, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 331 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on how the wealthy are often only "virtuous" because they lack the temptation of poverty. This page explores the relationship between moral upbringing, poverty, and the human "animal nature."</meta>
[[page number: 322]]

-> ————— <-

if they do not <note>Connecting to the previous page: "they do not deceive, they do not steal, they do not murder..."</note>, where is the virtue in that? Where is the merit? Change their circumstances, take away the thousands they enjoy, and see if they can stand the test. Poverty is the mother of many an ignoble deed; a lack of moral cultivation and upbringing <note>The German terms *Herzensbildung* (formation of the heart) and *Erziehung* (upbringing) imply the shaping of character and conscience rather than just academic schooling.</note> accompany it, and what is a human being without such an upbringing? His animal nature thirsts for the satisfaction of his needs; he seeks out means without investigating whether they are permitted or forbidden.

Place a pound on one side of a scale and leave the other side empty, and the side with the pound will necessarily outweigh the other. Imagine now a person entirely without an upbringing; needs, passions, and examples entice him toward vice. He does not know the flatteries of self-love, nor the seductions of self-interest; he follows blindly and plunges into his ruin.

O you! To whom chance and birth have granted advantages that you often have not earned: humble yourselves before the wretched and have compassion for the criminal. The poor man is also a human being, just like you; you are rich, you have everything, and you know no want.

-> The <-

<summary>This page argues that virtue is often a product of circumstance and upbringing rather than innate goodness, suggesting that the wealthy should feel compassion for criminals who were shaped by poverty and lack of moral education.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, poverty, upbringing (Erziehung), moral cultivation (Herzensbildung), social class, compassion, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 332 ---
<meta>This page continues the previous argument regarding the superficiality of virtue among the upper classes. While the previous page focused on how wealth shields people from the temptations of crime, this page critiques the "fashionable education" of the elite, which produces performative morality rather than true character.</meta>
[[page number: 323]]

-> ————— <-

The very first rising thought of a desire is already sated through satisfaction, and yet—and yet, what have you not already done! If you were now completely without an upbringing, completely without principles, what would become of you? But how so—your upbringing is often just a mask! You have learned to criticize virtuously, but not to act virtuously. The private tutor <term>Hofmeister: a live-in governor responsible for the education and manners of a nobleman's son</term> must for the most part be a dealer of masks <note>original: "Maskenkrämer"</note>, who has various disguises and costumes in his stall. This one wants to shape his son into a Spaniard, that one his child into a Frenchman; one is to wear the black cloak of a philosopher, and another the mask of the politician.

In the greenhouse of our fashionable education <note>original: "Modeerziehung"</note>, they grow up like sponges: strange to behold, but without spirit, without taste, without strength.

They do not think, but rather they speak what others have thought; they mock because others mock, laugh because others laugh, and are fools <note>original: "Thoren"</note> because others are fools. They have read much but thought little; they are literary men-of-the-letter <note>original: "litterarische Buchstaben-Menschen"; a critique of people who possess book-learning without any original thought or practical wisdom.</note>.

| | X 2 | The |

<margin>desire, principles, mask, tutor, dealer of masks, fashionable education, fools, literary men-of-the-letter</margin>

<summary>The author critiques the superficial "fashionable education" of the wealthy, arguing that it merely teaches children to perform roles and mimic others rather than developing genuine thought or virtue. He compares modern tutors to mask-sellers who provide children with various national or professional personas instead of real character.</summary>
<keywords>education, Hofmeister, superficiality, virtue vs. performance, social masks, pedagogy, intellectualism</keywords>

--- Page 333 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of superficial "fashionable education" discussed on page 323, where education was described as a mask that produces people without spirit or strength. It transitions into the specific pedagogical approach of the character "Old Valentour."</meta>
[[page number: 324]]

-> ————— <-

Hence those monstrosities <note>original: "Misgeburten"—literally "mis-births" or deformities of nature, used here to describe intellectual or moral failures</note> of the spirit; hence that multitude of adventurous beast-men <note>original: "Thiermenschen"—individuals who possess human form but animalistic, uncivilized instincts</note> who daily appear on the stage of the world. Hence those people who are half lamb and half wolf, half vulture and half dove, half good and half evil.

Old Valentour, who knew all of this, sought to form his son into neither a cavalier <note>a gentleman of high social standing, often associated with military or courtly grace</note> nor a courtier, nor into one of those so-called "men of the world," but rather to form him into a **human being**.

He never spoke to him of his ancestors, never of their merits, but only of those merits that the young Valentour should earn for himself.

If the blood, he often told him, that flowed from heroes in Valentour’s lineage spoils in these veins and creeps within a worthless person, then say: Valentour’s nobility <note>original: "Adel"—referring here to both the legal social rank and the inherent moral dignity of the family</note> has ceased to exist from the moment you began to be.

These noble principles, which Valentour instilled in his son, also became the happiness of his days.

-> The <-

The noble dispositions of his heart always knew how to maintain balance against his passions, and always triumphed over them.

Young Valentour’s fiery temperament would have inevitably plunged the good youth into ruin with a different upbringing.

The fire that glowed through Valentour’s veins often swept him away toward a dangerous step; but the noble principles soon awakened again and gained the upper hand.

When our heart is harmoniously tuned from youth onward, the soul soon senses when vice <note>original: "Laster"—referring to moral failings or wicked habits</note> attempts to put it out of tune.

The practice of acting nobly gives skill <note>original: "Fertigkeit"—a term implying a proficiency or ease of action acquired through constant habit</note>, and this skill is the victor over the passions.

Valentour often encountered wicked opportunities: once he saw Belmine, a beautiful creature, but one of those unfortunate souls whom poverty and a lack of education [had brought] to the shame of womanhood.

No, I will not so desecrate the feeling of nature. Extinguish, O Infinite One! <note>The "Infinite One" is a common Enlightenment-era reference to God or the Divine Creator.</note> every feeling of love in a heart, if animal passion could ever desecrate it.

Thus Valentour spoke to himself, and the first principles of his education triumphed over his passion. Philanthropy, benevolence, faith in Providence, a certain noble modesty, respect for humanity <note>original: "Menschenachtung." While in modern German this can imply "contempt," here it refers to the Enlightenment ideal of holding the dignity of the human race in high regard.</note>—all these things, which are noble and good for mankind, awoke in Valentour’s soul and led him back to the paths of virtue.

Valentour was happy, and often throughout the days of his life, he thanked the good education that his father had given him.

Respect for humanity is one of the most noble virtues, and it can never be laid close enough to the heart of a youth; it alone is capable of producing good men. In it alone rests the seed of all virtues; he who knows how to respect humanity will never lower himself to vice. Compassion, gentleness, and benevolence are [intertwined] with respect for humanity—

Self-distrust is the second pillar of virtue, for it makes us cautious on our paths. If one ever wishes to be convinced of the character <note>original: "Kavakter," likely an OCR error or archaic spelling for "Charakter."</note> of a young man, one need only look at how modest he is. Those impudent fellows <note>original: "Purſche" (Bursche). A term for young men or boys, often used here in a derogatory or dismissive sense.</note> of our century, who give their decisive opinion on everything, who despise everything because they believe themselves alone to be wise—what will these fellows become for humanity!

Their pride, their arrogance betray their lack of understanding; how the right-thinking man might weep for his fatherland when he sees the multitude of young fools, as they—puffed up by their self-satisfaction—fancy themselves clever and wise.

Their ridiculous, stiff manner, their "doctor’s expression" on a grimacing face, their pronunciation through their teeth—such as "H—o—w d—o y—o—u f—i—n—d y—o—u—r—s—e—l—f?" <note>The author is mocking an affected, upper-class, or pseudo-intellectual way of speaking slowly through clenched teeth.</note>—and everything that revolts a [true] man, bodes little good for future humanity.

<meta>The text continues the author's critique from page 329 regarding the moral and intellectual decline of the younger generation. There once were times when the young man sought the company of the mature man. His ear was attentive to the voice of the wise, and examples led him toward virtue; but those times have passed, and so too have the men whom posterity once admired.

We rarely raise thinkers anymore, but rather chatterers, who with a thousand words say ten times worse what some have already said poorly enough.

Their reading is like a beggar’s pot <note>The original German "Bettler Hafen" refers to a jar or pot into which a beggar would throw all the various scraps of food they received, creating a disorganized and unappealing mixture.</note>, in which good and bad scraps—everything mixed together, warm and cold, soft and hard, bitter and sweet—may be found. Why then are you surprised when virtue goes about in rags?

Nowadays, it is demanded that the boy should know a great deal; whether he knows it well, no one cares, so long as it dazzles—as if nature only required a coat of varnish <term>varnish (Firniß): a glossy coating used here as a metaphor for a superficial education that masks a lack of character</term>, even though it may be carved from rotten wood.

And then our tutors <term>tutors (Hofmeister): private instructors hired by wealthy or noble families to educate and supervise children, often living within the household</term>, how are they regarded? Do the fathers think of them as the primary friends of the family? Are they met with that respect with which one should meet men who sow the seeds for the happiness of subsequent centuries? Is it recognized that the gracious Lord Father has only made the animal, and it is the tutor who has first made the human? <note>The author uses "animal" to refer to the biological birth of the child, contrasting it with the "human," which he views as a product of moral and intellectual cultivation.</note>

Is the man to whom one entrusts the well-being of one’s children always allowed unrestricted freedom to shape the young heart?

Do parents not often ruin more in a single evening through their poor principles <note>The original "Grundsätze" refers to foundational moral maxims or rules of conduct.</note> than what the educator has made right over spans of years? Are the young ones not always told that they are of the nobility, and thus that merit is already born within them—that they therefore have no need to strive for merit? Show your pupils <term>pupils (Zöglinge): young people under the care and instruction of a mentor</term> the vanity of these principles. Noble actions give a person <term>merit</term> <note>original: 'Verdienst'; in this context, it refers to earned worthiness and moral character as opposed to inherited titles.</note>, not birth; one can never say this enough, never repeat it enough, for man, who by his nature is lazy and proud, likes to adorn himself with the merits of others and likes to fall asleep on laurels that others have gathered.

The elder Valentour once led his son, when he was still a boy, into the garden and showed him a tree that bore no fruit. "See," he said, "Son! The ancestors of this tree bore the best fruit; they were the ornament of this garden, but this <term>offspring</term> <note>original: 'Zögling'; while usually meaning a pupil or ward, here it refers to the young tree, creating a parallel between botanical cultivation and human education.</note> has degenerated. Through such examples, Valentour often instilled the impulse for <term>emulation</term> <note>original: 'Nacheiferung'; the act of striving to equal or excel the achievements of another, particularly through moral imitation.</note> into his son’s heart. One day he was with him in a <term>cabinet of natural curiosities</term> <note>original: 'Naturalienkabinet'; a precursor to the modern museum, these collections featured biological, geological, and archaeological specimens.</note>, where they were shown an embryo that had been removed from the body of a lady who had recently died.

Valentour led his son to this object. "Friend!" he said, "See this embryo; it descends from one of the best families. If the deeds of ancestors could affect us directly without our own <term>merits</term> <note>original: 'Verdienste'; refers to the worth one earns through their own labor or moral conduct.</note>, then there is no nobleman in the land who could dare to compare his merits with the merits of this embryo. Do you understand me?"

Young Valentour embraced <note>The original German contains a typo 'nmarmte' for 'umarmte' (embraced).</note> his father. "Yes, I understand you," he said. "I know that the merits of my ancestors will never affect me without the addition of my own; they will never serve as a source of pride for me, but rather as an inspiration to emulate them, so that I may become worthy of my ancestors."

[[notes: Catchword at bottom right: 'So']]

<summary>Valentour uses a macabre display in a cabinet of curiosities—a high-born embryo—to demonstrate to his son that noble lineage provides no personal merit if one has not performed any deeds of their own. The son accepts the lesson, viewing his ancestors as models for imitation rather than a reason for pride.</summary>

--- Page 343 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Valentour. While the previous page focused on his external role as a father and teacher using a scientific specimen to impart a moral lesson, this page shifts inward, exploring Valentour's private reflections and the psychological struggle between his natural impulses and his moral ideals.</meta>

[[page number: 334]]

-> ————— <-

As happy as Valentour truly was, he often considered himself unhappy when he felt the strength of the current of his passions <note>original: "Leidenschaften" — in the 18th century, "passions" referred to strong, often overwhelming emotions or biological drives that were seen as the opposite of rational reason.</note>, against which virtue often seemed to him but a weak dam. It often embittered the days of his life when he saw himself spinning in the whirlpool of life, and in many lonely hours, as he read through the history of his life, he shuddered at many passages because of the dangers that can sweep away even the firmest man. Even amidst the delight of having successfully survived them, he still trembled; at one memory he blushed, and at another, his dim, tearful gaze offered thanks to the Savior <note>Likely a reference to Divine Providence or God, common in Enlightenment-era literature.</note> who had rescued him from danger. Carefully and strictly—too wise to be vain—he examined every one of his steps; he rejoiced in those that led him closer to his goal and regretted others—yet he was fortunate enough never to have to curse a single one.

Ah, these hours—the most precious for the wise man—which he can enjoy undisturbed, free from business and worries! How quickly they slip away from time and too soon give way to the coming moments, where we, no longer so entirely our own masters, are once again the playthings of opportunity! —

<margin>Valen.</margin>

<summary>Valentour engages in a deeply personal reflection on his past, weighing his internal struggles and passions against his commitment to virtue. He treasures the rare, quiet moments of self-examination that allow him to remain his own master before returning to the distractions of the world.</summary>

<keywords>introspection, passions, virtue, Enlightenment morality, self-examination, passage of time, Providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the internal monologue of Valentour established on page 334. Valentour, in those hours of self-examination <note>original: "Selbstprüfung" — a central practice in 18th-century Pietism and Enlightenment philosophy, where one rigorously analyzed one's own thoughts and motives.</note>, thought himself so strong, so powerful; his resolution was so unshakeably fixed in his soul that he would have boldly defied the fiercest storms on the spot.

In the same way, a sick man, during those refreshing intervals where pain and despondency <note>original: "Kleinmuth" — a lack of spirit or courage, often viewed in this period as a physical symptom of long-term illness.</note> grant him some peace, imagines himself healthy and strong; he rejoices in his recovery and leaves his sorrowful bed. "Oh," he cries out, "how I shall now enjoy the rest of my life, where no pain shall imprison me further, where the doctor's cruel hand <note>A common sentiment in 18th-century literature, reflecting the often painful and invasive medical practices of the time, such as bloodletting or cauterization.</note> no longer digs wounds into me, and no creeping fever eats the marrow from my bones. Ah! I feel quite well!"—so he says, dreaming of life's joys, and staggers to the window to enjoy the fresh air. A harsh north wind blows a stiffening frost into his limbs; he sinks back onto his bed—and wrestles with death.

So Valentour rejoiced in the plans he had designed with an enthusiastic spirit, to enjoy his life in secure tranquility; standing firm on his principles against the folly

We often can hardly comprehend the possibility of our own weakness; we cannot grasp how, with so much mental power <term>Geisteskräfte: the combined strength of one's intellect, reason, and willpower</term>, one could still succumb to this weakness. We take new courage, a new pride in our strength, and then—in the turmoil of the heart, a sudden dizziness <note>original: "gäher Schwindel." Only a few weeks passed when Valentour once again—without his own heart even appearing to know it—threw himself into the arms of opportunity. These arms tightly entwine their prey, and he escaped them only by exerting all of his strength.

**S**o easily attracted by every image that his busy imagination <term>Phantasie: in the 18th-century context, this refers to the creative power of the mind which, if left unchecked, could lead to moral wandering</term> sought to embellish with even more vibrant colors, was he not bound to be deceived? She conquered without appearing to want to; and yet, victory was her desire. Thus, shortly after she appeared at court, she became the favorite in all assemblies; the universal desire of the opposite sex.

Valentour had seen her only a few times; sat by her side in social circles, and spoke with her nowhere else but there, yet he already felt himself so irresistibly drawn to her that no other choice remained for him. Through the death of his father, he was placed in a most considerable state of fortune, and he was all the more delighted to be able to make Heloise—whose domestic circumstances were not the best—happy through his hand. He desired nothing else than to be able to lay all his treasures at her feet; and she desired nothing else than to make a wealthy marriage <note>original: "Heurath"</note> in order to be able to play the coquette <note>original: "kokettiren," meaning to flirt or manipulate social attention</note> all the more pleasantly.

If her virtue was a pretense, her love for Valentour was even more so; in the way she loved him, she loved everyone; her heart knew only sensual feelings, and otherwise it was hardened against the receptivity of purer impulses.

When Valentour, glowing with the fire of his love, forgot himself and his all-powerful resolution, and seemed to break through the barriers that <term>propriety</term> <note>original: "Wohlstand" — in this historical context, it refers not to wealth, but to the social decorum and standards of behavior expected in polite society</note> set for him, her virtue stood in his way. Every frivolous glance was punished with a serious expression; every indecent outburst of his wit was met with solemn dignity, hardly distinguishable from that of genuine virtue. What she spoke was long-deliberated; it was chosen to be fitting for the character of the man who sat by her side, and thus, without any empathy for what Valentour felt for her, she could never be shaken from her <term>track</term> <note>original: "Geleise" — literally a track or rut; used here to mean her calculated routine or composure</note>, and could never reveal her true self; in this way, she always kept him at the proper distance, as only the strictest

...the void in his heart, which Heloise left behind, was filled with a worthier object.

The Countess Deligny brought her daughter from the countryside to the capital <note>Original: "Residenz," referring to the city where the sovereign resides.</note> some time ago. She was a lovely Grace <note>A reference to the Charites or Graces of mythology, personifications of charm and beauty.</note> in the bloom of spring, as nobly educated by her wise and virtuous mother as nature had beautifully formed her. Unspoiled and innocent, lifted from the lap of nature, fortune led her into Valentour’s arms.

Amalie was the bliss of his days; the years flew by for the happy couple in the embraces of a virtuous love like joyful spring days, and time, which weakens everything, gave their love ever new vitality. Amiable children, the most beautiful imprints of their excellent originals <note>A metaphorical way of saying the children were perfect likenesses of their parents.</note>, blessed the marriage of the lovers and ennobled Valentour’s ancient lineage. Thus, from the elder Valentour, who faithfully passed the teachings of wisdom and virtue from father to son, there sprang a line of noble people who promised humanity a progeny that, removed from the common sort of men, would become a new race of humanity. [[page number: 349]]

Valentour, who owed all his happiness, his peace, and all the joys of his **manhood** <term>Mannstage: literally "man-days," referring to the prime of one's adult life</term> to the wise education provided by his father, and who recognized what would have become of him without the beneficial power of that education upon the heart, also made the upbringing of his children his primary focus. In this, he faithfully followed the instructions of his late father, who followed the path of nature at every step.

He was not ashamed to visit the nursery during his hours of leisure, and there, with indescribable fatherly joy, he embraced his dear little ones. Amalie, too, was not ashamed to be a mother and to fulfill the high duties of her station like a **sturdy citizen’s wife** <note>Original: "wackere Bürgersfrau." This reflects a common Enlightenment-era trope where the middle class (Bürger) was seen as morally superior and more "natural" than the "lazy" or "artificial" nobility.</note>, despite how much fashion and convenience strove against it. But because of this, their children were also healthier and thrived better than most offspring of the nobility, whom a hired **wet nurse** <term>Amme: a woman employed to breastfeed another's child</term> had suckled, and a wrinkled **nursery maid** <note>Original: "Mabonne," derived from the French "ma bonne," a term used for a governess or children's nurse.</note> had raised into aristocratic cripples—unhealthy weaklings, neglected in both mind and body.

Oh, with what bliss the occupation with one's children fills the empty hours

...[the delight] of the father! — What a beautiful sight it is to see a tender mother in the circle of her children, to see them play, work, pray, and learn! — Oh, how much better a mother is adorned by her occupation with her children than by games and an *assemblée*! <note>original: "Assemblée" - a French term for a formal social gathering or fashionable evening party.</note> — How much more worthy is the sight when one sees her combing, washing, and grooming her children, than when one sees her counting *marquen*, <note>original: "Marquen" - tokens or counters used for keeping score in gambling or card games.</note> or sitting idly at the window with a novel in her hand, or obsessing over the selection of some finery. <note>original: "Puzkrämerey" - a derogatory term for excessive interest in millinery, trinkets, or fashion accessories.</note>

But I beg your pardon that I speak so plainly: <note>original: "bürgerlich" - literally "middle-class" or "bourgeois." The author is using a self-deprecating tone, acknowledging that their focus on domestic labor might seem unrefined to the upper classes.</note> surely there must be a distinction.

What would be said of a Selinde <note>A common name in 18th-century literature used to personify a vain, fashionable woman of the high social classes.</note> in social circles if she were to preempt her careless nursemaid at home and wash and dress her child herself; for she cannot even dress herself without the help of her lady's maid. Her delicate nose, accustomed only to smelling the lavender and ambergris <note>original: "Umbra" - likely referring to ambergris or a similar musk-based perfume popular among the elite.</note> of the sweet little gentlemen, would not be able to endure the offensive odor of the nursery. She is too sensitive, and her ear too melodiously tuned, to [endure] the screaming and whimpering of her—

[[page number: 351]]

...[so as not] to be able to hear their children. Therefore, they are banished to the farthest corner of the house, into a dark, damp <note>original: "dämpfigtes" - referring to air that is thick, humid, or suffocating, common in poorly ventilated rooms.</note> room that has no other supply of fresh air than what gathers in the courtyard and what the nearby horse stable exudes.

Thus was Selinde herself raised, and thus Selinde in turn raises her little ones to be soulless, sickly dolls. For the sake of her children, she does not leave the **gaming table** <note>original: "Spieltisch" - Refers to the tables used for high-stakes card games and gambling, a primary social pastime for the 18th-century elite.</note> a minute earlier, nor does she sacrifice any **ball** or **promenade**; not the slightest restriction is made for the sake of these insignificant creatures. Now they happen to be here; but even that was not her intention. She only wanted to have a husband who buys her beautiful **gowns, lace, and ribbons**, who keeps servants and horses for her, and who gives her enough money for her excesses; she views the children as a most troublesome burden of the married state.

Do not believe that Selinde is the only one; there are hundreds like her, for in our day, education has been made the business of poor, lowly people who, precisely because they are found fit for no other domestic task, can be placed nowhere better than in the **nursery**. <note>original: "Kindsstube"</note> — Which...

<meta>The previous page described the neglectful mothering of "Selinde," who abandons her children to damp nurseries and poor caretakers for the sake of her social life. This page expands that individual critique into a broader, apocalyptic vision of national physical and spiritual decay resulting from such upbringing.</meta>

[[page number: 352]]

-> ————— <-

— What a terrible prospect into the distance of future ages! — Ha! I see them, the coming generations; miserable, shriveled caricatures, dwarfs in spirit and body, parched, powerless skeletons, in whose delicate bones stinking pus lies instead of marrow. — People who carry nothing more of their fathers than their names. Ha! Does one not already shudder now when entering an armory <note>original: Zeughaus — a storehouse for weapons and armor</note> and seeing the weapons of our ancestors, which our enfeebled <note>original: entnervte — literally "denerved," a common 18th-century term for physical and moral weakness</note> hands can hardly lift from their place anymore? — Do we not gaze at them as wonders of antiquity? And soon — soon the deeds of our fathers will be taken for fairy tales. — If man were to descend thus from step to step, where would there be a planet for these puppet-figures? For surely the earth cannot carry nothing but worms? — Oh, may the call of truth from the mouths of so many brave men who are currently occupied with education <note>original: Erziehung</note> penetrate the hearts of many parents who have long since forgotten what they ought to be to their children. —

### -> Story in the Old Style, <-
