## -> **Suicides.** <-

**O**n a sultry summer evening, an old woman told me, more than a hundred years ago, several peasants sat under the large linden tree of the village of L—r <note>The use of a letter followed by a dash was a common 18th-century literary convention used to suggest a real location while maintaining a sense of mystery or anonymity.</note>. They drank until the clock struck eleven, and in this hour dedicated to the spirits, they chatted quite naturally of goblins and ghosts. One among them, who had served as a farmhand in the city, played the part of Doubting Thomas <note>original: ungläubigen Thomas — a reference to the biblical Apostle Thomas; a common idiom for a skeptic who demands physical proof.</note>, mocking every little tale that the others told, and finally asserted that spirits and goblins were merely an invention of old wives. "If you have such great courage <term>Kourage: a German loanword from the French 'courage,' often used in this period to denote a brash or worldly bravery</term> then," Michel interrupted him, "go now to our churchyard and fetch the skeleton <note>original: Todtengerippe — literally 'death-skeleton'</note> that stands in the hall." "If you do this," Jakob added, "then I will [hold] you for the..."

| 3 | brave- |

<summary>This page introduces a gothic moral tale about a skeptical man who is challenged by his fellow villagers to prove his bravery by visiting a graveyard at night.</summary>
<keywords>folklore, skepticism, ghosts, churchyard, 18th-century narrative, suicide biographies</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the story from page 353, where a skeptic has been challenged by fellow peasants to fetch a skeleton from the churchyard hall to prove his bravery.</meta>

[[page number: 354]]

-> ————— <-

...hold you for the bravest fellow in the whole village, believe along with you that there are no ghosts, and give you a new Thaler <term>Thaler: A large silver coin used throughout German-speaking lands from the 15th to the 19th century.</term> as a reward besides. The skeptic began to waver and finally admitted that while he feared nothing, he did not wish to commit a sacrilege <note>The original German "freveln" implies a disrespectful or sinful act against the sanctity of the churchyard.</note>. "Give me the Thaler, I’ll fetch it!" said the innkeeper’s maid, who was just then bringing a full pitcher and had overheard the conversation. The peasants, one and all, were astonished at this boldness, and promised to pay the Thaler if she truly fulfilled the wager. Kätchen <note>A diminutive of the name Katharina, or Catherine.</note> sprang away, and in a few minutes placed the skeleton on the table before the terrified peasants. Everyone trembled, the skeptic most of all. In his fear, he promised the maid a second Thaler if she would quickly carry it away again. Käthe took the Thaler, hoisted the skeleton <margin>Kätchen</margin> back onto her shoulders, and hurried with it toward the hall. When she tried to put it back in its place, she could not get it off her back. She tried with force, but even then she did not succeed. She shook and was very near to fainting.

> **The Skeleton.** (in a dull and somber tone) 
> I will not let you go!

> **Kätchen.** Holy Mother of God! Holy Guardian Angel, stand by me!

> **The Skeleton.** (in an even more serious and terrifying voice) I will not let you go!

> **Kätchen.** Jesus Christ, have mercy on me!

> **The Skeleton.** (extremely hideous) I will not let you go until you fulfill my request!

> **Kätchen.** (trembling and quaking) What should I do?

> **The Skeleton.** Go into the church; on the right hand, you will see an open vault <note>The German "Gewölbe" refers here to a burial vault or crypt within the church architecture.</note>. Follow your path through its open door until you come to a woman dressed in black <note>The original term "Frauenzimmer" was a standard 18th and 19th-century reference to a lady or woman.</note>. Ask her for forgiveness on my behalf! Will you do it?

> **Kätchen.** I will!

The skeleton now let her go; she wanted to flee, but immediately it hung upon her neck again and threatened to strangle her. Kätchen

The next morning, I went into the village church, where I had never been before. It was buried three times, and on the third day thereafter it stood again upon this spot. Anna Katharina Reicherin redeemed it on the night of St. John the Baptist through her intercession. In the year of Christ 1627. May God be gracious and merciful to his and all Christian-believing souls. J." — The inscription uses archaic, phonetic spelling and dialect (e.g., "Tottengeripe" for Totengerippe, "Rieters" for Ritters).</note>

This inscription piqued my curiosity anew, and after church, I asked the local pastor for clarification. He had long been known to me as a sensible man who thought without prejudice; I could therefore expect an answer from him that was consistent with his principles.

# Johanna and Ubaldus.

**I**n the year 1362, Margaretha von — <note>The dash indicates the family name is being withheld to protect the privacy of descendants, as mentioned in the introduction.</note> bore her husband a daughter who was her very image and, consequently, a model of beauty—an angel in female form. At that time, ladies were not yet ashamed to nurse their children themselves, and Margaretha often sat in this lovely occupation under the great linden tree that stood near the knightly fortress. A fortune-teller or gypsy passed by one day. "Noble lady, give me a gift!" "You shall receive it richly if you reveal to me the fate of my child." The fortune-teller peered for a long time into the child’s hand and finally said: "I have already looked into many thousands of hands, but never have I seen such lines! My art becomes that of a bungler <note>original: "Stümpferinn"; meaning her skills are made to look amateurish or inadequate by the complexity of the palm's lines.</note> before them. Ubald celebrated grandly the day he heard this joyful news from the father. He visited his lady often, went hunting by her side, and was soon loved by her with all fervor, with the first surging fire of youth. Her knight returned this love with equal strength. Her nod was his law, her will his command. He heard from her at the very beginning that she especially valued a brave man, and he soon became the bravest knight in the whole wide region.

The knights of the famous League of the Lion <note>original: *Löwenbund*; a historical alliance of noblemen in the 14th century who joined together to protect their interests</note> accepted him into their society; at their first assembly, he was elected as their Captain of the League. Many abbots <note>The OCR reads "Uebte," which is almost certainly a transcription of "Aebte" (abbots), common in Fraktur script where 'A' and 'U' can look similar.</note> placed themselves under his protection and shield, and the imperial cities <term>Imperial Cities: self-governing cities in the Holy Roman Empire that were subject only to the Emperor, often in conflict with local knights</term> feared him greatly.

**G. Eberhard.** Daughter!

**Agnes.** The storm is too great! My heart is too constricted! I must pour out my suffering into the breast of my father, so that he may help me and comfort me. I am ashamed of myself; I would like to hide in a dark cave, and yet I would tell my story even to that cave, or to the trees in the forest. If only he were still free, this great, this brave knight, and I were his! — — Oh, dearest, best father! If I were his! Who would dare to lay a finger on me or you? You could peacefully enjoy the longest, greatest old age; even as an old man on crutches, you could move about, still formidable to everyone! No enemy would approach your land; all would listen for your command, gather flatteringly around your castle, and wait slavishly until it pleased you to accept their tribute <note>original: "Zins"; in this feudal context, it refers to the taxes or dues paid by subjects to their lord</note>. And if death were to rob me of you, they would all fall to their knees, paying homage to the Invincible One through me, and revere my children as their rulers! But as it is! — Whom can I choose? Whom shall I one day marry? Since everyone saw and heard that he alone is invincible. Anyone to whom I give my hand will be envied for the land, warred upon, and defeated—perhaps even defeated by him. (Conclusion follows.)

For I noticed clearly that, when his lance splintered against Great Robert’s breastplate <term>Panzer: Heavy plate armor worn by a knight</term>, he kissed a picture while steering his horse, and then, strengthened by this kiss, he sent Robert flying into the air like a bird.

**G. Eberh.** But whose picture? Could it not have been the image of a Saint, the portrait of a beloved sister, or perhaps the last legacy of his mother?

**Agnes.** Oh, if only it were so! His saint should then also be my saint, and his sister my sister! She would become my bosom friend; I would share my heart with her—if it did not belong entirely to him—and I would pray at the grave of his mother, who gave birth to such a beautiful son.
-> **G. Eberh.** <-

<summary>Count Eberhard reveals his approval of the knight Ubald, while Agnes remains convinced that Ubald loves another woman because she saw him kiss a locket during his victory in the tournament.</summary>

**C. Eberh.** I shall sound him out tomorrow!

**Agnes.** And betray me! So that he may mock me, and laugh at me in the arms of his beloved!

**C. Eberh.** Agnes, do not speak nonsense! You, mocked? Ridiculed? Ha! I feel that deeply! I would sooner plunge the sword into your heart myself before I— —! No, he must petition me for your hand, he must woo you; he must plead with you for your favor! With you, he receives a beautiful land, and this is well worth the pleading and the petitioning.

**Agnes.** But what if he does not plead, does not petition?

**C. Eberh.** Then he shall never be your husband, never my son!

**Agnes.** And if I then wither away, if I languish?

**C. Eberh.** Then the father will mourn for you, weeping tears with you in secret; but the Prince <note>original: "Fürst"</note> shall walk unmoved behind your funeral procession, and will not falter when they throw earth upon the last branch of his great heroic lineage <term>Heldenstamm: A poetic term for a noble dynasty or family tree of heroes</term>. Prepare yourself accordingly! Tomorrow I will speak with the knight!

After the Mass, many knights departed. Ubald’s horses were also bridled, but a single word from the Count held him back—him, whom otherwise a hundred warriors would not have kept for half a minute from his Johanna. Soon the Count took the opportunity to speak with Ubald alone, and soon the latter confessed to him that he would be the happiest mortal if he could only harbor the distant hope of winning Agnes. "She shall be yours," said the Count, "for you are worthy of her; you shall inherit my land with her, for your bravery deserves it. Come now <note>The original text uses 'ist', likely a transcription error or archaic variant for 'jetzt' (now).</note> to my daughter; let us hear if she thinks just as I do!"

**Agnes.** How shall I receive you? How do you come? As a father or as a lord?

**G. Eberh.** <note>Count Eberhard</note> As a lord, who demands unconditional obedience from the first of his subjects.

**Agnes.** So I am rejected! <note>The German 'ausgeschlagen' suggests being cast aside or refused, likely referring here to her own agency or perhaps a previous engagement.</note> Cast aside! The German girl feels this insult and will avenge it!

**G. Eberh.** And the princess? <term>Fürstentochter</term> <note>Literally "daughter of a prince/sovereign," emphasizing her high noble rank over her personal feelings.</note>

**Agnes.** Will—must obey! Yet she wished to learn from her father: whether he had really put it to him so plainly that he could understand it...

<summary>Count Eberhard promises his daughter Agnes and his lands to Ubald as a reward for his bravery. Agnes reacts with dramatic conflict, feeling her personal identity as a "German girl" is insulted by her father's command, even as she acknowledges her duty as a princess to obey him.</summary>

**G. Eberh.** <note>Count Eberhard</note> He shall tell you himself when he speaks with you. But now answer: Will you accept any prince, count, or knight—let him be called what he may—in short, whether he be Ubald or not, from my hand as your husband?

**Agnes.** If he avenges me against that proud man! Yes!

**G. Eberh.** Yes? Spoken so rashly, so quickly! Consider this important "Yes" well! I will not yet accept it as final, and I ask you for the second time?

**Agnes.** I have nothing to consider if I must obey unconditionally; and yet I have added a condition which the fair <note>The original German 'billige' here means 'just' or 'fair-minded' rather than the modern meaning of 'cheap'.</note> prince will surely approve.

**G. Eberh.** Well, I agree to it! Only we must hear: whether the suitor also has enough courage to venture a bout with the Lion-Knight?

**Agnes.** Very unlikely!

**G. Eberh.** That remains to be seen!

He went and entered the chamber again with Ubald. This proud, brave knight, at whose lance everyone trembled, whose sword—

everyone yielded, now stood there blushing with shame and dismayed. He stuttered, spoke a few words, and stuttered again. Agnes remained completely silent, for joy and delight choked her speech. He remained for a few more days and then also returned home, soon to return forever.

His abandoned, completely forgotten Johanna meanwhile waited longingly for his arrival. She celebrated the day of his tournament with fasting and prayer for her Ubald's well-being, and busily calculated when he might arrive again. Often she saw dust from afar, hurried forward with open arms, and always found herself deceived. Strangers, knights unknown to her, passed by. **Johanna.** (shouting after him) What did you say? Explain it to me more clearly! (grabbing the reins of the last of his **servants**) <note>The original German word is *Knecht*, which in this chivalric context refers to a mounted attendant or squire serving a knight.</note> Oh good, dear man! What did your master say? Ubald received the Count's daughter as his reward?

**Servant.** Yes, noble lady! My master never lied! I saw it myself, how Ubald flirted with **Agnes** during yesterday's **farewell banquet**, <note>original: *Abschiedsmale*</note> and I heard the Count himself say: "It will be a beautiful match!" If you are perchance waiting for him, if he promised you the prize of the tournament, then you wait in vain.

Johanna, struck with astonishment, let go of the reins and staggered home, numb with grief. Her father and mother wished to comfort her, but she accepted no consolation; she wept, lamented, and withered away like a rose gnawed by a worm. <note>This "worm in the rose" is a classic literary metaphor for a hidden sorrow or a betrayal that consumes one's health and beauty from within.</note> She often still went to the window, looked out in vain, and listened whenever she heard the trot of horses. But her **faithless one** <note>original: *Treuloser*</note> thought of her no more, and was too "polite" to trouble her with his presence. Her father saw and felt her suffering, and traveled to **Ubald’s castle** to hear from the man himself: Was his daughter such a dishonorable wench that she could be cast aside however one pleased? But the guards would not let him—

...offender's sword. Ubald lamented greatly. "I wished," he said, "that Johanna would forget me, but not that she should curse me!" And yet he left the corpse in the hands of the two servants and, spattered with the old man's blood, set off for his wedding.

Johanna, just like her father, had heard the news of the wedding of her unfaithful lover <note>original: *Ungetreuen*</note> and, just like him, intended to wait for him along the road. Her goal was not to fight him, but to return all his vows and gifts, and to beg him to take from her a life that was once a delight <note>original: *Wonne*</note> but was now a torment <note>original: *Qual*</note> to her. Should he be so ungenerous as to deny her request, she intended at least to see him one more time, and then to mourn her life away forever in a convent.

Her mother’s watchful eye prevented her from departing at the intended time; she did not throw herself onto her horse until midnight. As the sun began to shine upon her, she encountered the corpse of her father, which his servants had laid upon his steed while they rode mournfully alongside. When she recognized him, she collapsed from her horse, and the two faithful servants now had two "dead" <note>In the romantic literature of this era, a profound swoon or collapse from grief was often described with the finality of death, even if the character later recovered.</note> to lead. Near the castle, Johanna recovered, only to see her poor mother in the same state of distress. Benno's body was finally buried. The tears of all his subjects <term>Unterthanen: in the feudal system, those who lived under the protection and rule of a lord</term>, the silent despair of his wife and child, gave loud testimony that he had been a gentle lord, a good husband, and a father.

After nine days, the desolate Johanna learned from a squire the name of the father-murderer <note>original: *Vatermörder*</note>, which until now had been carefully hidden from her. On that very day, she took a horse and fled from her mother once more. Many knights traveling to Ubald's wedding encountered her on the road to the Count's castle; they marveled to see a noble, mourning lady traveling so alone, honored her grief—though they did not know its cause—and took her under their protection to the castle. Many people of high rank, for people in those days were still very hospitable <note>original: *gastfrey*</note>, offered her a place to stay; but she declined this offer and moved into the hut of a poor woman, whose services she rewarded generously.

Ubald's wedding festival arrived. He and Agnes swam in bliss, in the feeling of the near...

approaching bliss. They processed to the church in the greatest pomp and glory. Three hundred knights, sixty-five noble ladies, and seven hundred servants accompanied them on horseback. The procession passed by Johanna's wretched dwelling, and she followed the joyful train alone in her mourning dress.

The betrothed had already stepped to the altar, and the priest had already begun the wedding ceremony, when Johanna drew her father's sharpest sword from beneath her dress and pushed through the assembled crowd toward Ubald. "Murderer of my father, oath-breaking coward <note>The original term "mortbrüchige Memme" implies a coward who has violated a legal peace or a solemn oath after a crime.</note>, die!" In that moment, she thrust the sword at him, but Ubald turned, and the blow passed under his arm without harming him. Overcome with astonishment and terrified by the immediate danger, he stumbled a few steps forward, and Johanna believed she had actually struck him. "God forgive you, I cannot!" she cried out, then quickly turned the point of the sword toward herself and ran against the altar. The sword pierced through her heart, and she sank down dead.

A murmur and noise now followed the deathly silence that had reigned until then. The church was

desecrated by murder. The priest fled, and all the people rushed after him. The Count <note>Agnese's father, who was giving her away in marriage.</note> tore his daughter from Ubald's side, and Ubald stood lonely and alone, perhaps without seeing or feeling what was happening. He had been indelibly branded with shame by a dead, noble lady, and according to the laws of knighthood, he could no longer wash it away. Two hours later, he was seen staggering out of the church, surrounded by a few of his loyal followers. After that time, he was seen no more. Johanna’s corpse was sent to her mother, and Agnese—for whom the disgraced Ubald was lost forever—took the veil <note>To "take the veil" (original: "nahm den Schleyer an") means to enter a convent and become a nun.</note> and died, according to the chronicler's testimony, as an abbess in the odor of sanctity <note>original: "im Geruche der Heiligkeit"; a traditional Catholic expression suggesting a person was so holy that their body or memory possessed a miraculous spiritual fragrance.</note>.

Poor, deeply aggrieved Margaretha sent messengers to every corner in search of her lost daughter, but all returned without comforting news, for they did not look for Johanna among the knights with whom she had traveled. On the day of her daughter’s death, she turned wearily on her bed and fell asleep at midnight, exhausted by many tears. Her daughter appeared to her in a dream. Blood welled from her left breast.

"Mother," she said slowly and sadly,
"I am dead; I have taken my own life. <note>original: "selbst entleibt" — literally "disembodied myself," a common historical idiom for suicide.</note> Soon
they will bring you my body; bury

Pray for me, for I am miserable!
The Righteous One <note>Refers to God as the ultimate judge.</note> pronounced a severe judgment

In the burial vault, whose bolts you
shall not open again after my burial—
indeed, you will *not be able* to open them—

"Never?" cried Margaretha in her dream,
"That would be terrible!" "As good as never,"
answered the ghost. "Ubald, after some

I shall languish and read <note>The ghost mentions "lesen" (reading), likely referring to a common folkloric motif where the dead must read a book of sins or prayers until their time is served.</note> for so long, and he shall
stand and wait for just as long, until a child
not yet born pleads with me on his behalf.
Will this, can this ever happen? And yet, hope
still glimmers in my breast! Oh, pray for me!"

# Conclusion of
# Johanna and Ubaldus.

**S**he disappeared; Margaretha awoke and, on the third day, saw her dream fulfilled with the greatest sorrow. They brought her the body of her child, which she had buried in the burial vault; she ordered the door to be closed forever and arranged for a Mass to be read every day in the church. The historian Father <note>The "F." stands for "Frater" or "Father."</note> Augustinus Bornerius or Bonorius, a Discalced <note>original: "barfüßer" — literally "barefoot," referring to an order of monks, such as the Franciscans, who wore sandals as a sign of poverty and devotion.</note> monk, asserts here that he himself read this foundation mass <note>A Mass established by a donation to be performed regularly for the soul of the deceased.</note> for fifteen years and had clearly noticed that at the beginning of each Mass, the vault door would open, and at the *Go, the mass is ended!* <note>original: "Ite, Missa est!" — the formal Latin dismissal at the conclusion of a Catholic Mass.</note> it would close again of its own accord. He and many other priests saw the miracle, but the laity did not.

After ten years, some foreign mounted soldiers <term>Reissige</term> <note>A historical term for armed riders or men-at-arms, often serving as mercenaries or a noble's escort.</note> arrived at the castle and asked for Margaretha. "Noble lady," they said, "you can surely tell us best: where is your daughter's grave? We have commissions there—"

Now, says the historian, whenever the vault opened during the Mass, the skeleton also turned and looked mournfully inside.

Every one of my readers can and may likewise consider this story to be a <note>original: "Mährchen," meaning a fairy tale or legend</note>. I separate the prophecy and the dream from it, and then I do not see why we could not accept it as having actually happened? It is, to be sure, written in the tone of a legend by a monk, but his additions are clearly visible; and when one considers that the story was preserved in a family archive, one finds even more reason [to believe it],

**O**n the banks of the Danube, not far from a small village, various ruins of old masonry still protruded from a hill. Legend had it that a <term>robber-baron's castle</term> <note>original: "Raubschloß," a castle used as a base by lawless knights to plunder travelers</note> was once in this area, and old grandmothers saw lights wandering about the rubble of the old <term>fortress</term> <note>original: "Veste"</note> on holy nights. A large, headless white horse <note>original: "Schimmel"</note> is also said to have once accompanied a stranger from this area to the neighboring village, and suddenly [disappeared] at the inn—

No one in this entire region knew as much reliable information to provide regarding this castle as an old shepherd, who still knew how to tell various reports passed down from his great-grandfather. It was once on a holy Christmas Eve, when several peasants came for a social gathering <note>original: "Heimgarten"—a traditional rural social meeting of neighbors for storytelling and crafts during winter evenings.</note> in neighbor Hans’s house, and chatted the evening away with tales until it was time for Midnight Mass. <note>original: "Mette"—the Catholic liturgy celebrated late at night on Christmas Eve.</note> Valentin, for that was the shepherd's name, was also there, and he began to tell his story when his turn came: "Look, neighbors! I still know it as well as if it were today. I was still just a boy, sitting on my father's lap, and listening to the wondrous things he told of old castles. Then he began—and made the sign of the Holy Cross, praying that God might preserve all of us who are here from evil spirits—but it was certainly true that such a poltergeist <term>Poltergeist: from the German "poltern" (to rumble/make noise) and "Geist" (spirit); a type of ghost known for physical disturbances.</term> ruled in our castle; what do you say?" the peasants replied, "a poltergeist is supposed to be in our castle? At midnight, the officer heard a terrifying rattling; he looked down into the chapel and found that it was illuminated throughout. The large tombstone that was against the wall opened of its own accord, and a man armored from head to foot <note>The "geharnischter Mann" or man in armor is a classic figure in German ghost stories, often representing a restless ancestor or a knight bound by an oath.</note> stepped out of the grave. ### -> Henry and Edward. <-

**I**t was an old Count named Henry, who was a rough man by nature. According to the customs of those times, he could throw his opponent to the ground, drink several measures <term>Maaß: a traditional Germanic unit of liquid volume, often roughly equivalent to a liter</term> of wine in one draught, and ride a horse to death.

[[page number: 393]]

-> ————— <-

ride. In the entire neighborhood, the nobles and knights feared him, and he was called the Wild One.

When he cursed, it was as if a storm wind <note>original: 'Sturmwind'</note> roared through the thickets, and when he stomped, the earth trembled beneath his foot. His gaze was like a consuming fire, and his eye was as terrible as the full moon rising on a foggy night. His hair was stiff like the clipped manes of horses, and his beard curled upward like the whiskers of a tiger’s snout <note>original: 'Tygerschnauze'</note>.

Even while he was still a boy, his wildness already betrayed itself, for there was no living animal in the castle <note>original: 'Schloße'</note> that he did not strangle.

Murder was his pleasure, and killing was his delight. For entertainment, he fought with bulls or tracked down wild boars <note>original: 'Schwein-Beber'—likely 'Schweineber', a male wild boar</note> in the forest and beat them to death with his fists. His favorite creatures were the pigs; he had ten of them with him at his castle <note>original: 'Burg'</note>, and they never left his side, neither at the dinner table <note>original: 'Tafel'</note> nor in bed.

He once threw a servant out of a window for accidentally harming one of these animals <note>original: "Thiere" — referring to the pigs mentioned on the previous page.</note>, and for another who failed to prepare their food to his satisfaction, he brutally stomped the man's intestines out.

For breakfast, he drank warm horse blood; or, if he had an appetite for food in the morning, he would sink his teeth into the nearest living animal, tearing a chunk of flesh directly from its body and chewing until his doublet <note>original: "Wamst" — a close-fitting jacket worn by men between the 15th and 17th centuries.</note> was stained red with blood and gore.

He was twenty-five years old when his father died and Heinrich became the lord of the inherited estate. —

At that time, he wished to marry, but young women far and wide fled from him, and every father locked up his daughter whenever word spread that Heinrich was out on the hunt.

In that region lived a beautiful young lady named Hedwig; she was the only daughter of a noble knight. Her nature <note>original: "Bildung" — here referring to her character and mannerisms rather than just formal education.</note> was as gentle as moonlight on a spring night, and her heart was pure,

Edward, a noble youth, loved the beautiful girl, and Hedwig loved the youth. The wedding days had already been set. The bride was already hastening at her father's side toward the castle of her beloved when Heinrich encountered them on the way, immediately strangled the old father, and abducted the young lady. She still lay unconscious in the arms of the savage <note>original: "des Wilden" — referring to Heinrich, whose nickname "Wild Heinrich" reflects his animalistic cruelty.</note> when she was brought into Heinrich’s fortress <note>original: "Veste" — an archaic German term for a stronghold or fortified castle.</note>. —

In vain, Heinrich tried everything to bring the girl, whose senses were numbed, back to life. Pale as a corpse, like a narcissus <note>A flower often associated in folklore and mythology with youth, beauty, and sometimes death.</note> that has fallen under the scythe, she lay stretched out on the daybed in the early slumber of death. —

Wild Heinrich gazed at her in astonishment, and for the first time, his heart stirred with a human feeling; it was as if a tear wanted to wet his eye; yet his savagery dried up the drop before the emotion could form it into a tear.

Because Eduart was by Hedwig’s corpse in total confidence <note>meaning he was unsuspecting and vulnerable</note>, the malicious servant went and betrayed everything to the evil Heinrich. Heinrich reached furiously for his sword and hurried into the vault; but hardly had Eduart spotted him when revenge and rage glowed in every nerve. Sparks of fire instead of blood rolled in every vein; he fell upon him like a lion, and with a single thrust, struck the monster to the ground, so that his soul breathed its last into hell.

Eduart’s servants, who had approached the fortress <note>original: "Veste"</note> during this time, heard the commotion, forced their way through the trapdoor—which Heinrich’s servant had forgotten to lock—into the castle, and massacred everyone.

Eduart was already the victor when he once again approached his beloved, who gradually awoke from her deathly slumber. Hedwig was not dead; only a fainting spell <term>Ohnmacht; a state of deep unconsciousness or a swoon</term> had fettered the powers of her spirit within her suffering body.

Who

This story was told often,
and through telling and retelling,
the truth lost itself in a fairy tale <note>original: "Mährchen" (modern: Märchen). The author suggests that while the events were real, they have since passed into the realm of local folklore and myth</note>.

### 26.
## For the New Year
-> to <-
# my Prince.

**Y**ou, whom the Divinity <note>original: "Gottheit"; here referring to the Divine or God as the source of the ruler's authority</note> gave to us as a father,
Prince! see the thousands of children
Whom Providence <note>original: "Vorsicht"; in this 18th/19th-century context, it refers to Divine Providence or the guiding hand of God</note> entrusted to You,
See how their hearts, moved by Your
goodness,
Beat warmly in return to Yours.
The gentle fire that flows through the veins,
That gives life to every heart, this fire
Is gentle filial love <note>original: "Kindesliebe"; literally "child's love," used here to describe the paternalistic relationship between a ruler and his people</note>.
From the beggar who rests in the cottage,
To the man whom birth exalts,
Who dwells in palaces, love reigns,
Yes, love for him who is our father.
Ah! let us, best of Princes! say it often to You,
That everything that animates us,
That everything that gives us being,
That our existence is Your own.
Love has always chained us to princes,
See the altar, upon which is scattered
Cc <note>A signature mark or catchword used by printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order</note> From

--- Page 411 ---
<meta>Page 402. This page continues the formal New Year's poem dedicated to a Prince, which began on page 401. The tone remains one of high praise and total devotion, typical of courtly literature of the period.</meta>

[[page number: 402]]

-> ————— <-

From our fathers’ hands
Still incense rises—
Rises up to the Godhead
For the welfare of the Sovereign.
O see us too, bowed to the earth,
As our gaze presses through the clouds
To implore divine bliss <note>The original German "Götterseligkeit" implies a state of heavenly or god-like happiness.</note> for You—
To implore blessings upon Your head.
A glance from You is like the rays of the sun,
Which gently warm every flower,
From the violet that stands in the valley
To the lily that shines upon the mountains.
O be to us always what You truly are,
And may the cloud of misfortune never
Withdraw Your protection from us;
For as You are the soul of us all,
So is Your existence and Your life
Our own.
Take our property, take all that we
possess;
It is surely Yours, for we are Your people;
Only do not take Yourself and Your love away from us.

-> ————— <-

### For the New Year
# To the noble ladies of our city
### who are wives and mothers.

You! who like the roses in spring
Bloom for the eye of the gardener
You! the noblest of women
So you bloom — for your husbands.
Charming and beautiful are your features
More charming than the features of Venus <note>The Roman goddess of love and beauty.</note>
For Minerva’s <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom; her mention suggests the women are valued for their intellect as much as their beauty.</note> spirit elevates your cultivation <note>original: "Bildung," referring here to the refinement of character, education, and moral upbringing.</note>.
Spirit and feeling speak
From the soulful eye
Audible to him who understands the language of the soul <note>original: "Seelensprache," a Romantic-era concept suggesting a deep, wordless connection between kindred spirits.</note>
In the explanatory glance.
The brush of the best of artists
Does not depict the touching scene
When your children, attentive to your lessons,
Hang upon your arms.
From a distance the husband smiles, and feels
Fully the joy of being a father.
Virtue’s harmonious voice
Sounds with gentleness from your lips
Into the delicate ear of the listening boy

### To
# Mr. Le Brun.

-> ————— <-

Orpheus <note>In Greek mythology, Orpheus was the legendary musician whose music could charm all living things and even stones.</note> falls silent when Le Brun <note>Ludwig August Lebrun (1752–1790) was a celebrated oboist and composer from Mannheim, famous across Europe for his expressive playing.</note> plays
The sound departs from Apollo’s lyre <note>Apollo was the Greek god of music; his lyre represented the pinnacle of divine musical artistry.</note>
And soul-bliss <term>Seelenwonne <note>original: "Seelenwonne"; a compound word describing a deep, spiritual joy or rapture of the soul.</note></term> lulls
Humanity to rest,
In a foretaste of the most blessed feelings,
Which once only the gods enjoyed,
Before Le Brun existed.
The nightingale forgets its sweet notes
And listens; the shepherd falls silent and sinks
In a frenzy of feeling onto the breast of his be-
loved,
Passion knows no further expression
When Le Brun plays.
Sensation, bliss, and delight—
The child of the Godhead — Harmony <term>Harmonie <note>original: "Harmonie"; in this 18th-century context, it refers to the divine order of music that connects the human spirit to the celestial.</note></term>
Enlivens — breathes soul into the listener.
When Le Brun falls silent—ah! how vexingly
One awakes from the enjoyment of divine bliss <term>Götterwonne <note>original: "Götterwonne"; literally "the joy of the gods," a state of ecstasy triggered by the music.</note></term>
Back into human life
Into the life of poor mortals.
The tone with which the Godhead speaks
Into <note>This word "Ins" (Into) serves as a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

## To
# Madame Le Brun.

When Le Brun was still a girl
She once listened in the bushes
To the noblest of nightingales,
Apollo’s <note>The Greek and Roman god of music, poetry, and the sun.</note> finest, which sang.
Enchanted by its magical voice
Le Brun sank down by the wild rosebush.
The God of Tones saw this and gave
The most beautiful of Philomelas <term>Philomela: A figure from Greek mythology who was transformed into a nightingale. In poetry, the name is often used as a synonym for the bird itself.</term>
To the good girl to be raised.
But robbed of the freedom of its wings,
Locked in a cage, Philomela soon died.
The God pitied her, and Jupiter <note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology.</note>
In order to comfort him,
Has since that time placed the nightingale's tones
Into Le Brun’s song.

-> ————— <-

## For the New Year
# to my wife. <note>original: "Weib." In the 18th and early 19th centuries, this was the standard, respectful term for a wife, though it evolved to mean "woman" or "female" in modern German.</note>

If you love me not, the loveliest spring
The loveliest region charms me not,
And sadly then do the waterfalls murmur,
The sun itself loses its light.
In vain do the ripe fields beckon to me,
In vain the most pleasant meadow laughs,
The grove is hateful to me, I flee the for-
-> ests <-
I flee myself and nature.
If you love me, the desert is beautiful to me,
And every harsh morning is bright;
The North <note>The North Wind, traditionally associated with biting cold and hardship.</note> will blow gently around my temples,
The harsh winter smells of herbs.
Pleasure is sent from the snow-covered expanse
To me from the withered treetops,
Frozen streams rustle for me,
And in the storm-wind I find rest.

-> ————— <-

-> To <-

# To All Humanity. <note>original: "An alle Menschen." While "Menschen" means "people," the context here is a universalist address to the human race.</note>

To all of you, whom the Deity gave souls,
And hearts for feeling,
To all of you, whoever you are, from the noble beggar
Even to the king — to all of you I wish
The most blessed enjoyment of life.
For God commanded us to live, and to live, that
-> means to feel <-
And to feel means to be a sentient being. <note>original: "fühlbares Wesen." In 18th-century philosophy, this refers to a "sensitive" being capable of moral and emotional perception.</note>
Live, all of you — faithful to virtue, because har-
-> mony <-
Of creation is virtue.
Live, but not a sluggish plant-like life;
Live a human life, <note>The author contrasts "Pflanzenleben" (an existence of mere biological survival) with "Menschenleben" (a life of reason, emotion, and moral purpose).</note> of the noble spark
-> worthy <-
Which from the Deity’s essence
Flowed into our hearts,
And does not perish, even if the shell <note>original: "Hülle." This refers to the physical body as a temporary vessel for the immortal soul.</note>
Into which the Deity breathed it
Sits at the oar of the galleys. <note>A reference to galley slaves; the author argues that the divine spark of human dignity cannot be extinguished even by the most brutal forms of slavery or hardship.</note>
Be humans! and if you feel the dignity of hu-
-> manity <-
Then let your eye look up to heaven,
Where no fleeting change of times
[measures] Life with the number of days
-> In <- <note>This word "In" is a catchword, placed at the bottom of the page to indicate the first word of the following page.</note>

Whips into the wide space of antiquity.
Look up to heaven, and learn the dignity
-> of humanity <-
In the land of souls.
Blessings be to him who is human,
In whichever part of the world he may dwell;
Be he a Moor, a White, a Creole,
A Jew, a Turk, a Heathen, —
<note>original: "Mohr, ein Weisser, ein Creol, Ein Jud, ein Türk, ein Haid." These terms reflect 18th-century categorizations of race and religion. "Moor" was used for Black people, "Turk" was a common synonym for Muslims, and "Heathen" (Haid/Heide) referred to those outside the Abrahamic faiths.</note>
Blessings to him, if he is but human —
The Godhead’s child — my brother.
